# Nursery 2 — First Term

*Pupil's book · every subject offered to this class, every teaching week of the term.*

**11 subjects · 105 lessons · 13 sections.** Weeks 7, 10, 11 and 12 are Mid-Term Break, Revision, Examination and Closing: no lesson is written for a week the school keeps for those, except where the school's own table prints a teaching row there.

Each lesson ends with a worksheet and a piece of my own work; each subject ends with the term practice paper — 30 objective, 10 sub-objective and 5 theory questions. The contents page lists every part and every lesson of this book.

# Basic Science

Weeks 1 to 6, 8 and 9. Week 7 is Mid-Term Break, Week 10 Revision, Week 11 Examination,
Week 12 Closing — those weeks carry no note.
*Depth: NERDC 2025 · Nursery 3 · Basic Science and Technology · First Term, pp 491–497.*


### WEEK 1 — OUR BODY

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · Basic Science and Technology · First Term W1 (pp 491–494).*

**You will learn to**

• name the five big parts of my body
• point to the parts of my head, my trunk and my limbs
• say what my body parts do
• know that my body is special and belongs only to me

**Things to know**

• My body is everything about me — from the top of my head to the tips of my toes.
• The five big parts of the body are the **head, neck, trunk, arms and legs**.
• The head is on top and it is round. Inside the head is the brain.
• The neck joins the head to the body. The trunk is the middle part.
• Parts of the head: **eyes, nose, ears, mouth, hair**.
• Parts of the trunk: **chest, stomach, back, waist**.
• Parts of the limbs: **shoulders, elbows, wrists, hands, fingers, hips, knees, ankles, feet, toes**.
• I have ten fingers and ten toes.
• Eyes see, ears hear, nose smells, mouth eats and talks, legs walk, hands hold.
• Nobody else has a face exactly like mine. My body is special.
• I use my body to see, hear, run, jump, eat, hold and play.

**Words for my notebook**

I have a body. My body has a head, neck, trunk, arms and legs. My body is special.

**Let us talk**

1. Point to your neck.
2. Name the parts on your head.
3. What do we use to hear?
4. Touch your elbow, then your ankle.
5. Say one thing only you do with your hands.


![A girl standing head to feet, showing her whole body.](assets/img/nursery-2/1st-term/basic-science/week1-my-whole-body.png)
**Worksheet**

1. Which of these is NOT a part of the head? A) hair B) elbow C) nose D) ear
2. The part of the body between the head and the trunk is the A) leg B) waist C) neck D) finger
3. Which one is on the trunk? A) wrist B) knee C) ankle D) chest
4. We use our ____ to smell. A) nose B) ears C) hands D) feet
5. Fill in: I have ____ fingers on both hands.
6. Name two things your body helps you to do every day.
7. Write two sentences about the parts of your body that you can count.

**My own work**

Draw your own body in your notebook and label five parts you know.

---

### WEEK 2 — CARE FOR OUR BODY

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · Basic Science and Technology · First Term W2 (pp 491–494).*

**You will learn to**

• say why I must care for my body
• tell the daily body-care practices in order
• wash my hands and brush my teeth the right way
• say how much sleep and rest my body needs

**Things to know**

• A body that is looked after stays strong, does not itch, does not smell and does not fall sick often.
• I bathe every day with soap and water.
• I brush my teeth **twice a day** — in the morning and at night.
• I wash my hands before eating and after using the toilet.
• Hair: I wash it, I comb it and I keep it neat.
• Nails: I keep them short and clean, because long nails hide dirt and scratch the skin.
• I wear clean clothes every day; the dirty ones go to the basin, not back on my body.
• I eat good food — rice, beans, yam, vegetables and fruit — and I drink clean water.
• I sleep early and rest well, so that my body can grow.
• Running and playing make my body strong.
• Once in a while a health worker checks my teeth, my eyes and how tall I have grown.

**Words for my notebook**

I bathe daily. I brush twice a day. I wash my hands. I keep my nails short and my clothes clean.

**Let us talk**

1. How many times a day do we brush our teeth?
2. Show me how you wash your hands.
3. Name two things we do to the hair.
4. Why must nails be kept short?
5. What do we do when we are very tired?


![A girl brushing her teeth beside a washstand with soap and a towel.](assets/img/nursery-2/1st-term/basic-science/week2-brushing-my-teeth.png)
**Worksheet**

1. We brush our teeth ____ a day. A) four times B) twice C) once D) three times
2. Which one keeps our nails clean and safe? A) a cap B) a comb C) a cup D) a nail cutter
3. Before eating we must A) wash our hands B) sing C) run D) sleep
4. Dirty clothes should be A) put in the basin B) worn again C) given away D) kept under the bed
5. Fill in: We drink ____ water so that we do not fall sick.
6. Mention two things you do every morning to keep your body clean.
7. Write two sentences on how you keep your body strong.

**My own work**

Colour the picture of the clean child and cross out the one who is dirty.

---

### WEEK 3 — FIRSTAID: FIRSTAID BOX CONTENTS

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · Basic Science and Technology · First Term W3 (pp 491–494).*

**You will learn to**

• say what first aid is in one line
• name six things inside a first aid box
• point to where the box is kept
• say who may use the things in the box

**Things to know**

• First aid is the **first help** we give to a person who is hurt or suddenly sick, before the nurse or the doctor comes.
• The things in the box are kept in a box so that help is ready when it is needed.
• Contents of the first aid box: **plasters (band-aids), bandages, cotton wool, antiseptic liquid or cream, scissors, thermometer, safety pins, gloves**.
• Other things that may be inside: pain relief medicine, gauze, tape and tweezers.
• A plaster covers a small cut. A bandage wraps a wound. Cotton wool cleans a wound.
• The box is kept clean, in one fixed place, and it is always filled up.
• Only grown-ups use the things in the box. We do not take the scissors, we do not open the medicine and we never drink the antiseptic.

**Words for my notebook**

First aid is the first help for a hurt person. The box has plasters, bandages, cotton wool and a thermometer.

**Let us talk**

1. What is first aid?
2. Mention four things in the box.
3. Where is the box kept in our school?
4. Who is allowed to use it?


![An open first aid box with a bandage, a bottle, cotton wool, scissors, a plaster and a thermometer.](assets/img/nursery-2/1st-term/basic-science/week3-inside-the-first-aid-box.png)
**Worksheet**

1. First aid means the ____ help given to a hurt person. A) slow B) secret C) last D) first
2. Which of these covers a small cut? A) a comb B) a shoe C) a plaster D) a spoon
3. Which one is NOT kept in the first aid box? A) chewing stick B) cotton wool C) bandage D) plaster
4. Who should use the things in the first aid box? A) a baby B) grown-ups C) a friend D) any pupil
5. Fill in: We keep the first aid box clean and always ____.
6. Name three things you can find inside a first aid box.
7. Write two sentences on what first aid means.

**My own work**

Draw the two things in the box that you have seen at home.

---

### WEEK 4 — FIRSTAID: USES

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · Basic Science and Technology · First Term W4 (pp 491–494).*

**You will learn to**

• match each first aid thing to what it is used for
• say when a grown-up must be called at once
• say what a thermometer is for
• stay calm when somebody is hurt

**Things to know**

• Plasters and band-aids cover **small cuts** and scrapes.
• A bandage wraps a wound and **supports a sprained** wrist or leg.
• Cotton wool cleans a wound and carries the medicine to it.
• Antiseptic washes the wound so that **germs do not enter**; it may sting once.
• A thermometer checks whether the body is hot with fever.
• Call a grown-up at once for a deep cut, blood that will not stop, a big burn, an animal bite, or a nail or thorn gone deep into the skin.
• We never try to be the doctor for a big wound; we go and call for help.
• When somebody is hurt and we shout, everybody becomes frightened. We breathe, we speak well, and we say: *Are you okay? I am calling a teacher.*

**Words for my notebook**

Plaster for a small cut. Bandage to wrap. Cotton to clean. Thermometer for fever. Call a grown-up.

**Let us talk**

1. What do we use for a small cut?
2. What is the thermometer for?
3. Mention two injuries that need a grown-up at once.
4. Show me the calm voice you use when a friend is hurt.


![A teddy bear with a bandaged arm and a plaster on its knee beside a first aid box.](assets/img/nursery-2/1st-term/basic-science/week4-the-bandaged-teddy.png)
**Worksheet**

1. A bandage is used to A) brush the teeth B) write on the book C) wrap a wound and support a sprain D) cook food
2. A thermometer tells us whether the body has A) dirt B) fever C) a cut D) a bruise
3. Antiseptic is put on a wound to A) make it bleed B) keep germs out C) colour it D) stop it feeling
4. Which one must make us call a grown-up at once? A) a torn book B) a small scrape C) a deep cut that will not stop bleeding D) a dirty shoe
5. Fill in: When somebody is hurt we must keep ____ and call a grown-up.
6. Name the thing used for (a) a small cut (b) checking fever.
7. Write two sentences on how you will help a friend who has fallen.

**My own work**

Match with a line: plaster–cut, bandage–wrap, cotton–clean, thermometer–fever.

---

### WEEK 5 — BODY CARE FOR WOUNDS: MEANING, CAUSES AND TYPES

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · Basic Science and Technology · First Term W7 (pp 491–494).*

**You will learn to**

• say what a wound is
• name the causes of wounds at home and at school
• tell the five types of wounds apart
• say two ways of preventing wounds

**Things to know**

• A wound is an injury that **breaks the skin** — where the skin is open, or cut, or has blood on it.
• If the skin is not broken, it is not a wound.
• Cuts come from sharp things: a knife, broken glass, a razor blade, a tin lid.
• Falls and scrapes come from running in the compound or falling from a chair.
• Burns come from a hot pot, a kettle, a lantern or a candle.
• Insect bites (mosquito, bee, ant) and animal scratches (cat, dog) also make wounds.
• A **cut** is a straight opening made by something sharp.
• A **scrape (graze)** is the top skin rubbed off after a fall.
• A **bruise** is a blue-black mark from bumping; the skin is not open.
• A **burn** is made by heat or fire. A **puncture** is a small deep hole from a nail or a thorn.
• We do not play with knives, blades or broken glass, and we keep away from the pot on the fire.
• Some wounds are small and we clean them; some are big and only a doctor or nurse can treat them.

**Words for my notebook**

A wound breaks the skin. Types: cut, scrape, bruise, burn, puncture. Small wounds are cleaned; big wounds need a doctor.

**Let us talk**

1. What is a wound?
2. Name two things in the house that can cut.
3. Show me the picture of a burn.
4. What kind of wound comes from a thorn?
5. Mention one way of preventing wounds.


![A knife, a needle, a broken cup and a hot pot that can cause a wound.](assets/img/nursery-2/1st-term/basic-science/week5-things-that-can-cut-or-burn.png)
**Worksheet**

1. A wound is an injury that A) paints the skin B) breaks the skin C) cleans the skin D) warms the skin
2. A blue-black mark from bumping, with the skin not open, is a A) cut B) bruise C) plaster D) burn
3. A puncture wound can come from A) soap B) a bandage C) a thorn or a nail D) a plaster
4. Which one can cause a burn? A) a hot pot from the fire B) a toy C) a book D) cold water
5. Fill in: We do not play with knives, glass or ____ blades.
6. Mention three things that cause wounds at home.
7. Write two sentences on the difference between a bruise and a cut.

**My own work**

Draw one thing that can cause a cut and one thing that can cause a burn.

---

### WEEK 6 — BODY CARE FOR WOUNDS: CARE FOR WOUNDS

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · Basic Science and Technology · First Term W8 (pp 491–494).*

**You will learn to**

• put the four steps for a small wound in order
• say when a grown-up must be called
• keep the rules about the scab and the bandage
• know the signs that a wound is going bad

**Things to know**

• Step one: **wash the hands first**.
• Step two: **clean the wound with clean water**.
• Step three: **put on the antiseptic** with cotton wool — we dab it, we do not rub it.
• Step four: **cover the wound with a plaster or a bandage**.
• Never touch a wound with dirty hands.
• Do not pick at the scab. The scab is the body's own plaster; when it is picked, the wound opens again and can leave a mark.
• A wet or dirty bandage is not a bandage — it is changed.
• If the wound becomes red, or swells, or pus comes out, or it pains more after two days, that is infection: tell a grown-up.
• A wound heals in some days if we keep it clean and let the scab finish its work.
• Prevention is better than treatment: it is better not to be cut at all.

**Words for my notebook**

Wash hands, clean with water, put on antiseptic, cover. Do not pick the scab. Red or swollen = tell a grown-up.

**Let us talk**

1. What is the first thing we do for a small wound?
2. Show me step two and step three.
3. Why must we not pick a scab?
4. Mention one sign that a wound is going bad.
5. What does "prevention is better than treatment" mean?


![A girl putting a plaster on her knee.](assets/img/nursery-2/1st-term/basic-science/week6-a-plaster-on-my-knee.png)
**Worksheet**

1. What is the FIRST thing to do before caring for a wound? A) wash our hands B) put on a plaster C) blow on it D) run to the doctor
2. We clean a small wound with A) dirty water B) pepper C) mud D) clean water
3. On a healing wound there is a scab; we must A) leave it to fall off itself B) pick it daily C) rub sand on it D) cut it
4. Which one says the wound is going bad? A) it is dry B) it itches a little C) it is red and swollen D) its colour is the same
5. Fill in: When a bandage gets wet or dirty, we ____ it.
6. Write the four steps for caring for a small wound in order.
7. Write two sentences on why we must not pick a scab.

**My own work**

Number 1 to 4 the pictures of the four steps in your notebook.

---

### WEEK 8 — SICKNESS: DEFINITION, TYPES AND SYMPTOMS, CAUSES AND CARE

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · Basic Science and Technology · First Term W9 and W10 (pp 491–494) — this
week takes both NERDC weeks together.*

**You will learn to**

• define sickness in one line
• name common sicknesses of children and the signs they show
• say what brings sickness and how to prevent it
• describe how a sick person is cared for

**Things to know**

• Sickness (illness) is when the body is **not working properly** and we feel unwell.
• A well body eats and plays; a sick body is weak and needs rest.
• Common sicknesses of children: **cold and cough, fever, stomach ache, headache, malaria, diarrhoea, measles, chicken pox**.
• The signs of sickness are **fever or high temperature, pain, coughing, sneezing, vomiting, weakness, loss of appetite and rashes**.
• Signs are how the body tells us that something is wrong.
• A thermometer is used to check whether the body has fever.
• Sickness is brought by germs (bacteria and viruses), dirty water and dirty food, the bite of an infected mosquito, not eating well, not sleeping enough and very cold weather.
• We prevent sickness by washing the hands, eating healthy food, drinking clean water, covering the mouth when we cough, and sleeping under a mosquito net.
• A sick person needs rest and sleep, the medicine the doctor gave at the time given, plenty of water, good food and warmth.
• When somebody is sick we go to the doctor or the clinic; we do not take medicine alone.
• We are kind to a sick person — we do not laugh at them, we speak softly, we take them a cup of water.
• Sickness is not for ever: with care we get better.

**Words for my notebook**

Sickness is the body not working well. Rest, water, good food and the doctor's medicine make us well again.

**Let us talk**

1. What is sickness?
2. Name three signs of sickness.
3. What does a thermometer check?
4. Mention two things that bring sickness.
5. What do you do for a brother or sister who is sick?


![A girl resting in bed with a cool cloth on her head and a thermometer beside her.](assets/img/nursery-2/1st-term/basic-science/week8-resting-when-sick.png)
**Worksheet**

1. Sickness means the body A) is sleeping well B) is growing C) is working properly D) is not working properly
2. Which one is a sign of sickness? A) a clean cloth B) a sharp pencil C) running fast D) high temperature
3. Malaria is brought to us by A) rain B) a book C) a mosquito D) a goat
4. A sick person should drink plenty of A) kerosene B) cold soda C) water D) palm oil
5. Fill in: We cover our mouth when we ____.
6. Mention three ways of preventing sickness.
7. Write two sentences on how a sick person is cared for at home.

**My own work**

Write four words that tell us a person is sick (for example: fever, ____).

Draw the four things a sick child needs to get well again and colour them.

---

### WEEK 9 — ROCKS AND SOIL

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · Basic Science and Technology · Second Term W1 (pp 494–497).*

**You will learn to**

• define a rock and soil in one line each
• sort stones by size
• name three uses of rocks
• tell sandy, clayey and loamy soil apart by touch

**Things to know**

• A rock is a **hard, solid thing found in nature** on the earth; nobody made it.
• Some rocks were made when hot liquid from inside the earth (lava) cooled and became hard.
• Some were made when little bits of stone were pressed together for a very long time; others were changed by great heat and pressure.
• Sizes we can see: **big rocks, small stones, pebbles and sand**.
• Rocks are used for building houses, making roads, decoration and making some tools.
• Soil is the **top layer of the earth where plants grow**.
• Soil comes from rocks that broke into tiny bits over many years, mixed with dead plants and dead animals.
• **Sandy soil** is grainy and the water runs through it quickly.
• **Clay soil** is sticky when wet and smooth when dry; it can be moulded into a ball.
• **Loamy soil** is a mixture of both and is the best soil for plants.
• Plants grow in soil, animals live in soil, and the food we eat comes from soil.
• Take the soil away and there is no yam, no rice and no tree.

**Words for my notebook**

A rock is hard and comes from nature. Soil is the top layer of the earth. Loamy soil is best for plants.

**Let us talk**

1. What is a rock?
2. Show me the pebbles.
3. Name two uses of rocks.
4. Which soil is sticky when it is wet?
5. Which soil is best for plants, and why?


![A big rock, small stones sorted by size and a plant growing in soil.](assets/img/nursery-2/1st-term/basic-science/week9-rocks-and-soil.png)
**Worksheet**

1. A rock is A) a kind of animal B) a kind of food C) soft and made by people D) hard and found in nature
2. Which of these is the smallest size of rock? A) pebble B) stone C) big rock D) boulder
3. Sandy soil lets water A) run through quickly B) stay on top C) freeze D) rise up
4. Which soil is the best for growing plants? A) clay B) sand C) gravel D) loam
5. Fill in: Soil is the ____ layer of the earth where plants grow.
6. Mention three uses of rocks.
7. Write two sentences on the three types of soil.

**My own work**

Put two small stones and one leaf in a paper bag and bring them to class tomorrow.

# Cultural and Creative Arts (CCA)

Weeks 1 to 6, 8 and 9. No note for Week 7 (Mid-Term), 10 (Revision), 11 (Examination).
*Depth: NERDC 2025 · Nursery 3 and Nursery 2 · Creativity · pp 412–414 and 564–571.*


### WEEK 1 — COLOURING A CAR

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · Creativity · First Term W1 (pp 564–566).*

**You will learn to**

• know the parts of a car that I can see on paper
• colour inside the lines without going out
• choose the colours I want for my car
• say what a car does for people

**Things to know**

• A car is a means of transportation: it carries people and things from place to place.
• The parts of the car on my colouring paper: **body, wheels, windows, doors and headlights**.
• Any colour is a good colour for a car — the colour I choose is my own idea.
• Windows are often blue or black; wheels are black or grey; headlights are yellow.
• Cars come in different types: **sedan, SUV and sports car**.
• A picture looks complete when I add a background: the road under it, the sky above it, and trees beside it.
• Colouring inside the lines makes my work neat; finishing the whole page makes it complete.

**Words for my notebook**

A car has a body, wheels, windows, doors and headlights. A car carries people and things.

**Let us talk**

1. Point to the wheels on your car.
2. What colour did you give the windows, and why?
3. Name one other thing that carries people.
4. What did you draw under the car?


![A simple car with big shapes ready to colour.](assets/img/nursery-2/1st-term/cultural-and-creative-arts/week1-colouring-a-car.png)
**Worksheet**

1. Which part of the car makes it move? A) horn B) door C) wheel D) window
2. Colouring inside the lines makes my work A) dark B) wet C) messy D) neat
3. Which of these is a type of car? A) single B) sandal C) sedan D) centre
4. A car is a means of A) eating B) sleeping C) transportation D) writing
5. Fill in: The part you look through in a car is the ____.
6. Name three parts of a car you coloured.
7. Write two sentences on the picture you made and the colours you chose.

**My own work**

Finish your car: colour the whole outline and add a road, a sky and one tree.

---

### WEEK 2 — THUMB PRINT AND HAND PRINT ART

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · Creativity · First Term W4 (pp 564–566).*

**You will learn to**

• press my thumb and my hand on paper to make pictures
• make a caterpillar, a butterfly, a fish or a flower from prints
• add details after the paint has dried
• wash my hands well after the activity

**Things to know**

• A thumb print is made by dipping the thumb in washable paint or an ink pad and pressing it on paper.
• Many thumb prints in a line make a **caterpillar**; two joined make a **butterfly**; small ones scattered make a **ladybug on a leaf**; three in a row can make a **fish**.
• A hand print is made by painting the whole palm and fingers and pressing the hand flat on the paper.
• A hand print can become a bird, a fish, a tree or a person with fingers as legs and hair.
• Every thumb print is different — mine is not like my friend's; that is what makes my picture mine.
• After the paint is dry, I add eyes, legs and lines with a marker or a pen.
• More than one print can make a whole scene: a garden, a pond, a family.
• When I finish, I wash my hands with soap and water.

**Words for my notebook**

I dip, I press, I lift. My thumb print is unique — nobody has another one like it.

**Let us talk**

1. What do we dip the thumb in?
2. Show me your butterfly or caterpillar.
3. Name one thing you made with your hand print.
4. What must be dry before I use a marker?


![A caterpillar, a butterfly, a fish and a flower made from prints.](assets/img/nursery-2/1st-term/cultural-and-creative-arts/week2-print-art-shapes.png)
**Worksheet**

1. A thumb print is made with A) a ruler B) chalk C) water only D) paint
2. Many thumb prints in a line can look like a A) car B) house C) ball D) caterpillar
3. Before I add details with a marker, the paint must be A) cold B) dry C) wet D) hot
4. Every thumb print is A) square B) unique C) the same D) blue
5. Fill in: After the activity I wash my ____ with soap.
6. Name three pictures you can make with thumb prints.
7. Write two sentences on the picture you made in class today.

**My own work**

Make six more thumb prints on a spare page and turn three of them into animals.

---

### WEEK 3 — COLOURING BARNEY

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · Creativity · First Term W8 (pp 564–566).*

**You will learn to**

• colour a character inside its outline
• keep the body colours the character is known for
• add a background to my picture
• colour carefully and neatly to the end

**Things to know**

• Barney is a **purple dinosaur** in a children's programme; his belly and his back details are **green**.
• A character picture has an outline — the lines that show where the body, the arms and the legs end.
• Colouring inside the outline keeps the character looking like itself.
• I start with the biggest part (the body), then the smaller parts (the belly, the hands, the feet).
• A background makes the picture complete: a playground, a park, or the sky with clouds.
• Characters also live in books, on cards and on screens; a colouring page takes one of them onto my paper.
• Neat work is finished work: no white patches left inside, no colour outside the lines.

**Words for my notebook**

Barney is a purple dinosaur with a green belly. I colour inside the lines and finish my whole page.

**Let us talk**

1. What colour is Barney's body?
2. What did you draw behind him?
3. Show me one place where you stayed inside the line.
4. Why should we not leave white patches?


![A friendly dinosaur outline ready to colour.](assets/img/nursery-2/1st-term/cultural-and-creative-arts/week3-colouring-the-dinosaur.png)
**Worksheet**

1. Barney is a A) red bird B) yellow goat C) purple dinosaur D) blue fish
2. The lines that show where the body ends are the A) paper B) pencil C) colours D) outline
3. A background in my picture can be A) my bag B) the sky C) a shoe D) a spoon
4. Which colour is Barney's belly? A) brown B) black C) white D) green
5. Fill in: I colour inside the ____ so my work is neat.
6. Name two things you can draw behind a character.
7. Write two sentences on how you finished your colouring.

**My own work**

Draw one friend standing beside Barney and colour both of them.

---

### WEEK 4 — COLOURING A PIG

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 2 · Creativity · First Term W9 and Nursery 3 · Creativity · First Term W9 (pp 412–414, 564–566).*

**You will learn to**

• name the parts of a pig I am colouring
• choose the colours a pig can be
• colour a curved shape neatly
• say two things we get from farm animals

**Things to know**

• Parts of a pig: **snout** (the long nose), **curly tail**, **round body**, **small ears** and **hooves**.
• A pig is a farm animal; it lives in a pen on the farm with goats, sheep and hens.
• Pig colours: **pink** is the most common; some are **brown, black or white**.
• Details that make the picture real: two eyes, two nostrils on the snout, and hooves on the legs.
• A muddy background, a barn or a fence tells where the pig lives.
• We learn about farm animals through art: the shape of the body, the size of the ears, the kind of legs.
• Farm animals give us food, and they are looked after every day.
• Curved lines are harder to colour inside than straight ones; I go slowly on the round back and the snout.

**Words for my notebook**

A pig has a snout, a curly tail, a round body and hooves. I colour inside the curved lines.

**Let us talk**

1. Point to the snout on your picture.
2. What colour did you make the pig?
3. Where does a pig live?
4. Name one other farm animal.


![A pig outline with a curly tail ready to colour.](assets/img/nursery-2/1st-term/cultural-and-creative-arts/week4-colouring-a-pig.png)
**Worksheet**

1. The long nose of a pig is called its A) hoof B) horn C) snout D) wing
2. Which colour is the most common for a pig? A) grey B) purple C) orange D) pink
3. A pig lives A) in the river B) in a tree C) on a farm D) in the sky
4. The foot of a pig is a A) web B) hoof C) claw D) paw
5. Fill in: A pig's tail is ____.
6. Name three parts of the pig you coloured.
7. Write two sentences on a farm animal you know and what it gives us.

**My own work**

Add a fence and a barn behind your pig, then colour the whole page.

---

### WEEK 5 — STRAW JEWELRY MAKING

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · Creativity · First Term W10 (pp 564–566).*

**You will learn to**

• cut straws into small bead-sized pieces with help
• thread the pieces onto a string
• make a pattern that repeats
• tie a knot so that my necklace does not open

**Things to know**

• The things I need: **drinking straws cut into small pieces, string or yarn, and scissors**.
• The straw pieces are as small as beads, so the necklace can lie flat around my neck.
• Straws are cut into bead-sized pieces with a grown-up guiding the scissors.
• Threading: I push the string through one piece, then the next, keeping them in a line.
• A pattern is a repeat: red, blue, red, blue — or red, red, yellow, red, red, yellow.
• The same pieces can make a **necklace** (longer) or a **bracelet** (shorter).
• A knot at both ends, tied with help, keeps the work from falling apart.
• Patterns are everywhere: on cloth, on beads, on the floor tiles; my necklace is a pattern I made.
• Rolling, pushing and threading between my fingers make my hands clever for writing.
• The jewelry I made can be worn, and it can be a gift for somebody I love.

**Words for my notebook**

I cut, I thread, I repeat a pattern, I tie a knot. My necklace is wearable art.

**Let us talk**

1. Show me the pattern you repeated.
2. How many colours did you use?
3. What keeps the beads from falling off?
4. Who helped with the scissors, and why?


![A necklace and a bracelet made of straw beads in a repeating pattern.](assets/img/nursery-2/1st-term/cultural-and-creative-arts/week5-straw-bead-jewelry.png)
**Worksheet**

1. The small pieces for my necklace are cut from A) wood B) paper C) leaves D) straws
2. Red, blue, red, blue is a A) pattern B) knot C) colour D) line
3. What do I use to hold the pieces together? A) water B) glue C) chalk D) a string
4. A shorter necklace worn on the wrist is a A) hat B) bag C) bracelet D) shoe
5. Fill in: I tie a ____ at the end so my work does not open.
6. Name the three things needed for straw jewelry.
7. Write two sentences on the pattern you chose and how you made it.

**My own work**

Wear your necklace or bracelet to school tomorrow and say its pattern out loud in class.

### WEEK 6 — COLOURING AN UMBRELLA

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 2 · Creativity · First Term W10 and Nursery 3 · Creativity · Second Term W1 (pp 412–414, 568–571).*

**You will learn to**

• name the parts of an umbrella
• mix bright colours together without fear
• make stripes, dots or sections on my umbrella
• colour a curved outline neatly

**Things to know**

• Parts of an umbrella: the **canopy** (the round top), the **handle**, and the **spokes** inside the canopy.
• An umbrella keeps rain off us, and it keeps the strong sun off us too.
• Bright colours look good on an umbrella — red, blue, yellow, green and orange can sit together.
• Patterns I can make: **stripes** from the top to the edge, **polka dots**, or **sections** where each part gets its own colour.
• Colour combinations: yellow goes with blue, red goes with green; two colours in one section make it look designed.
• The background can carry **rain, raindrops, clouds** — or a shining sun if the day is hot.
• The curved edge of the canopy is where neatness shows; I colour slowly near the curve.
• Finishing the whole page — canopy, handle and background — makes the work complete.

**Words for my notebook**

An umbrella has a canopy, spokes and a handle. It keeps rain and sun off us.

**Let us talk**

1. Point to the canopy on your picture.
2. What pattern did you put on it?
3. What did you draw in the sky?
4. Name two colours that sit well together in your work.


![An open umbrella with blank panels ready to colour.](assets/img/nursery-2/1st-term/cultural-and-creative-arts/week6-colouring-an-umbrella.png)
**Worksheet**

1. The round top of the umbrella is the A) spoke B) canopy C) roof D) handle
2. An umbrella keeps off rain and A) sun B) dust C) dogs D) wind
3. Dots all over the canopy are called A) stripes B) lines C) boxes D) polka dots
4. Which of these is inside an umbrella? A) wheels B) leaves C) spokes D) strings
5. Fill in: I colour slowly near the curved ____ of the canopy.
6. Name the parts of an umbrella.
7. Write two sentences on the colours you used for your umbrella.

**My own work**

Draw an umbrella over two people walking in the rain, and colour it.

---

### WEEK 8 — VEGETABLE PRINT ART

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · Creativity · Second Term W2 (pp 568–571).*

**You will learn to**

• use vegetables as stamps
• know the shape each vegetable makes
• dip, press and lift without smearing
• clean up after the activity

**Things to know**

• Vegetables can be stamps: an **okra cut across prints a star**, a **carrot prints a circle**, a **potato** can be carved into shapes, and **bell pepper, cabbage and celery** print their own patterns.
• The vegetable is cut for us by a grown-up — we do not use the cutting blade ourselves.
• I dip the cut face of the vegetable into paint, then press it on the paper and lift it straight up.
• Pressing hard and moving the stamp smears the print; a firm, still press gives a clean mark.
• Different vegetables can be combined: okra stars on cabbage circles, carrot rings in a line like a road.
• Prints can be made into a greeting card, a wrapper design, or a picture of a garden.
• The shape a vegetable already carries is what makes its print different from another's.
• When I finish, I wash the stamps or put them where they belong, wipe the table and wash my hands.
• Art can come from food and from nature; the same plant that feeds us can also print.

**Words for my notebook**

Okra makes a star, carrot makes a circle. I dip, press and lift — then I clean up.

**Let us talk**

1. Which vegetable made a star in your work?
2. Show me a print that came out clean.
3. What do we do after printing?
4. Name one thing you can make with vegetable prints.


![Cut okra, potato and carrot beside the star, heart and ring prints they make.](assets/img/nursery-2/1st-term/cultural-and-creative-arts/week8-vegetable-stamps.png)
**Worksheet**

1. Which vegetable prints a star shape? A) onion skin B) rice C) okra D) yam
2. A carrot cut across prints a A) circle B) line C) square D) star
3. Who cuts the vegetables for printing? A) a grown-up B) any pupil C) the class goat D) nobody
4. To get a clean print I must A) blow on it B) press and hold still C) move it about D) rub hard
5. Fill in: After printing, I wash the ____ and the table.
6. Name three vegetables that can be used as stamps.
7. Write two sentences on the picture you made with prints.

**My own work**

Make one more print on a spare page and write under it the name of the vegetable that made it.

---

### WEEK 9 — PLASTICINE (ADVANCED)

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · Creativity · Second Term W3 (pp 568–571).*

**You will learn to**

• roll, flatten, pinch and coil plasticine
• build a figure that has several parts
• mix two colours to make a new one
• make a scene with more than one object

**Things to know**

• Four movements with plasticine: **rolling** (a ball or a snake), **flattening** (a pancake), **pinching** (ears, a nose) and **coiling** (a snail's shell, a rope).
• A figure with parts: a **person** with a head, a body, two arms and two legs; the limbs can be joined loosely so they move.
• A detailed animal has ears, a tail, legs and eyes; a vehicle has a body, wheels and windows.
• Small pieces go on big pieces: make the body first, then add the smaller parts.
• Two colours pressed together make a new shade: red and yellow make orange; blue and yellow make green; black and white make grey.
• A scene holds more than one object: a house with a car and a tree; a farm with animals and a farmer.
• Building makes me think in three ways: what sits under, what sits on top, what will fall.
• Too much pressing spoils the shape; I hold my work from the bottom, not the top.
• My plasticine work is put on the table to dry a little before it is moved into my bag.

**Words for my notebook**

I roll, flatten, pinch and coil. Big part first, then small parts. Red and yellow make orange.

**Let us talk**

1. Show me how you make a coil.
2. What two colours did you mix, and what did you get?
3. Name the parts of the figure you built.
4. What did you put in your scene?


![A plasticine figure with a coil, a pancake and balls of plasticine.](assets/img/nursery-2/1st-term/cultural-and-creative-arts/week9-plasticine-figure.png)
**Worksheet**

1. Making a long snake of plasticine is A) folding B) cutting C) rolling D) painting
2. Red and yellow plasticine together make A) brown B) purple C) black D) orange
3. Blue and yellow make A) green B) gold C) pink D) grey
4. A snail's shell is made by A) flattening B) tearing C) pinching D) coiling
5. Fill in: I make the ____ part first, then the small parts.
6. Name the four movements we use with plasticine.
7. Write two sentences on the figure you built today.

**My own work**

Build one small animal with four legs, a tail and two ears, and name it.

Draw the animal you are going to build in plasticine first, colour your drawing, then build it.


---

# Christian Religious Studies (CRS)

Weeks 1 to 6, 8 and 9. No note for Week 7 (Mid-Term), 10 (Revision), 11 (Examination).
*Depth: NERDC 2025 · Primary 1 · Christian Religious Studies · First Term, pp 638–639 — the school
teaches this band two classes earlier, so the Primary 1 list is kept and told as story.*


### WEEK 1 — GOD THE CREATOR (PART 1)

*Depth: NERDC Primary 1 · CRS · First Term W1 (pp 638–639).*

**You will learn to**

• say who God is in one line
• tell what God made on the first and the second day
• name things in God's creation around me
• thank God for what I see

**Things to know**

• God is the **Supreme Being**, the **Almighty** — the greatest, and there is nobody greater than Him.
• God made the world and everything in it.
• **Day 1:** God said, "Let there be light." There was light. God divided the **light from the darkness**, and called them **day and night**.
• **Day 2:** God made the **sky** and the **clouds** above the earth.
• The sun, the moon, the trees, the rivers, my eyes and my dog are all part of God's creation.
• Things that people make (a chair, a cup, a book) are not creation the way the sky is — people make them from things God already made.
• When I look at something beautiful, I can say: "Thank you, God, for Your creation."

**Words for my notebook**

God is the Almighty. God made the world. On Day 1 He made light; on Day 2 He made the sky.

**Let us talk**

1. Who made the world?
2. What did God make on the first day?
3. Name three things around you that God made.
4. What do we say when we see something beautiful?

**Worksheet**

1. God is the A) small B) guest C) neighbour D) Supreme Being
2. On Day 1 God made A) fish B) light C) stars D) birds
3. The division of light from darkness gave us A) church and mosque B) yam and rice C) day and night D) rain and wind
4. Which of these is part of God's creation? A) a plastic cup B) a school bag C) the sky D) a chair
5. Fill in: On Day 2 God made the sky and the ____.
6. Mention two things God made.
7. Write two sentences thanking God for something you saw today.

**My own work**

Write the names of five things you can see from your window that God made.

Draw three things God made on the first day and colour the light.

---

### WEEK 2 — GOD THE CREATOR (PART 2)

*Depth: NERDC Primary 1 · CRS · First Term W2 (pp 638–639).*

**You will learn to**

• tell what God made on Days 3, 4 and 5
• name the living things God made
• sing a creation song
• care for what God made

**Things to know**

• **Day 3:** God made the **dry land and the seas**, and He made **grass, plants, trees and flowers**.
• **Day 4:** God made the **sun** to rule the day, the **moon** to rule the night, and the **stars**.
• **Day 5:** God made the **fish in the sea** and the **birds in the sky**, the great whales and every small creeping thing of the water.
• God told the fish and the birds to be many all over the earth.
• Each living thing has its own home: fish in water, birds in the air and on trees, animals on the land.
• Naming the creatures is part of knowing them: goat, hen, eagle, tilapia, catfish, sparrow.
• Creation songs we can sing: "All good gifts around us are from God above"; "He made the stars also."
• Because God made these things, I do not waste food, I do not break branches for fun, and I do not throw dirt into the water where fish live.

**Words for my notebook**

Day 3 — land, sea and plants. Day 4 — sun, moon and stars. Day 5 — fish and birds.

**Let us talk**

1. What did God make on the third day?
2. Which two lights did God make on the fourth day?
3. Name three living things from Day 5.
4. Mention one way we care for God's creation.

**Worksheet**

1. On Day 3 God made A) the birds B) man C) land, seas and plants D) the sun
2. The light that rules the night is the A) fire B) lamp C) moon D) torch
3. Fish and birds were made on Day A) 7 B) 5 C) 1 D) 3
4. Which of these did God NOT make on Day 4? A) goat B) sun C) stars D) moon
5. Fill in: God told the fish and the birds to be ____ on the earth.
6. Mention two things we do to care for God's creation.
7. Write two sentences on the day of creation you like best and why.

**My own work**

Draw one thing God made on Day 3, one on Day 4 and one on Day 5, and write the day under each.

---

### WEEK 3 — GOD THE CREATOR (PART 3)

*Depth: NERDC Primary 1 · CRS · First Term W3 (pp 638–639).*

**You will learn to**

• tell what God made on Days 6 and 7
• say in one line what it means that we are made in God's image
• say why rest and worship matter
• thank God for making me

**Things to know**

• **Day 6:** God made the **animals of the land** — cattle, creeping things and wild beasts — and then He made **man and woman**.
• God made people **in His own image**: like God in the way we think, love, speak and choose what is right.
• God told the first people to be kind, to have children, and to take care of the earth.
• **Day 7:** God **rested** from all His work, and He made that day holy.
• Rest is not laziness: God Himself rested, so rest is good for the body that works.
• On the seventh day people go to church or mosque to **worship** God and thank Him.
• I am special because God made me — not by chance, but on purpose, as part of the last day of creation.
• A prayer of thanks: "Thank You, God, for making me and for making everything."

**Words for my notebook**

On Day 6 God made animals and people. On Day 7 He rested. We are made in God's image.

**Let us talk**

1. Whom did God make on Day 6?
2. What did God do on Day 7?
3. Say one thing that shows you are made in God's image.
4. Why do we go to church or mosque on the seventh day?

**Worksheet**

1. On Day 6 God made A) light B) animals and people C) the sea D) the stars
2. People are made in the image of A) the king B) their friends C) the sun D) God
3. God rested on Day A) 6 B) 5 C) 1 D) 7
4. The first people God made were A) a king and a queen B) twins C) two brothers D) a man and a woman
5. Fill in: We are special because ____ made us.
6. Mention two things God made on Day 6.
7. Write two sentences on why rest and worship are good for us.

**My own work**

Write a short prayer of three sentences thanking God for making you.

Draw two animals from the story and colour the bigger one.

---

### WEEK 4 — GOD, THE GIVER OF GOOD THINGS (PART 1)

*Depth: NERDC Primary 1 · CRS · First Term W4 (pp 638–639).*

**You will learn to**

• name the good things God gives us
• say that life and breath come from God
• thank God for my family and my home
• share good things with others

**Things to know**

• God is the One who gives us good things, and we thank Him for them.
• God gives us **life and breath** — the fact that I am awake this morning is a gift.
• God gives us **families**: father, mother, brothers, sisters and relatives.
• God gives us **homes** to live in — a room to sleep in, a place to eat, a door to shut at night.
• Nobody can add one hour to their life by worrying; the days we live are God's gift.
• When a good thing comes to me, I do two things: I say "Thank you, God", and I share it.
• A home is kept clean and peaceful by everybody in it — that too is part of valuing the gift.

**Words for my notebook**

God gives me life, my family and my home. I thank Him and I share.

**Let us talk**

1. Who gives us life and breath?
2. Name the people in your family that you thank God for.
3. What is one good thing about your home?
4. What two things do we do when something good comes to us?

**Worksheet**

1. God gives us life and A) trouble B) sleep only C) breath D) toys only
2. The people God puts around us first are our A) dogs B) strangers C) enemies D) family
3. Our home is a gift from A) the market B) God C) the shop D) the neighbour
4. When somebody gives me something good, I A) thank God and share B) fight C) hide it D) boast
5. Fill in: God gives us ____ to live in.
6. Mention three good things God has given you.
7. Write two sentences of thanks to God for your family.

**My own work**

Write the names of four people in your family and one good thing each of them does.

Draw four good things God gives us and colour the one you like best.

---

### WEEK 5 — GOD, THE GIVER OF GOOD THINGS (PART 2)

*Depth: NERDC Primary 1 · CRS · First Term W7 (pp 638–639).*

**You will learn to**

• name the food, water and clothing God provides
• say who gives us schools and teachers
• give thanks before a meal
• not waste what God has provided

**Things to know**

• God gives us **food to eat**: fruits (mango, banana, watermelon, orange), vegetables (ewédú, ugu, spinach), rice, yam, beans, cassava and fish.
• The rain that waters the farm comes from God; the farmer who plants is also God's help.
• God gives us **water to drink** — clean water keeps the body well.
• God gives us **clothes to wear** — cotton grown from the earth, cloth woven and dyed.
• God gives us **schools and teachers** so that we can learn to read, to count and to know right from wrong.
• Before a meal we say: "Thank You, God, for this food." We do not throw food on the floor and we finish what we take.
• Daily provision means: today's food for today, and thanks today — not keeping greed for tomorrow.

**Words for my notebook**

God gives food, water, clothes, schools and teachers. We thank God before we eat.

**Let us talk**

1. Name three foods God has provided.
2. Where does the water we drink come from?
3. Who does God give us so that we can learn?
4. What do we say before a meal?

**Worksheet**

1. Which of these is a fruit? A) watermelon B) plate C) chalk D) chair
2. God gives us ____ to drink. A) sand B) kerosene C) ink D) water
3. The rain that waters the farm comes from A) the market B) the shop C) God D) the river only
4. We thank God for schools and A) teachers B) tins C) ticks D) tears
5. Fill in: Before eating we say "Thank You, God, for this ____."
6. Mention four things God gives us every day.
7. Write two sentences on how we show that we do not waste God's gifts.

**My own work**

Write the prayer you say before eating, in your own three sentences.

Draw one good thing you have at home and colour it.

---

### WEEK 6 — GOD, THE GIVER OF GOOD THINGS (PART 3)

*Depth: NERDC Primary 1 · CRS · First Term W8 (pp 638–639).*

**You will learn to**

• name the good things God gives that we cannot buy
• say how God protects us
• thank God in prayer for health, friends and weather
• ask God for help before doing something risky

**Things to know**

• God gives us **friends to play with** — a friend is a gift, not something we buy.
• God gives us **health and strength**: a body that runs, eyes that see, a mouth that speaks.
• God gives us the **sun** for light and warmth, the **rain** for the farm, and good **weather** for our journeys.
• God **protects us from danger** — from the road, from water, from a bad fall, from people who mean harm.
• Thanking God is called **praise**; asking God for help is called **petition**; both belong in our prayers.
• A short prayer of thanks: "Thank You, God, for my friends, for my body, for the sun and the rain. Keep me from danger."
• Before crossing a busy road or going near water, we still ask God's help — and we also use what we were taught to do.

**Words for my notebook**

Friends, health, sun, rain and safety are God's gifts. We thank God and we ask for protection.

**Let us talk**

1. Name one gift from God that you cannot buy with money.
2. What does God give that makes crops grow?
3. How does God protect us?
4. What is the difference between praise and petition?

**Worksheet**

1. God gives us friends to A) play with B) sell C) fight with D) fear
2. Health and strength come from A) money B) the market C) the doctor only D) God
3. The rain that makes crops grow is given by A) the driver B) God C) the trader D) the farmer
4. God ____ us from danger. A) forgets B) punishes C) hides D) protects
5. Fill in: Saying "thank You God" is called ____.
6. Mention three gifts from God that money cannot buy.
7. Write two sentences of a prayer of thanks.

**My own work**

Write a prayer of four sentences: thank God for two gifts and ask for protection in two ways.

Draw two hands: one for giving and one for working. Colour the giving hand.

---

### WEEK 8 — GOD'S GIFT OF HIS SON (PART 1)

*Depth: NERDC Primary 1 · CRS · First Term W9 (pp 638–639).*

**You will learn to**

• say why God sent Jesus
• tell the beginning of the birth story
• name the people in the story
• say what the angels told the shepherds

**Things to know**

• **John 3:16** — God loved the world so much that He gave His only Son, so that whoever believes in Him should not perish but have everlasting life.
• God sent **His Son Jesus** into the world. This is God's greatest gift.
• **Mary** was a young woman who lived in Nazareth; an angel told her that she would have a son. **Joseph** was a carpenter, and he took care of Mary.
• Jesus was born in **Bethlehem**, and He was laid in a **manger** — a feeding trough for animals — because there was no room in the inn.
• **Angels** appeared to **shepherds** who were watching their flocks at night, and said: "Fear not: unto you is born this day a Saviour, which is Christ the Lord."
• The shepherds went and saw the baby, and they told everybody what the angels had said.
• Wise men later came from the east following a star — that part of the story belongs to next week.

**Words for my notebook**

God loved the world and sent Jesus, His Son. Jesus was born in Bethlehem in a manger.

**Let us talk**

1. Why did God send Jesus?
2. In which town was Jesus born?
3. Where was the baby laid?
4. Who heard the angels first?

**Worksheet**

1. God sent His Son because He A) was busy B) was angry C) loved the world D) was far away
2. Jesus was born in A) Lagos B) Nazareth C) Jericho D) Bethlehem
3. The baby Jesus was laid in a A) car B) bed C) manger D) basket
4. The angels first spoke to A) soldiers B) shepherds C) kings D) fishermen
5. Fill in: Mary's husband-to-be, who took care of her, was ____.
6. Name four people or beings in the birth story.
7. Write two sentences on why Jesus is called God's greatest gift.

**My own work**

Draw the manger with the baby Jesus, and write "God loved the world" under it.

---

### WEEK 9 — GOD'S GIFT OF HIS SON (PART 2)

*Depth: NERDC Primary 1 · CRS · First Term W10 (pp 638–639).*

**You will learn to**

• name the three gifts of the wise men
• say why God sent Jesus
• tell what Jesus came to teach
• say how Christmas is celebrated

**Things to know**

• **Jesus is God's greatest gift** to us, and a gift is received with thanks.
• Wise men from the east came following the star, and they brought gifts: **gold, frankincense and myrrh**.
• **Gold** is for a king; **frankincense** is a sweet perfume burned in worship; **myrrh** is an ointment used to prepare a body for burial — it told us ahead of time that Jesus came to die for us.
• Why God sent Jesus: **to save us from sin** and **to show us God's love**.
• Jesus came to **teach us how to live**: to love God, to love our neighbour, to forgive, to care for the poor and the small.
• We remember Jesus' birth at **Christmas**, on the 25th of December: churches have services, families share food, and children receive gifts.
• The best Christmas gift we can give is a kind act — the same love we received.

**Words for my notebook**

The wise men brought gold, frankincense and myrrh. God sent Jesus to save us and to show us love.

**Let us talk**

1. What three gifts did the wise men bring?
2. Why did God send Jesus?
3. Name two things Jesus taught us.
4. What do people do at Christmas?

**Worksheet**

1. The gifts of the wise men were gold, frankincense and A) money B) mango C) myrrh D) milk
2. God sent Jesus to A) save us and show God's love B) build houses C) become a king of Rome D) punish the world
3. Jesus taught us to love God and A) our neighbour B) our enemies' gifts C) money D) only ourselves
4. Christmas keeps the memory of Jesus' A) teaching B) death C) journey D) birth
5. Fill in: Frankincense is a sweet ____ burned in worship.
6. Mention two reasons why God sent Jesus.
7. Write two sentences on a kind act you can give as a Christmas gift.

**My own work**

Write one sentence for each of the three wise men's gifts, saying what each gift tells us about Jesus.

Draw the three gifts the wise men brought and colour the gold one.


---

# Basic Digital Literacy

Weeks 1 to 6, 8 and 9. No note for Week 7 (Mid-Term), 10 (Revision), 11 (Examination).
*Depth: NERDC 2025 · Primary 4 · Basic Digital Literacy · First Term W1–W2, pp 827–828 — the school
redistributes this content into the lower classes, so the Primary 4 week is the depth floor and every
line is pitched as naming, pointing and handling, not reading and writing code.*


### WEEK 1 — TECHNOLOGY AND DIGITAL DEVICES

*Depth: NERDC Primary 4 · Basic Digital Literacy · First Term W1 (pp 827–828).*

**You will learn to**

• say what technology means in one line
• name the digital devices I know
• point to a computer, a tablet, a phone, a watch and a camera
• tell which things at home are digital

**Things to know**

• **Technology** means the smart things people make and use to do work more easily.
• A **digital device** is a machine that thinks with electricity and shows us things on a screen.
• Devices I know: **computer, tablet, smartphone, smart watch, camera**.
• A **computer** is big and has a screen, keys and a mouse; a **tablet** is flat and I touch the screen with my finger.
• A **smartphone** fits in the hand; it calls, photographs, and plays music.
• A **smart watch** is worn on the wrist; it tells the time and counts my steps.
• A **camera** takes pictures; some cameras are inside a phone.
• Digital things at home: the television remote, the decoder, the radio with a screen, the microwave, the fan regulator, the ATM.
• Not everything electrical is a computer — the torch and the fridge use electricity but do not think.

**Words for my notebook**

Technology is what people make to work easily. Computer, tablet, phone, watch and camera are digital devices.

**Let us talk**

1. What is a digital device?
2. Point to the device your father or mother uses for calling.
3. What can a camera do?
4. Name one thing at home that is not a digital device.


![A desktop, a laptop, a tablet, a phone and a camera on a table.](assets/img/nursery-2/1st-term/basic-digital-literacy/week1-five-devices-on-the-table.png)
**Worksheet**

1. A digital device works with A) electricity B) wind C) water D) fire only
2. Which device is worn on the wrist? A) monitor B) smart watch C) tablet D) camera
3. Which one is used for taking pictures? A) keyboard B) camera C) CPU D) mouse
4. A tablet is mostly A) pushed with a rope B) blown C) touched with the finger D) watered
5. Fill in: A ____ lets us talk to people far away.
6. Name five digital devices.
7. Write two sentences on one device you have seen and what it does.

**My own work**

Count the digital devices you see in your house today and write the number in your notebook.

Draw three machines in your house and colour the one that makes the most noise.

---

### WEEK 2 — TYPES OF COMPUTER: DESKTOP, LAPTOP, TABLET

*Depth: NERDC Primary 4 · Basic Digital Literacy · First Term W1 (pp 827–828).*

**You will learn to**

• tell a desktop from a laptop and a tablet
• say what each type is good for
• say which parts come with a desktop
• choose the right computer for the right work

**Things to know**

• A **desktop** sits on a table and does not move about; it has a separate **monitor, keyboard, mouse** and a **CPU box**.
• A **laptop** opens and shuts like a book; the screen, the keys and the touchpad are all in it, and it has a battery.
• A **tablet** is one flat board with a screen you touch with your finger; it is the smallest and the lightest to carry.
• A desktop is used at the office or at home where it can stay; a laptop is carried to school, to work, to a journey.
• A tablet is good for watching, reading and drawing; a desktop or laptop is better for typing a long letter.
• All three can do the same kind of work; they differ in size, in how they are held, and in how they are moved.
• The **CPU** is the part that does the thinking, so it is called the brain of the computer.
• A laptop is closed when it is not in use, so that its screen is safe.

**Words for my notebook**

Desktop stays on the table, laptop folds and goes, tablet is held in the hand. The CPU is the brain.

**Let us talk**

1. Which computer do you carry in a bag?
2. What is the brain of the computer called?
3. Point to a device in this room that has a touchpad.
4. Why do we close a laptop?


![Three computers in a row: a desktop with a separate screen, keyboard, mouse and box, a laptop opened like a book, and one flat tablet.](assets/img/nursery-2/1st-term/basic-digital-literacy/week2-the-desktop-the-laptop-and-the-tablet.png)
**Worksheet**

1. Which computer stays on a table? A) phone B) desktop C) tablet D) laptop
2. The part of the computer that does the thinking is the A) pad B) CPU C) case D) mouse
3. Which one is touched with the finger on the screen? A) printer B) CPU C) desktop D) tablet
4. A laptop is shut mainly to protect its A) bag B) cable C) screen D) keys
5. Fill in: A desktop has a separate monitor, keyboard, mouse and ____.
6. Mention the three types of computer you know.
7. Write two sentences on which computer your family uses and why.

**My own work**

Draw one desktop, one laptop and one tablet, and write one use under each.

---

### WEEK 3 — BASIC PARTS: MONITOR, KEYBOARD, MOUSE, CPU

*Depth: NERDC Primary 4 · Basic Digital Literacy · First Term W1 (pp 827–828).*

**You will learn to**

• name the four basic parts of a computer
• say what each part does
• point to each part on a real computer
• put the parts in the right order of work

**Things to know**

• The four basic parts: **monitor, keyboard, mouse and CPU**.
• The **monitor** is the screen; it shows what the computer is doing — pictures, letters and numbers.
• The **keyboard** has the letters and numbers; we press the keys to put information into the computer.
• The **mouse** is held in the hand; it moves the arrow on the screen and clicks to choose things.
• The **CPU** is the box that does the work and remembers things — the brain of the computer.
• Order of work: I **give** with the keyboard or the mouse → the **CPU thinks** → the **monitor shows** the answer.
• Other parts I may see: speakers (they give out sound), a printer (it puts the picture on paper), and a USB drive (it carries files about).
• The mouse is not an animal — it is a part of the computer, and it is held gently, not thrown.

**Words for my notebook**

Keyboard and mouse put work in; CPU does the work; monitor shows the work.

**Let us talk**

1. Which part shows you the pictures?
2. What do we press to type a letter?
3. Show me how you hold the mouse.
4. Which part is called the brain?


![The monitor, the keyboard, the mouse and the CPU box joined by cables.](assets/img/nursery-2/1st-term/basic-digital-literacy/week3-the-four-basic-parts.png)
**Worksheet**

1. The screen of the computer is the A) monitor B) bag C) mouse D) keyboard
2. We type with the A) keyboard B) cable C) monitor D) speaker
3. The part that moves the arrow on the screen is the A) key B) CPU C) mouse D) fan
4. The brain of the computer is the A) mouse pad B) wire C) monitor D) CPU
5. Fill in: A printer puts the picture on ____.
6. Name the four basic parts of a computer.
7. Write two sentences on what happens when you click the mouse.

**My own work**

Label a printed picture of a computer with the four parts: monitor, keyboard, mouse, CPU.

Draw the four parts of a computer as four shapes on a desk and colour the CPU box.

---

### WEEK 4 — USES OF THE COMPUTER IN DAILY LIFE

*Depth: NERDC Primary 4 · Basic Digital Literacy · First Term W1 (pp 827–828), with the Primary 5
Third Term "AI in daily life" line kept only as examples of uses.*

**You will learn to**

• say what people use a computer for at work
• say what I use a tablet for at home
• name machines in the shop, the bank and the hospital
• tell the difference between work play and learning on a device

**Things to know**

• People write letters, draw pictures and keep records on a computer.
• In the **bank**, a computer counts money and prints the receipt; in the **shop**, the machine that reads the price is a computer.
• In the **hospital**, the computer keeps the name of the patient and shows the result of the test.
• In the **school**, we use it to learn counting, letters and reading; a tablet can show me a story with pictures.
• Computers send messages and calls to people far away, and they print photographs.
• **Voice assistants** answer a question when we speak to them, and the device that shows me cartoons also **learns which cartoons I like** — that is the computer learning from what I do.
• Time on a device is of two kinds: **learning** (letters, numbers, a story) and **play** (games, cartoons). Play is only for a little while, and my eyes rest when I look away and go out to run.
• Nobody should watch a screen for the whole day; a device is a servant, not a master.

**Words for my notebook**

The computer counts money, prints, teaches and carries messages. We use it for learning, not only for play.

**Let us talk**

1. Mention two places where a computer is used.
2. What does the machine at the shop do?
3. Say one thing you can learn on a tablet.
4. Why should we not look at a screen all day?


![Machines that help in the shop, the bank and the hospital.](assets/img/nursery-2/1st-term/basic-digital-literacy/week4-machines-at-work.png)
**Worksheet**

1. In the bank, a computer is used to A) cook B) plant C) sweep D) count money
2. A device that answers when we speak to it is a A) voice assistant B) clock tower C) broom D) chair
3. Which one is a good use of a tablet for a child? A) learning letters B) watching in the dark C) all-night games D) throwing it
4. The machine at the shop reads the A) newspaper B) book C) price D) shoe
5. Fill in: A computer can ____ photographs and messages.
6. Mention three places where computers are used every day.
7. Write two sentences on one thing you learnt on a device this week.

**My own work**

Write down two ways your family uses a phone or computer for work, and two ways for rest.

---

### WEEK 5 — CARE AND HANDLING OF DEVICES

*Depth: NERDC Primary 4 · Basic Digital Literacy · First Term W1 (pp 827–828).*

**You will learn to**

• keep a device clean and dry
• hold and carry a device with two hands
• charge a device safely with a grown-up near
• shut a device the right way

**Things to know**

• Devices are **expensive and easily spoiled**, so we handle them gently.
• Keep food and drink away from the keyboard; water inside a device spoils it for good.
• Wipe a screen with a **soft, dry cloth** — not with a rough rag, not with wet water.
• Carry a laptop or tablet with **two hands**, closed, and put it in a padded bag before it travels.
• Do not pull the cable out of the socket by the wire — hold the plug itself.
• Charging: the charger is put in and taken out by a grown-up; a charging device is not played with in the bath.
• Switch off properly: save my work, shut the programme, then turn the power off; do not just remove the light.
• Do not drop, throw, hit or sit on a device; keep it out of the sun and away from a fan blowing dust.
• A phone with a cracked screen can cut a finger — tell a grown-up at once if a device breaks.

**Words for my notebook**

Clean hands, dry cloth, two hands to carry, grown-up for the plug. Care for the device and it serves long.

**Let us talk**

1. What two things spoil a keyboard quickly?
2. Show me how you carry a tablet.
3. Who puts the charger into the socket?
4. What do you do before switching a computer off?


![A boy carrying a tablet in two hands while a device charges near a grown-up.](assets/img/nursery-2/1st-term/basic-digital-literacy/week5-carrying-with-two-hands.png)
**Worksheet**

1. A screen is wiped with A) a soft dry cloth B) a duster with water C) a rough stone D) a wet mop
2. Liquid near a keyboard is A) cheap B) dangerous to the device C) needed D) good
3. A laptop is carried with A) one finger B) a rope C) the teeth D) two hands
4. The charger is put into the socket by A) a grown-up B) the class dog C) any pupil D) a baby
5. Fill in: Do not pull the cable by the ____; hold the plug.
6. Mention three rules for taking care of devices.
7. Write two sentences on what you do before switching a computer off.

**My own work**

Wipe the dust off one device in your house with a soft dry cloth, and tell your class what you cleaned.

---

### WEEK 6 — COMPUTER LAB SAFETY RULES

*Depth: NERDC Primary 4 · Basic Digital Literacy · First Term W1 (pp 827–828).*

**You will learn to**

• say what a computer lab is
• state the rules we keep in the lab
• sit and move correctly in the lab
• tell the teacher at once when something is wrong

**Things to know**

• A **computer lab** is the room in the school where many computers are kept for lessons.
• We **walk** in the lab; we do not run, because a cable or a chair can throw us down.
• We go in **quietly**, when we are called, and we sit at the machine we are given.
• **No food, no drink and no water bottle** on the desk with a computer.
• We do not touch the cables, the sockets or the back of the machine; electric wire can bite.
• Only the teacher switches on the computers and the lights.
• We do not change the settings, delete somebody's work, or move the mouse and keyboard violently.
• If a machine gives smoke, a bad smell, a shock or a strange sound: **leave it and tell the teacher at once**.
• We push our chair in and leave the lab as we found it.

**Words for my notebook**

In the computer lab we walk, we sit quietly, we keep food out, and we tell the teacher at once if something is wrong.

**Let us talk**

1. What is a computer lab?
2. Why must we not run in the lab?
3. Who switches on the computers?
4. What do you do if a machine begins to smoke?


![A boy sitting straight at a computer desk with both feet on the floor.](assets/img/nursery-2/1st-term/basic-digital-literacy/week6-sitting-correctly-in-the-lab.png)
**Worksheet**

1. In the computer lab we must A) run B) walk C) shout D) jump
2. Food and drink in the lab are A) not allowed B) allowed on the desk C) needed D) given to machines
3. The first thing to do when a cable is loose is to A) pull it B) tell the teacher C) hide it D) cut it
4. The computers are switched on by A) the monitor B) the teacher C) the pupils D) any visitor
5. Fill in: We leave the lab as we ____ it.
6. Mention four safety rules of the computer lab.
7. Write two sentences on what you saw in the lab today and the rule that kept it safe.

**My own work**

Write five rules of the computer lab on a sheet and put it on the wall of your notebook.

---

### WEEK 8 — FROM THE ABACUS TO TODAY'S COMPUTER

*Depth: NERDC Primary 4 · Basic Digital Literacy · First Term W2 (pp 827–828).*

**You will learn to**

• name the first tool people used to count
• tell how computers changed over time
• name the five generations in order
• say what became smaller, faster and cheaper

**Things to know**

• Long ago people counted with **stones and fingers**, then with the **abacus** — a frame with beads that slide on wires.
• The abacus is still used in some markets and some schools: beads up means add, beads down means take away.
• The first real computing machines were **very big** — one filled a whole room, needed a bed of vacuum tubes, and could be spoiled by a fly inside.
• **Five generations of computers**, in order: **vacuum tubes → transistors → integrated circuits → microprocessors → AI**.
• Vacuum tubes glowed and grew hot; the **transistor** replaced them and was tiny; an **integrated circuit** put many parts on one little chip; a **microprocessor** put the whole brain on one chip.
• The fifth generation is the generation of **AI** — machines that learn and decide.
• With every generation the computer became **smaller, faster and cheaper**: what once filled a room now sits on a wrist.
• Old machines are found in museums; some grandparents still keep an abacus in a drawer.

**Words for my notebook**

Before computers: fingers, stones, the abacus. Then five generations — each smaller, faster and cheaper.

**Let us talk**

1. What did people use to count before machines?
2. What does an abacus look like?
3. Name the two biggest changes from the first computer to your father's phone.
4. Which generation are we living in now?


![An abacus, an old counting machine and a new laptop in a row.](assets/img/nursery-2/1st-term/basic-digital-literacy/week8-from-the-abacus-to-today.png)
**Worksheet**

1. The frame with sliding beads used for counting is a A) abacus B) laptop C) scale D) ruler
2. The first generation of computers used A) phones B) vacuum tubes C) chips D) pens
3. The tiny part that replaced vacuum tubes is the A) fan B) transistor C) abacus D) candle
4. Computers over the years have become A) heavier B) smaller and faster C) dearer D) bigger and slower
5. Fill in: The fifth generation of computers is the generation of ____.
6. Name the five generations in order.
7. Write two sentences on how the computer of today is different from the first computers.

**My own work**

Draw an abacus with ten beads on one wire and write what you use your fingers for when you count.

---

### WEEK 9 — KEY INVENTORS: CHARLES BABBAGE AND ADA LOVELACE

*Depth: NERDC Primary 4 · Basic Digital Literacy · First Term W2 (pp 827–828).*

**You will learn to**

• name the father of the computer
• say what Ada Lovelace did
• put the two inventors in their time order
• say why an idea can be as valuable as a machine

**Things to know**

• **Charles Babbage** was an Englishman who, about two hundred years ago, designed a machine called the **Analytical Engine**; because of that plan he is called the **father of the computer**.
• His first machine was called the **Difference Engine**; it was built to work out tables of numbers without mistakes.
• Babbage drew the plan and made parts, but the workshop of his day could not build the whole thing from his drawing.
• **Ada Lovelace** wrote the first set of instructions for his machine — the first **computer programme**; she is called the first programmer in the world.
• Ada saw further than her machine: she wrote that one day such a machine could compose music and do more than merely count.
• The **Ada** language of programming was named after her.
• One built the body, the other taught the body to think: an idea can be as valuable as a machine.
• Their names are remembered today in museums, in books and in the names of computer languages.

**Words for my notebook**

Babbage planned the first computer machine; Ada Lovelace wrote the first programme for it.

**Let us talk**

1. Who is called the father of the computer?
2. What did Ada Lovelace write?
3. Which of them came first with the plan — the machine or the programme?
4. Why do we still say their names today?


![An old brass counting machine with gears beside a blank paper and a pen.](assets/img/nursery-2/1st-term/basic-digital-literacy/week9-the-thinking-machine.png)
**Worksheet**

1. The father of the computer is A) Ada Babbage B) Thomas Edison C) Charles Babbage D) Isaac Newton
2. Babbage's machine plan was called the A) Steam Boat B) Printing Press C) Loom D) Analytical Engine
3. The first computer programmer was A) Mary Kingsley B) Ada Lovelace C) Flora Shaw D) Queen Amina
4. Ada Lovelace wrote A) the first programme B) a law C) a story book D) a song
5. Fill in: A ____ language was named after Ada Lovelace.
6. Name the two inventors and say one thing each of them did.
7. Write two sentences on why an idea can be as valuable as a machine.

**My own work**

Write one sentence about Babbage and one about Ada Lovelace, and say which of the two you would like to meet.

Draw one big old machine with many wheels on it, the kind Babbage made, and colour the wheels.


---

# Nigerian History

Weeks 1 to 6, 8 and 9. No note for Week 7 (Mid-Term), 10 (Revision), 11 (Examination).
*Depth: NERDC 2025 · Primary 1 · Nigerian History · First Term, pp 649–650 — the school teaches this
band two classes earlier, so the Primary 1 list stands as the floor and is told as story and picture.*


### WEEK 1 — MEANING AND IMPORTANCE OF HISTORY (PART 1)

*Depth: NERDC Primary 1 · Nigerian History · First Term W1 (pp 649–650).*

**You will learn to**

• say what history is in one line
• give an example of the past from my own life
• name the people and things that tell us about the past
• say one reason we learn history

**Things to know**

• **History is the story of the past** — of what happened before now.
• Yesterday is past. Last week is past. Last year is past. This very minute, when it has gone, is past too.
• What I did yesterday is a little history: "I went to my uncle's house." "We ate rice."
• Last week: a birthday, a naming ceremony, a funeral, the day the rain broke the footpath.
• Last year: "I was in Nursery 1, and I was smaller."
• Who tells us the past? **Grandparents, fathers and mothers, the pastor and the imam, the old men of the street.**
• What shows us the past? **A photograph, an old object (the kerosene lamp, the iron that was heated in the fire), a book, a ruin, a monument.**
• Why we learn history: **it tells us about our families** — who our grandparents were, and where our people come from.
• History is not a fairy tale: a story that is *true* and *happened* is history.

**Words for my notebook**

History is the story of the past. Yesterday, last week and last year are all past. Grandparents and photographs tell us the past.

**Let us talk**

1. What is history?
2. Say one thing you did yesterday.
3. Mention two people who can tell you about the past.
4. What can an old photograph tell us?
5. Say one reason we learn history.

**Worksheet**

1. History is the story of A) today's lunch B) the past C) the future D) the sky
2. Which one belongs to the past? A) right now B) yesterday C) this minute D) today at noon
3. Who can tell us about the past? A) a grandparent B) a plate C) a stone D) a chair
4. A photograph of when you were small tells us about A) next term B) the weather C) the past D) the price of yam
5. Fill in: Last year I was in Nursery ____.
6. Mention two things that help us know about the past.
7. Write two sentences on something you did last week.

**My own work**

Ask at home for one true story about when you were a baby, and come to tell the class.

Draw three things that were here before you were born and colour the oldest one.

---

### WEEK 2 — MEANING AND IMPORTANCE OF HISTORY (PART 2)

*Depth: NERDC Primary 1 · Nigerian History · First Term W2 (pp 649–650).*

**You will learn to**

• say which family I belong to
• say where my people come from
• name a lesson the past teaches
• draw my family tree

**Things to know**

• History is about **identity**: to which family do I belong? Every family has a name, and my name is part of it.
• "I am a child of the ______ family." That family name came from my grandfather, and his father before him.
• History tells us **where we come from**: my father's people come from ______; my mother's people come from ______. It may be a village, a town, or a street in this city.
• Learning from the past is how we grow wise: grandma says, *"The farmer who worked his land ate well; the one who slept was hungry."*
• History helps us **make good decisions**: before I do a thing, I can ask "What happened last time somebody did this?"
• A **family tree** is a drawing that shows the order of the family: **grandparents at the top, then father and mother, then the children**.
• Drawing activity of the week: our trees went on the wall, and we looked at one another's.
• The past also tells us what has changed: lamp then, bulb now; mortar and pestle then, milling machine now.

**Words for my notebook**

I belong to the ______ family. My people come from ______. The past teaches us what is right.

**Let us talk**

1. What is your family name?
2. Where do your grandparents come from?
3. Say one lesson the past teaches us.
4. Whom do we put at the top of a family tree?
5. Mention one thing that has changed since your grandfather was small.

**Worksheet**

1. At the top of the family tree we write A) the babies B) the friends C) the grandparents D) the children
2. Your father's mother is your A) aunt B) sister C) cousin D) grandma
3. Knowing your family's name gives you A) a pen B) a shoe C) food D) identity
4. The past can teach us to A) work hard B) quarrel C) steal D) lie
5. Fill in: My father's people come from ____.
6. Draw or name the three levels of a family tree.
7. Write two sentences on something your family is known for.

**My own work**

Finish your family tree and write one thing your family is known for under it.

---

### WEEK 3 — PEOPLES AND PLACES IN THE COMMUNITY (PART 1)

*Depth: NERDC Primary 1 · Nigerian History · First Term W3 (pp 649–650).*

**You will learn to**

• say what a community is
• name the people who live around me
• name the people who help us in the community
• greet a neighbour well

**Things to know**

• A **community** is the people and the places around us — the street, the houses, the shops, the church and the mosque, the field where children play.
• In our community there are **different people**: children, youths, mothers, fathers, grandmothers, teachers, drivers, traders, the watchman.
• **Neighbours** are the people who live beside us; **friends** are the people we play with. A neighbour is not always a relation, but we still greet him.
• People who help us: **the nurse** (she treats the sick), **the policeman** (he keeps us from harm), **the driver** (he carries us), **the trader** (she sells food), **the teacher** (she teaches us), **the cleaner** (he sweeps the street).
• Some people help with their hands, some with a machine, some with words — all of them are needed.
• Nobody can do everything alone: the man who plants the rice is not the man who drives it to town.
• Living near people means: greet in the morning, do not throw dirt into another man's gutter, share what you have, and keep noise down when a baby is sleeping.
• Role-play of the week: carry a basket to the neighbour's door, ask for a cup, say thank you.

**Words for my notebook**

A community is the people and places around us. Neighbours, friends and helpers all belong to it.

**Let us talk**

1. What is a community?
2. Name two people who live near you.
3. Who treats people when they are sick?
