4. Who keeps the street safe?
5. Say two things we do to live well with neighbours.

**Worksheet**

1. A community is made up of A) only motor cars B) only children C) only houses D) people and places around us
2. The person who sells us food is a A) nurse B) trader C) teacher D) pilot
3. Who keeps the community safe? A) the goat B) the baker C) the policeman D) the baby
4. Our neighbours are the people who A) live beside us B) sleep C) teach in school D) live far away
5. Fill in: The person who sweeps our street is the ____.
6. Mention three people who help us in the community.
7. Write two sentences on what you do when you meet your neighbour in the morning.

**My own work**

Draw three people who help people in your community and write one thing each of them does.

---

### WEEK 4 — PEOPLES AND PLACES IN THE COMMUNITY (PART 2)

*Depth: NERDC Primary 1 · Nigerian History · First Term W4 (pp 649–650).*

**You will learn to**

• name the three major ethnic groups of Nigeria
• say one thing each group is known for
• name other groups living in our community
• say how we treat people of other groups

**Things to know**

• Nigeria has many peoples. The three **major ethnic groups** are the **Hausa**, the **Igbo** and the **Yoruba**.
• The **Hausa** are mainly in the **north**; they greet with **"Sannu"**; they eat **tuwo** with soup; the big embroidered robe is the **babban riga**.
• The **Igbo** are in the **south-east**; they greet with **"Ndewo"**; they eat **fufu** with soup; some men wear a red cap.
• The **Yoruba** are in the **south-west**; they greet with **"Ẹ̀ káàárọ̀"**; they eat **amala** with **ewédú** soup; their cloth is **àdìrè**, and a chief wears **agbádá**.
• Other groups in Nigeria: **Nupe, Tiv, Kanuri, Ijaw, Efik, Fulani, Igala, Ibibio, Edo, Urhobo** — some of them live in our own community.
• What we learn from this: **different is not wrong**. Another people's food is not a laughing matter; another people's greeting is not a foolish thing.
• We **respect** people of every group: we greet in their own language when we can, and we do not call anybody's tribe names.
• The line of the week, said three times: **"We are all Nigerians."**

**Words for my notebook**

Hausa, Igbo and Yoruba are the three big groups. There are many others. Different tongues, one country — we are all Nigerians.

**Let us talk**

1. Name the three major ethnic groups of Nigeria.
2. Which greeting belongs to the Hausa people?
3. Mention one food and one cloth of the Yoruba.
4. Name one other ethnic group you know.
5. What should we never do to another group's language?

**Worksheet**

1. Which of these is a major ethnic group of Nigeria? A) Toyota B) Naira C) Lagos D) Hausa
2. "Sannu" is the greeting of the A) Tiv B) Hausa C) Igbo D) Yoruba
3. Amala and ewédú are food of the A) Kanuri B) Hausa C) Ijaw D) Yoruba
4. The three big groups live in A) different parts of Nigeria B) another country C) the bush only D) one town only
5. Fill in: "Ẹ̀ káàárọ̀" is a greeting in the ____ language.
6. Name three other ethnic groups in Nigeria.
7. Write two sentences on how we treat people of another group.

**My own work**

Write the names of the three groups and draw one food that any of them eats.

---

### WEEK 5 — PLACES IN THE COMMUNITY (PART 1)

*Depth: NERDC Primary 1 · Nigerian History · First Term W7 (pp 649–650).*

**You will learn to**

• name the important places in our community
• say what each place is used for
• say how we behave in each place
• tell where help is found in an emergency

**Things to know**

• Important places: **school, hospital, market, church, mosque, police station, fire station, park and playground**, the motor park, the bank, the post office.
• The **school** is where we learn; we come on time and we carry our books.
• The **market** is where food and things are bought; we hold our money well and we say "thank you" to the seller.
• The **hospital** is where the sick are treated; we speak softly and we sit down to wait for our turn.
• **Church** and **mosque** are places of prayer: we go in quietly, we do not run about, and we respect the people worshipping whatever our own family's place is.
• The **police station** is for a thief, for a lost child, for any danger; the **fire station** is for fire. Both work at night as well as in the day.
• The **park and playground** are for games and rest: we take turns and we do not push the smaller children.
• If a grown-up is not around and something is wrong, a child can go to the police station — help is kept there for everybody.

**Words for my notebook**

School for learning, market for buying, hospital for sickness, church and mosque for prayer, police and fire station for help.

**Let us talk**

1. Where do we buy tomatoes and fish?
2. Where do we go when somebody is sick?
3. Whom do we call when a house is on fire?
4. How should we behave in a place of prayer?
5. What do we do on the playground when two children want one swing?

**Worksheet**

1. We buy food and things at the A) police station B) mosque C) market D) hospital
2. A house on fire needs the A) tailor B) park C) library D) fire station
3. A lost child should be taken to the A) police station B) barber C) market D) playground
4. In the hospital we must A) climb the chairs B) run about C) shout D) speak softly and wait our turn
5. Fill in: We learn our books in the ____.
6. Name four places in your community and what each is used for.
7. Write two sentences on how you behave in a church or a mosque.

**My own work**

Draw two places in your community and write what each one is used for.

---

### WEEK 6 — PLACES IN THE COMMUNITY (PART 2)

*Depth: NERDC Primary 1 · Nigerian History · First Term W8 (pp 649–650).*

**You will learn to**

• name the traditional places in our community
• say who rules in the palace and what he does
• say what the community hall is used for
• describe one old thing in our area

**Things to know**

• Besides the hospital and the market, every town has **old, traditional places**: the **palace**, the **community hall**, and sometimes a **monument**.
• The **palace** is the home of the **traditional ruler**. In Yorubaland he is the **Oba** (in some towns the Aláàfin, the Deji, the Olú); in the north he is the **Emir**; in Igboland he may be the **Igwe** or the eldest man of the kindred.
• The ruler wears **beads and a crown**, sits on a **stool**, gives titles, and settles quarrels in his palace. He is honoured, and he listens to the chiefs with him.
• The **community hall** is where the whole town meets: to plan the water, to fix the broken road, to arrange the festival, to congratulate a child who got a scholarship.
• A **monument** is something old that people keep because it tells the story of the place: an old bridge, a wall, a tree where the first settlers met, the site of the first school.
• Picture study of the week: the class looked at the pictures and said — What is this? Where is it? Is it old or new? Who uses it?
• If our town has an old place near us, a short walk to see it is a field trip — the safety rules of Basic Science apply.
• These places belong to **everybody**; that is why we do not write on their walls or break anything there.

**Words for my notebook**

The palace of the ruler, the community hall of the town, and the monuments of the past are our traditional places.

**Let us talk**

1. Where does a traditional ruler live?
2. Name the ruler's title in the north of Nigeria and in Yorubaland.
3. What happens in the community hall?
4. What is a monument?
5. Who do the old places of a town belong to?

**Worksheet**

1. The home of a traditional ruler is the A) garage B) market C) clinic D) palace
2. In the north the traditional ruler is the A) Emir B) Igwe C) Deji D) Oba
3. Town matters are discussed at the A) shed B) community hall C) bus park D) kitchen
4. An old building kept to tell the story of a town is a A) monument B) gutter C) market D) fence
5. Fill in: The ruler settles quarrels in his ____.
6. Name the ruler's title among the Yoruba, the Hausa and the Igbo.
7. Write two sentences on an old thing in your town and why it is kept.

**My own work**

Draw the palace or the community hall and write one line about what happens there.

---

### WEEK 8 — LIVING TOGETHER IN PEACE

*Depth: NERDC Primary 1 · Nigerian History · First Term W9 (pp 649–650).*

**You will learn to**

• say what tolerance means
• name ways of respecting people of other groups
• say why we play and learn together
• give examples of sharing and caring

**Things to know**

• **Tolerance** means: other people are different from me, and that is all right.
• Different things about people: their name, their language, their food, their cloth, their place of worship, the way they greet.
• A child with tolerance does four things: he **does not mock another child's name**, he **does not say another person's god is wrong**, he **is ready to sit with anybody**, and he **learns to greet in another language**.
• Playing together teaches more than any lesson: on the ball field the big boy must pass to the small one, or there is no game.
• Learning together: when one child knows the answer and another does not, the one who knows helps, and both are better for it.
• **Sharing and caring**: share the biscuit, share the crayons; help the child that fell; speak to the child who is sitting alone.
• We are **stronger together**: one stick breaks in the hand, a bundle of sticks does not; one thread is not a rope, many threads are.
• If a quarrel starts, the peaceful child walks away and tells a grown-up; he does not add to the noise.
• The sentence of the week: **"What I want for myself, I also want for you."**

**Words for my notebook**

Tolerance is respecting people who are different. We play together, learn together, share and care. We are stronger together.

**Let us talk**

1. What is tolerance?
2. Mention two things a tolerant child does not do.
3. What happened when the class drew on one sheet of paper?
4. Name two things you can share at school.
5. What do you do when two friends begin to quarrel?

**Worksheet**

1. Tolerance means A) mocking names B) staying alone always C) respecting people who are different D) fighting
2. When a friend falls we should A) laugh B) run away C) help him D) hit him
3. With one ball in class we should A) take it home B) fight for it C) take turns D) hide it
4. A bundle of sticks is hard to break because we are A) alone B) weaker together C) stronger together D) sleepy
5. Fill in: What I want for myself, I also want for ____.
6. Mention four ways of showing respect to people of other groups.
7. Write two sentences on a time you shared something and how it felt.

**My own work**

Write three ways you will show tolerance in this class next week.

---

### WEEK 9 — NATIONAL UNITY AND DIVERSITY

*Depth: NERDC Primary 1 · Nigerian History · First Term W10 (pp 649–650).*

**You will learn to**

• say that Nigeria is one country with many peoples
• name the differences among Nigerians
• describe how our diversity is celebrated
• identify the Nigerian flag and say what it represents

**Things to know**

• **Nigeria is one country with many peoples** — more than **two hundred and fifty** groups live inside it.
• Our differences: **languages** (Hausa, Igbo, Yoruba, Tiv, Nupe, Ijaw and the rest), **foods** (garri, tuwo, fufu, eba, rice), **cloths** (agbádá, babban riga, George wrapper, àdìrè), and **festivals**.
• Festivals we know: the **Durbar** in the north (horses and colour), **Eyo** in Lagos (white cloth and beads), the **New Yam** festival in the south-east (the first yam of the season), the **Argo/masquerade** days in many towns.
• At a festival, **everybody is invited**; food is given to the stranger as well as to the family. That is how diversity is celebrated, not hidden.
• The language we all use to speak to one another in school is **English**, while each child keeps his own language at home — a Nigerian can hold two or three languages.
• The **Nigerian flag** is **two green strips with one white strip in the middle**: **green for the land and its green things**, **white for peace and unity**.
• We stand and sing the **national anthem**; the flag is saluted in the morning assembly.
• The sentence of the week, said by the whole class: **"Different people, one Nigeria."**

**Words for my notebook**

Nigeria has more than 250 peoples. Different tongues, foods and festivals; one flag, one country. Different people, one Nigeria.

**Let us talk**

1. About how many groups of people live in Nigeria?
2. Name three languages you have heard this term.
3. What do the colours of our flag stand for?
4. Mention one festival and where it is held.
5. Say the sentence of the week.

**Worksheet**

1. Nigeria has about how many groups of people? A) three B) ten C) one D) more than 250
2. The strip in the middle of our flag is A) green B) white C) blue D) red
3. The green colour on the flag stands for A) milk B) the land C) the sky D) the river
4. The language used in our school to join all groups is A) English B) Arabic C) Hausa D) Yoruba
5. Fill in: The Durbar festival is held mainly in the ____ of Nigeria.
6. Mention three ways Nigerians are different and two things that make us one.
7. Write two sentences on a festival you have seen or heard of.

**My own work**

Colour the Nigerian flag correctly — two green strips, one white in the middle — and write "one Nigeria" under it.


---

# Physical and Health Education (PHE)

Weeks 1 to 6, 8 and 9. No note for Week 7 (Mid-Term), 10 (Revision), 11 (Examination).
*Depth: NERDC 2025 · Nursery 3 · Physical and Health Education · pp 547–557.*


### WEEK 1 — EXERCISES AND GAMES: IMPORTANCE

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · PHE · First Term W1 (pp 547–551).*

**You will learn to**

• say what exercise means and what a game is
• name the things exercise does for my body
• tell why games have rules
• show that exercise is fun, not a punishment

**Things to know**

• **Exercise** means the physical activities that make the body move and work.
• A **game** is an organized activity with rules, usually played for fun and sometimes to win.
• Exercise and games keep the body strong and healthy and help me grow properly.
• They develop my muscles and bones, and they make my body bend and balance better.
• They give me energy, and they help my heart and my lungs work well.
• Playing keeps me from becoming too heavy (obesity).
• A child who plays sleeps better at night and wakes up fresh.
• Games teach discipline and focus: I wait for my turn, I listen for the whistle, I remember the rule.
• Team games teach me to play with others, and my eyes and hands learn to work together.
• Every game has rules, and the rules make the game fair for everybody.

**Words for my notebook**

Exercise makes the body move. Games have rules. Both keep me strong, quick and happy.

**Let us talk**

1. What is exercise?
2. Mention three things exercise does for the body.
3. Why does a game need rules?
4. Say one game you love to play.

**Worksheet**

1. Exercise means activities that make the body A) move and work B) sleep C) grow cold D) rest
2. A game is played with A) rules B) money C) no rule D) books only
3. Which one does exercise NOT do for us? A) spoil our teeth B) give energy C) strengthen muscles D) improve balance
4. Playing games at the right time helps us to A) fight B) skip food C) stay awake all night D) sleep well
5. Fill in: Exercise should be ____, not a punishment.
6. Mention four benefits of exercise and games.
7. Write two sentences on the game you play best and its rule.

**My own work**

Do ten jumping jacks, ten arm circles and ten toe touches, then tick the box your classmate draws for you.

Draw yourself playing your favourite game and colour the ball.

---

### WEEK 2 — GAMES: BASIC GAMES

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · PHE · First Term W2 (pp 547–551).*

**You will learn to**

• play the basic games of the field
• keep the balance in the egg-and-spoon race
• follow the rules of each game
• play fairly and encourage the others

**Things to know**

• **Egg and spoon race:** a small ball or bean bag is balanced on a spoon and carried to the line; if it falls, you pick it up and go on from where it fell.
• The egg-and-spoon game teaches balance and control of the hand.
• **Sack race:** you stand inside a sack and jump to the finish line; it trains the legs and the sense of balance.
• **Blindfold and fill a basket:** a player is blindfolded and, guided by the voices of teammates, picks objects up and puts them in a basket.
• The blindfold game teaches trust, listening and knowing where things are around you.
• **Three-legged race:** the inside legs of two players are tied together, and the pair must walk as one.
• Other basic games: **musical chairs, freeze dance and the obstacle course** (run, crawl under, step over, jump across).
• Fair play means: no pushing, no cheating, no taking another's place.
• We take turns, and when a friend loses we say "well played" instead of laughing.

**Words for my notebook**

Balance on the spoon, jump in the sack, listen with the eyes shut. Play fairly and clap for the others.

**Let us talk**

1. Which game uses a spoon and a ball?
2. What does the sack race train?
3. Who guides the blindfolded player?
4. Say two things fair players do.

**Worksheet**

1. In the egg and spoon race, if the ball falls you A) cry B) hit your friend C) pick it up and carry on D) go home
2. The sack race is played by A) running with a bucket B) jumping in a sack C) throwing a ball D) sitting down
3. Musical chairs is a game of A) moving when the music plays B) reading C) swimming D) writing
4. Fair play means A) hiding the ball B) pushing the small ones C) not cheating D) taking another's turn
5. Fill in: In the three-legged race, two players must walk as ____.
6. Mention three games you played today.
7. Write two sentences on one rule that makes a game fair.

**My own work**

Draw one of the games and write the rule that you must not forget.

---

### WEEK 3 — BODY EXERCISE: STRETCHING, BENDING, RAISING ARMS AND LEGS, SKIPPING, LEAPING AND HOPPING

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · PHE · First Term W3 and W4 (pp 547–551) — the school takes both weeks together.*

**You will learn to**

• stretch the neck, arms, back and legs
• bend and raise the arms and legs in the right form
• skip with and without a rope
• leap and hop without falling

**Things to know**

• **Stretching**: reach for the sky, touch your toes, bend sideways, roll the neck slowly, turn the arms in circles, turn the ankles.
• Stretching warms the muscles up, keeps us from being hurt, and makes the body bend easily.
• **Bending**: bend forward, bend backward carefully, bend sideways, and bend the knees (squat) like a frog.
• **Raising arms and legs**: arm raise to the front, to the side, up over the head; leg raise marching, to the side, and behind.
• **Skipping**: jump with a rope forwards, jump backwards, or skip without a rope and count the skips — 1, 2, 3… to 20.
• Skipping makes the legs strong, the heart quick, and the whole body well-coordinated.
• **Leaping** is a big jump from one foot to the other foot; arms swing to help; we leap over a line or a cone.
• **Hopping** is on one foot only — five hops on the right leg, five on the left.
• Form and safety come first: land with soft knees, keep space between you and your neighbour, and never push.

**Words for my notebook**

Stretch, bend, raise, skip, leap and hop. Warm up first, land with bent knees, and keep your space.

**Let us talk**

1. Why do we stretch before exercise?
2. Show me a squat and a side bend.
3. What is the difference between leaping and hopping?
4. Count twenty skips out loud.

**Worksheet**

1. Stretching before exercise mainly A) hurts the legs B) cools the body C) warms the muscles up D) makes us tired
2. A leap is a jump A) from one foot to the other B) in bed C) with eyes shut D) on one foot
3. Hopping is done on A) one foot B) the knees C) both hands D) the back
4. Which one is NOT a stretching action? A) ankle rotation B) touching the toes C) sleeping D) arm circles
5. Fill in: We land from a jump with soft, bent ____.
6. Name the four body exercises in this lesson.
7. Write two sentences on the exercise you found hardest.

**My own work**

Do ten stretches and twenty skips at home; write down how many skips you managed without stopping.

Draw one child stretching up tall and one bending down; colour the shoes of both.

---

### WEEK 4 — BODY CONDITIONING AND BALANCING

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · PHE · First Term W7 (pp 547–551).*

**You will learn to**

• say what body conditioning means
• say what balance is
• stand on one leg for ten counts
• play the dance-and-freeze game well

**Things to know**

• **Body conditioning** means exercises that make the whole body fit and in good control.
• **Balance** means staying steady without falling.
• Stand on the right leg and count to ten; then the left leg; then, when you are strong, try with your eyes shut.
• Arms are stretched out sideways while balancing — they act like the beam of a scale.
• Balancing strengthens the small muscles that keep us upright, and it teaches the mind to focus.
• **Dancing and freezing game**: dance while the music (or a drum) plays; when it stops, freeze like a statue and hold that position.
• Holding the freeze tests balance, teaches rhythm and trains the ears to listen.
• Other balancing activities: walk heel to toe on a line drawn on the floor, stand on tiptoe, walk along a low, safe beam.
• Balance falls when the eyes are shut, when the head moves about, or when somebody pushes — so we try again quietly.

**Words for my notebook**

Conditioning keeps the body fit; balance keeps us steady. Arms out, eyes fixed, count to ten.

**Let us talk**

1. What is balance?
2. What do the arms do when we stand on one leg?
3. When do we freeze in the dancing game?
4. Name one other balancing activity.

**Worksheet**

1. Balance means staying A) fast B) loud C) asleep D) steady without falling
2. In the freezing game we hold our position when the music A) is too loud B) is soft C) stops D) starts
3. Which of these helps us balance? A) running about B) eyes shut tight C) arms out sideways D) jumping high
4. Walking heel to toe is a game of A) writing B) balance C) singing D) swimming
5. Fill in: Body conditioning keeps the whole body ____ and in good control.
6. Mention three balancing activities.
7. Write two sentences on how long you can stand on one leg with your eyes shut.

**My own work**

Draw the mark of ten on the floor and walk heel to toe along it five times without stepping off.

---

### WEEK 5 — SIMPLE MOVEMENTS OF PARTS OF THE BODY

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · PHE · First Term W8 (pp 547–551).*

**You will learn to**

• move to the beat of a tambourine
• walk, jog, run and crawl correctly
• bend, turn, jump and swing the body parts
• move safely in a space shared with others

**Things to know**

• **Tambourine movement**: move when it plays; move **fast** for fast beats, **slow** for slow beats; **freeze** when it stops.
• Different body parts answer the beat: the hands clap, the feet stamp, the head nods, the trunk twists.
• **Walking** has many forms: normal walk, fast walk, slow walk, on tiptoe, on the heels, and walking backwards.
• **Jogging** is light running — in one place or moving along.
• **Running** is faster: knees lift, arms pump at the elbow, eyes ahead.
• **Bending** is at the waist or at the knees, forwards, backwards and sideways.
• **Turning, spinning, jumping and crawling** are also basic movements; crawling is done on hands and knees under a line.
• Fundamental movement patterns are the foundation of every sport: without them a child cannot kick, throw or catch well.
• Moving safely in a shared space: keep your own spot, do not run into a neighbour, stop when the whistle blows.

**Words for my notebook**

Fast, slow, freeze — that is the tambourine. Walk, jog, run, bend, turn, jump, crawl. Keep your space.

**Let us talk**

1. What do you do when the tambourine stops?
2. Show me walking on the heels and on tiptoe.
3. What makes running faster than walking?
4. Name three movements you do with the trunk.

**Worksheet**

1. When the tambourine plays quickly we move A) backwards only B) slowly C) not at all D) quickly
2. Light running is called A) skipping B) sleeping C) crawling D) jogging
3. Walking on the heels is a way of practising A) eating B) reading C) writing D) balance
4. Which one is NOT a basic movement? A) bending B) brushing C) crawling D) spinning
5. Fill in: In a shared space we keep our own ____ and stop at the whistle.
6. Name four ways of walking.
7. Write two sentences on the movement you can do best.

**My own work**

Set out six bottles in a line and crawl or step through them twice, then write how many you touched.

Draw the five parts of your body you move with and colour each one a different colour.

---

### WEEK 6 — MANIPULATIVE MOVEMENTS: THROWING AND CATCHING

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · PHE · First Term W9 (pp 547–551).*

**You will learn to**

• say what manipulative movement means
• throw underhand and overhand
• catch with two hands at different heights
• keep the safety rules when throwing

**Things to know**

• **Manipulative movements** are movements that control and move an object using a body part.
• They are the skills every sport is built on: throwing, catching, kicking, hitting, bouncing, rolling.
• **Underhand throw**: the arm swings from behind and low, the object leaves the hand in front of the body.
• **Overhand throw**: the arm goes up behind the head, the body turns sideways, and we **step and throw**.
• We can throw at a **target** (a hoop on the wall), for **distance** (as far as we can), or into a partner's hands.
• **Catching** is done with **two hands**, arms out in the ready position, eyes on the object all the way to the hands.
• Catch a bean bag first, then a soft ball; catch high throws and low throws separately.
• With a partner we throw and catch, and slowly move a step further apart after each ten good catches.
• Safety: only soft objects are used for throwing in class; we never throw at a person's face, and we look before we throw.

**Words for my notebook**

Throw underhand or overhand; step and throw. Catch with two hands, eyes on the ball. Soft things only.

**Let us talk**

1. What is a manipulative movement?
2. Show me the ready position for catching.
3. What do we do with our feet when we throw far?
4. Name one throwing safety rule.

**Worksheet**

1. Controlling and moving an object with the body is a A) manipulative movement B) song C) stretch D) balancing skill
2. An overhand throw is made with the arm A) in water B) up behind the head C) low D) behind the back
3. We catch mainly with A) one hand B) two hands C) the knee D) the head
4. Which object is safe to throw in class? A) a bottle B) a shoe with a rock C) a stone D) a bean bag
5. Fill in: We never throw at a person's ____.
6. Name three manipulative skills.
7. Write two sentences on catching: what do the eyes and the hands do?

**My own work**

Throw and catch a soft ball twenty times with a brother, sister or friend, and count the catches you made.

Draw a ball in the air above your two hands and colour the ball.

---

### WEEK 8 — NEURO-MUSCULAR SKILLS: DANCING AND SOMERSAULTING

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · PHE · First Term W10 (dancing) and Nursery 2 · PHE · Second Term W10 (somersaulting), pp 547–551.*

**You will learn to**

• say what a neuro-muscular skill is
• do simple dance steps with the arms and legs together
• roll forward on a mat with the chin tucked
• say why the roll is done only with supervision

**Things to know**

• **Neuro-muscular** means the working together of the nerves (the brain and its wires) and the muscles to produce movement.
• A skill of this kind needs timing: the brain says go, the muscles obey, and the body obeys at the right moment.
• **Dancing** is moving the body to music or rhythm: step left, step right, clap, jump, turn.
• Simple dance steps are done in a routine — the same order again and again so the body remembers it.
• Cultural dances (simple ones) teach us where our people come from, and the whole class joins in.
• Dancing trains rhythm, timing and the coordination of the arms and the legs, and it is fun exercise.
• **Somersaulting (forward roll)**: from a squat, hands on the mat, **tuck the chin to the chest**, push with the legs and roll on the rounded back.
• The roll is done **only on a mat or soft ground, with a grown-up watching** — never on a hard floor, never on a bed, never on a friend.
• A child who is not ready does not roll: that is alright. Confidence is built one small step at a time.

**Words for my notebook**

Neuro-muscular = brain and muscles working together. Dance to the beat. Roll only on a mat, chin tucked, with a grown-up.

**Let us talk**

1. What does neuro-muscular mean?
2. Show me two dance steps in order.
3. Where should the chin be during a forward roll?
4. Why must an adult watch the roll?

**Worksheet**

1. A neuro-muscular skill joins the A) brain and the muscles B) shoe and the leg C) nose and the ear D) two hands
2. A dance routine is A) one long jump B) the same steps in order C) a food D) a song about books
3. In a forward roll the chin must be A) up B) in the air C) tucked to the chest D) sideways
4. The roll must be done on A) a mat or soft ground B) a hard floor C) a bed D) the staircase
5. Fill in: If a child is not ready to roll, that is ____.
6. Name two things dancing does for the body.
7. Write two sentences on how a forward roll is done safely.

**My own work**

Set out four dance steps in order, name them, and show them to your class tomorrow.

Draw the pose you dance in and colour the cloth you dance in.

---

### WEEK 9 — DEVELOPMENT THROUGH SPORT ACTIVITIES: PHYSICAL DEVELOPMENT

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · PHE · Second Term W1 (pp 552–557).*

**You will learn to**

• name the parts of the body that sport develops
• say what flexibility, balance and speed mean
• tell which activity builds which quality
• say what a child of my age can do

**Things to know**

• Sports and physical activity help the **whole body develop**, not only the legs.
• **Flexibility** is the ability to bend and stretch easily; it is improved by stretching exercises.
• **Agility** is the ability to move quickly and change direction easily; games and drills build it.
• **Balance** is staying steady without falling; balancing exercises (one leg, the line, the beam) improve it.
• **Coordination** is using different parts of the body together smoothly — eyes and hands, arms and legs.
• **Reaction** is answering a signal quickly: ear hears the clap, leg starts to run.
• **Speed** is how fast we move; running activities develop it.
• **Strength** is how strong the muscles are; exercise and play build it.
• Regular sport develops all of these together; one game can train three or four of them.
• At my age I am learning these things, not competing as a grown-up athlete; my bones and muscles are still growing, so rest is part of the plan.

**Words for my notebook**

Sport builds flexibility, agility, balance, coordination, reaction, speed and strength — at my own age, with rest.

**Let us talk**

1. What is flexibility?
2. Which quality tells you how fast you move?
3. Name one activity that builds balance.
4. Why do children also need rest?

**Worksheet**

1. Being able to bend and stretch easily is A) flexibility B) reaction C) speed D) strength
2. Changing direction quickly in a game is A) reading B) balance C) sleep D) agility
3. Which one is NOT developed by sport? A) speed B) coordination C) strength D) taller books
4. The ability to stay steady is A) speed B) balance C) reaction D) flexibility
5. Fill in: ____ is answering a signal quickly.
6. Mention five qualities sport builds in the body.
7. Write two sentences on the sport that trains your balance best.

**My own work**

Write the five qualities you are strongest in, and pick one you want to improve by next term.

Draw the ground you play on and colour the line you run along.


---

# Prevocational Studies (PVS)

Weeks 1 to 6, 8 and 9. No note for Week 7 (Mid-Term), 10 (Revision), 11 (Examination).
*Depth: NERDC 2025 · Primary 4 · Prevocational Studies · First Term W1–W2, p 833 — this subject is
redistributed into the lower classes, so the Primary 4 week on the concept of agriculture is the floor,
told here with things a 4–5 year-old can see, touch and name.*


### WEEK 1 — MEANING OF AGRICULTURE; IMPORTANCE OF AGRICULTURE TO HUMANS

*Depth: NERDC Primary 4 · Prevocational Studies · First Term W1–W2 (p 833).*

**You will learn to**

• say what agriculture means in one line
• name the things agriculture gives us
• tell who a farmer is
• say why every person needs agriculture

**Things to know**

• **Agriculture** is the work of growing crops and rearing animals for man to use.
• A person who does this work is a **farmer**.
• Agriculture gives us the food we eat: yam, rice, beans, maize, plantain, vegetables and fruit.
• It gives us animals for food: eggs and meat from the hen, milk from the cow, meat from the goat and the sheep.
• It gives us cotton for cloth, wood for furniture, and leaves and roots some people use as medicine.
• Without agriculture there would be no food on the table and no raw material for the factory.
• Farmers sell their surplus at the market and use the money for school fees, clothes and houses.
• Many people in Nigeria earn their living from agriculture.
• Agriculture is important to every one of us, whether we live in the town or in the village.

**Words for my notebook**

Agriculture is growing crops and rearing animals. It gives us food, clothes, work and money.

**Let us talk**

1. What is agriculture?
2. Who is a farmer?
3. Mention three things agriculture gives us.
4. Why does a town child also need agriculture?

**Worksheet**

1. Agriculture is the work of A) driving taxis B) selling cloth only C) growing crops and rearing animals D) building houses
2. A person who grows food for us is a A) banker B) driver C) farmer D) pilot
3. Which of these comes from agriculture? A) plastic chair B) radio C) yam D) bicycle
4. Cotton for our clothes comes from A) the mine B) a machine C) a crop D) the river
5. Fill in: The cow gives us beef and ____.
6. Mention four things we get from agriculture.
7. Write two sentences on why agriculture is important to your family.

**My own work**

Write the names of five foods in your house and say which of them came from a farm.

Draw a hoe, a cutlass and a basket, then colour the basket.

---

### WEEK 2 — AGRICULTURE IN OUR DAILY LIVES

*Depth: NERDC Primary 4 · Prevocational Studies · First Term W1–W2 (p 833).*

**You will learn to**

• trace a meal from the farm to the table
• name farm foods taken at breakfast, lunch and dinner
• say which farm things are used at home besides food
• tell why we must not waste farm produce

**Things to know**

• The swallow we eat — rice, yam flour, beans, garri — all came from a farm.
• The oil in our soup came from the palm fruit; the groundnut in our snack came from the ground.
• Fresh fish and catfish come from the river and the fish pond; milk and eggs come from animals kept at home.
• Fruit that I eat between meals — mango, orange, watermelon, banana — grew on a tree or a creeper.
• Farm things are not only for eating: broom from the midrib, mats from raffia, wood for the chair, and the cow's skin for shoes.
• A market day is where farm produce comes together: the seller's basket holds what the farmer's hoe made.
• Food that is wasted was grown with rain, sweat and time; we finish what we take and we keep the rest for later.
• If there is no farm work, the shop has nothing to sell and the table has nothing to carry.

**Words for my notebook**

From the farm to my plate: rice, oil, fish, milk, fruit. What the farm gives, we do not throw away.

**Let us talk**

1. Name three foods you ate today that came from a farm.
2. Where does palm oil come from?
3. Mention one farm thing used at home that is not food.
4. Why must we not waste food?

**Worksheet**

1. Garri is made from A) stone B) milk C) fish D) cassava
2. Palm oil used for our soup comes from A) the palm fruit B) the river C) the shop D) the mine
3. Which of these is NOT from a farm? A) table salt from the sea B) yam C) egg D) rice
4. Raffia mats are made from A) sand B) plastic C) iron D) a farm plant
5. Fill in: The market seller sells what the ____ has grown.
6. Mention four farm foods you eat at home.
7. Write two sentences on one kind of waste we should stop in our food.

**My own work**

Draw your last meal and write under each item where on the farm it came from.

---

### WEEK 3 — BASIC FARMING ACTIVITIES

*Depth: NERDC Primary 4 · Prevocational Studies · First Term W1–W2 (p 833).*

**You will learn to**

• put the farming activities in order
• name the tools used at each stage
• say how crops are cared for
• tell what happens at harvest time

**Things to know**

• Farming has an order, and every stage waits for the one before it.
• **Clearing**: the bush and old sticks are removed from the land with a cutlass.
• **Tilling**: the soil is turned and broken with a hoe so that it becomes soft for planting.
• **Planting**: seeds, yam setts, cassava sticks or cocoyam are put into the ground at the right time of the year.
• **Weeding and watering**: grass that competes with the crop is removed; where there is no rain, the crop is watered.
• **Applying manure**: rotten leaves and animal droppings are added to make the soil rich.
• **Protecting the crop**: birds, grasshoppers, goats and cattle are kept away from the growing farm.
• **Harvesting**: yam, maize, cassava and rice are gathered when they are ripe.
• **Storage and selling**: the harvest is dried, kept safe from weevils, eaten, or taken to the market.
• Simple tools of the small farm: cutlass, hoe, rake, watering can, knapsack sprayer, basket.

**Words for my notebook**

Clear, till, plant, weed, water, harvest, store, sell — the farmer's work goes in that order.

**Let us talk**

1. What is done before planting?
2. Which tool turns the soil?
3. Why do we weed the farm?
4. Name two things done after harvest.

**Worksheet**

1. The first farming activity is A) cooking B) clearing C) selling D) harvesting
2. Breaking and turning the soil is called A) shelling B) tilling C) milling D) weeding
3. Rotten leaves and animal droppings used to enrich the soil are A) cement B) manure C) chalk D) garri
4. Which tool is used for cutting bushes? A) cutlass B) rake C) spade D) hoe
5. Fill in: Young plants are watered when there is no ____.
6. Write the first four farming activities in order.
7. Write two sentences on what happens during harvest in your place.

**My own work**

Number these in your notebook: harvesting, planting, clearing, weeding, tilling.

Draw the four farm jobs in order — clear, plant, water, harvest — and colour the harvest.

---

### WEEK 4 — SCOPE OF AGRICULTURE: CROPS

*Depth: NERDC Primary 4 · Prevocational Studies · First Term W1–W2 (p 833).*

**You will learn to**

• say what crop production means
• group crops as food, cash and fruit crops
• name crops grown in our state
• say which part of a crop we eat

**Things to know**

• **Crop production** (crop farming) is the branch of agriculture that deals with growing plants for man and animals.
• **Food crops** are grown to feed the family: rice, yam, maize, cassava, beans, plantain, cocoyam.
• **Cash crops** are grown to be sold and to bring money: cocoa, groundnut, cotton, palm produce, rubber, ginger.
• **Fruit and vegetable crops**: mango, orange, watermelon, banana; tomatoes, pepper, spinach, ewédú, ugu.
• **Cereals** are grain crops — rice, maize, guinea corn, wheat; they are the staff of life.
• Some crops give us oil (palm, groundnut, sunflower) and some give us drink (cocoa, coffee, sugarcane).
• We eat different parts: the **root or tuber** (yam, cassava), the **grain** (maize, rice), the **leaf** (spinach), the **fruit** (mango).
• **Leguminous crops** such as beans and groundnut make the soil rich as they grow.
• Trees like iroko and mahogany are forestry; crops in the field are crop production — they are not the same branch.

**Words for my notebook**

Crops divide into food crops, cash crops, fruit and vegetable crops. Cereals are grains; beans make soil rich.

**Let us talk**

1. What is crop production?
2. Name three food crops and two cash crops.
3. Which part of the cassava plant do we eat?
4. Give one example of a cereal crop.

**Worksheet**

1. Crops grown mainly to bring money are called A) food crops B) weeds C) cash crops D) grasses
2. Which of these is a cereal crop? A) cassava B) palm C) banana D) maize
3. Cocoa and cotton are examples of A) cash crops B) tools C) fish D) food crops
4. The crop that makes the soil rich as it grows is A) plantain B) yam C) bean D) rice
5. Fill in: We eat the ____ of the yam plant (yam, cassava).
6. Mention three crops grown in our state.
7. Write two sentences on a fruit crop and the part of it we eat.

**My own work**

Bring one grain, one tuber and one leaf to school and write their names on a paper.

Draw four crops you know and colour the yam.

---

### WEEK 5 — SCOPE OF AGRICULTURE: ANIMALS

*Depth: NERDC Primary 4 · Prevocational Studies · First Term W1–W2 (p 833).*

**You will learn to**

• say what animal rearing means
• name farm animals and their young ones
• state what each animal gives us
• say how farm animals are cared for

**Things to know**

• **Animal rearing** (animal husbandry) is the branch of agriculture that deals with keeping farm animals.
• Farm animals: **cow, goat, sheep, pig, rabbit, dog, cat**, and the birds we keep — **hen, duck, turkey, guinea fowl**.
• The young of the cow is the **calf**; of the goat, the **kid**; of the sheep, the **lamb**; of the hen, the **chick**.
• The cow gives milk, meat and hide; the goat gives meat and skin; the hen gives eggs and meat.
• Animals also give **manure** for the farm and **strength** for ploughing and carrying loads.
• An animal needs four things every day: **food, clean water, shade, and a clean, safe place** to sleep.
• The animal is dosed and vaccinated when the vet or the health worker says so; sick animals are kept apart.
• Animals are not beaten; they are driven gently, and water is given to them in the hot season.
• Fish are kept in ponds and in the river — that part of agriculture is called fishery.

**Words for my notebook**

Animal rearing gives milk, eggs, meat and manure. Every animal needs food, water, shade and a safe place.

**Let us talk**

1. What is animal rearing?
2. What do we call the young one of a hen?
3. Mention two things the cow gives us.
4. Say three things an animal needs daily.

**Worksheet**

1. The young one of a goat is a A) chick B) kid C) calf D) lamb
2. The branch of agriculture that keeps animals is A) crop production B) animal rearing C) fishing D) forestry
3. Which animal is reared mainly for eggs? A) goat B) pig C) hen D) sheep
4. Manure from animals is used to A) enrich the soil B) boil water C) build a house D) thatch a roof
5. Fill in: A sick animal should be kept ____ from the others.
6. Name four farm animals and one product from each.
7. Write two sentences on how an animal is cared for in your home or in your village.

**My own work**

Draw a farm animal, its young one, and write two things it gives people.

---

### WEEK 6 — SCOPE OF AGRICULTURE: FORESTRY

*Depth: NERDC Primary 4 · Prevocational Studies · First Term W1–W2 (p 833).*

**You will learn to**

• say what forestry means
• name trees that give timber and trees that give fruit
• state the uses of trees
• say why we plant trees and do not cut them all

**Things to know**

• **Forestry** is the branch of agriculture that deals with the growing, management and protection of trees and the forest.
• **Timber trees** give us wood for planks, furniture and building: iroko, mahogany, teak, pine, obeche.
• **Fruit trees** give us food: mango, orange, guava, coconut, oil palm, cashew.
• Trees also give us medicine (neem leaves, bitter leaf roots), firewood, charcoal, and shelter from the sun.
• The forest is home to wild animals and birds: monkey, antelope, parrot, snail.
• Trees hold the soil with their roots, so the ground does not wash away when rain falls.
• Trees clean the air we breathe and bring the rain; where the trees are all cut, the land becomes dry.
• We cut only the trees that are marked, we cut a tree and **plant two**, and we do not burn bushes in harmattan.
• In Nigeria there are forest reserves, and there is a ministry that looks after them.

**Words for my notebook**

Forestry deals with trees: timber, fruit, medicine, shade. Cut one, plant two.

**Let us talk**

1. What is forestry?
2. Name two timber trees and two fruit trees.
3. What do the roots of trees do for the soil?
4. Why should we not burn bushes in the dry season?

**Worksheet**

1. Forestry is the branch of agriculture that deals with A) roads B) fish C) trees D) poultry
2. Iroko and mahogany are trees grown for A) timber B) fruit C) eggs D) milk
3. The part of the tree that keeps the soil in place is the A) leaf B) bark C) root D) flower
4. Which of these is a product of the forest? A) rubber B) garri C) milk D) egg
5. Fill in: When we cut a tree, we ____ two.
6. Mention four uses of trees.
7. Write two sentences on why the land becomes dry where trees have all been cut.

**My own work**

Water one tree or one plant near your house for five days and write what you noticed.

---

### WEEK 8 — SCOPE OF AGRICULTURE: FISHERY

*Depth: NERDC Primary 4 · Prevocational Studies · First Term W1–W2 (p 833).*

**You will learn to**

• say what fishery means
• name fish we eat and where they come from
• state the tools used in fishing
• say how fish are kept and prepared for market

**Things to know**

• **Fishery** is the branch of agriculture that deals with catching or rearing fish and other water animals.
• **Catch fishing** takes place in the river, the lake and the sea; **fish farming** (aquaculture) keeps fish in a pond or a cage.
• Fish we eat in Nigeria: **catfish, tilapia, titus/mackerel, croaker, bonga, sardine**; also prawn, crayfish and periwinkle.
• The tools of the fisherman: **net, hook and line, fishing rod, basket, trap, canoe and boat**.
• Fish are kept fresh with **salt and smoke or ice**; dried fish and smoked fish travel far to the market.
• Fish give us body-building food (protein); a child who eats fish grows strong.
• Fishing is done with rules: small fish are thrown back, so that there will be fish next year.
• Fish must not be taken from dirty water, and water for the pond must be kept clean.
• The river, the lake and the sea give work to fishermen, fish sellers and the women who smoke the fish.

**Words for my notebook**

Fishery is catching or rearing fish: net, hook, canoe, pond. Small fish thrown back keeps the water full.

**Let us talk**

1. What is fishery?
2. Name three fish we eat.
3. What tool does a fisherman use?
4. Why is a small fish thrown back into the water?

**Worksheet**

1. Fish farming in a pond is also called A) forestry B) tillage C) milling D) aquaculture
2. Which of these is a fishing tool? A) hoe B) net C) cutlass D) rake
3. Catfish and tilapia are A) animals of the bush B) crops C) birds D) fish
4. Fish are preserved by smoking, drying or using A) leaves B) sand C) ice D) salt water only
5. Fill in: The ____ is the fisherman's boat on the water.
6. Mention three water animals we eat besides fish.
7. Write two sentences on why we should not take fish from dirty water.

**My own work**

Write the name of the fish your family buys, and the place it came from.

Draw a fish, a net and a canoe, and colour the net.

---

### WEEK 9 — AGRICULTURE IN NIGERIA

*Depth: NERDC Primary 4 · Prevocational Studies · First Term W1–W2 (p 833) and Primary 6 ·
Prevocational Studies · Third Term W9 (p 952) for the sustainable-use lines.*

**You will learn to**

• name the crops Nigeria is known for
• say where farming is done in the country
• explain sustainable and organic farming in a child's words
• say what a pupil can do to protect the environment

**Things to know**

• Nigeria farms in every part of the country: **groundnut and cotton** in the north, **rice and maize** on the middle belt, **palm produce, cocoa and cassava** in the south, **fish** in the rivers of the south-south.
• Crops that bring money into Nigeria include **cocoa, groundnut, palm oil, rubber and cashew**.
• Most food in Nigeria comes from small family farms, worked with the hoe and the cutlass.
• **Sustainable agriculture** means farming in a way that the land will still be good for our children — not taking everything and leaving nothing.
• **Organic farming** uses compost (rotten leaves and dung) instead of only chemical fertiliser.
• **Reducing waste at home**: finish the food on the plate; use the peels for the compost; do not pour oil down the gutter.
• **Conserving resources**: water is used once well; trees are left standing; the bush is not burnt.
• **Protecting the environment** keeps the soil, the river and the air clean for farming and for us.
• A pupil's part: grow one plant, water a tree, keep the compound clean, and finish the food on the plate.

**Words for my notebook**

Nigeria farms groundnut, cocoa, palm produce, rice and fish. Sustainable farming leaves good land for the next child.

**Let us talk**

1. Name two crops of the northern part of Nigeria.
2. What does sustainable agriculture mean in your own words?
3. Mention two ways of reducing waste at home.
4. Say one thing you can do to protect the environment.

**Worksheet**

1. Cocoa and palm oil are products of A) the sea B) Nigeria C) the mine D) the desert
2. Farming that leaves the land good for the future is A) organic only B) wet farming C) lazy farming D) sustainable farming
3. Organic farming makes use of A) compost and manure B) plastic C) only water D) only chemicals
4. The best way to reduce waste at home is to A) burn everything B) finish our food and compost the peels C) throw waste in the gutter D) bury plastic in the yard
5. Fill in: We should not ____ the bush in the dry season.
6. Mention three crops grown in Nigeria and where they are grown.
7. Write two sentences on what you will do this week to protect your environment.

**My own work**

Plant one seed (orange seed, bean, or a fruit stone) in a tin, water it, and write its progress for five days.

Draw one food that came from a farm and colour it.


---

# Social and Citizenship Studies (SCS)

Weeks 1 to 6, 8 and 9. No note for Week 7 (Mid-Term), 10 (Revision), 11 (Examination).
*Depth: NERDC 2025 · Primary 1 · Social and Citizenship Studies · First Term W1–W10 and Second Term
W1–W2, pp 655–658 — taught here two bands earlier, so every idea is carried by naming and role-play.*


### WEEK 1 — LEADERSHIP

*Depth: NERDC Primary 1 · SCS · First Term W1 (pp 655–656).*

**You will learn to**

• say what a leader is in one line
• name the leaders in my life
• tell the qualities of a good leader
• say why we need leaders

**Things to know**

• A **leader** is somebody who guides others and shows them what to do.
• My leaders are: **father and mother** at home, **the teacher** in class, **the head teacher** in the school, and **the class captain** among the children.
• Outside: the **street leader (baale)**, the **chairman** of the local government, the **governor** of the state, and the **President** of the whole country.
• A good leader is **kind**, **fair**, **honest** and **helpful**.
• A good leader goes first in what is right, and does not do what he forbids others to do.
• We need leaders so that people will be **guided**, order will be **kept**, and work will be **finished**.
• A class without a monitor becomes noisy; a house without a head becomes untidy; a country without leaders has no direction.
• Leaders are given titles: chief, emir, oba, igwe, chairman, president, captain.
• We respect our leaders: we listen when they speak, we obey the rules they set, and we greet them.

**Words for my notebook**

A leader guides others. A good leader is kind, fair, honest and helpful. We listen to our leaders.

**Let us talk**

1. Who is a leader?
2. Name your four leaders at home and in school.
3. Mention three qualities of a good leader.
4. What happens in a class with no leader?

**Worksheet**

1. A leader is somebody who A) sleeps B) buys things C) runs away D) guides others
2. Which of these is a quality of a good leader? A) stealing B) rudeness C) fairness D) laziness
3. The leader of our class is the A) class captain B) driver C) head teacher D) trader
4. The leader of the whole country is the A) monitor B) baale C) president D) chairman
5. Fill in: A good leader is kind, fair, honest and ____.
6. Name four leaders you know.
7. Write two sentences on what a good leader does for people.

**My own work**

Write the names of the four leaders of your home and one thing each of them does.

Draw the person who leads in your class and colour the thing they hold.

---

### WEEK 2 — LEADERSHIP AT HOME

*Depth: NERDC Primary 1 · SCS · First Term W2 (pp 655–656).*

**You will learn to**

• say who leads in my home
• state what parents do for the family
• name other leaders in the family
• say how I can help at home

**Things to know**

• **Father and mother** are the leaders of the home; one of them carries the final decision.
• A parent's duties are to give **food, a place to live, clothes, education, love and protection**.
• **Grandparents** are leaders too: their advice is heavy, and we do not answer them back rudely.
• An **elder brother or sister** leads the smaller children when father and mother are not at home.
• Children help by **tidying their own things, sweeping, fetching light items, carrying messages, and looking after the babies**.
• We obey our family leaders: their rule is our safety — the way we greet, the time we return, the food we eat.
• A home where children help is a home that finishes its work early and rests in peace.
• Respect at home begins with small things: answering "yes ma/sir", carrying a bag, not slamming doors.

**Words for my notebook**

Father and mother lead the home. They feed, clothe, teach and protect us. I help and I obey.

**Let us talk**

1. Who are the leaders of your home?
2. Mention two things parents do for the family.
3. What can you carry or tidy at home?
4. Why should we obey the rules of our home?

**Worksheet**

1. The leaders of the home are the A) father and mother B) traders C) neighbours D) friends
2. Which of these is a duty of parents? A) buying toys only B) playing all day C) sleeping D) feeding the children
3. When parents are away, the smaller children are led by A) the dog B) an elder sibling C) a baby D) the visitor
4. Grandparents' advice is A) forbidden B) to be respected C) useless D) not needed
5. Fill in: Children can tidy their own ____ and fetch light items.
6. Mention three duties of a parent.
7. Write two sentences on one way you help in your house.

**My own work**

Tidy your own sleeping place and your box of books today, and write what you did.

Draw your own house and colour the door of the room where the family talks.

---

### WEEK 3 — COMMUNITY MOBILIZATION (PART 1)

*Depth: NERDC Primary 1 · SCS · First Term W3 (pp 655–656).*

**You will learn to**

• say what a community is
• name the kind of community I live in
• list the people who make up a community
• name the leaders of our community

**Things to know**

• A **community** is people living together in one area — a street, a village, a town.
• My community has **neighbours, shopkeepers, drivers, teachers, nurses, farmers and worshippers**.
• Community names differ: **village** is small, **town** is bigger, **city** is very big; a **estate** or **layout** is a planned community.
• Our community has a **name**; I can say it: "I live in ______."
• The leaders of the community are the **chief (oba, emir, igwe)**, the **baale** of the street, the **imam** of the mosque, the **pastor** of the church, the **mayor or chairman**.
• These leaders meet to plan for the people: a new borehole, a blocked gutter, a school, a market.
• **Mobilization** means calling the people together to do a work they must do — clean-up, protecting children, keeping the peace.
• Everybody belongs to a community, and nobody lives in a community alone.

**Words for my notebook**

A community is people living in one area. Its leaders plan for everybody; mobilizing means calling the people together.

**Let us talk**

1. What is a community?
2. What is the name of your community?
3. Name three people who live in a community.
4. Who are the leaders of our community?

**Worksheet**

1. A community is A) people living together in one area B) a school bag C) one house D) a market day
2. A bigger town is called a A) city B) hut C) farm D) village
3. The head of a village or town in Yorubaland may be called the A) monitor B) oba C) pilot D) trader
4. Mobilizing the people means A) sending them away B) closing the market C) sleeping D) calling them together for a work
5. Fill in: Every community has a ____, and I can say mine.
6. Name three leaders of your community.
7. Write two sentences on one problem your community can solve together.

**My own work**

Write the name of your street or village, and the names of two neighbours.

Draw five neighbours doing one job together and colour the tools they share.

---

### WEEK 4 — COMMUNITY MOBILIZATION (PART 2)

*Depth: NERDC Primary 1 · SCS · First Term W4 (pp 655–656).*

**You will learn to**

• say what cooperation means
• name community activities people do together
• state how communities help one another
• tell the children's part in the community

**Things to know**

• **Cooperation** means people working together to finish a job that one person cannot finish alone.
• Community activities: **clean-up exercise** on the last Saturday, **festivals**, **town hall meetings**, **building a bridge or a classroom**, **watering the school garden**.
• When somebody's house falls, the neighbours bring sand, blocks and hands — that is community support.
• At a naming ceremony, a wedding or a funeral, the whole street comes; joy and sorrow are shared.
• A strong community gives **safety** (watchmen, street light, neighbours who know one another), **unity** and **progress**.
• Our part as children: **greet elders, do not litter, keep the gutters free, help the old cross the road, do not fight on the street**.
• Work done together finishes quickly: "Many hands make the work light."
• A community that does not work together leaves its gutters blocked and its park in weeds.

**Words for my notebook**

Cooperation = working together. Clean-up, festivals and meetings are community work. Many hands make light work.

**Let us talk**

1. What is cooperation?
2. Mention three community activities.
3. How do neighbours help in times of need?
4. Name two things you can do for your community.

**Worksheet**

1. People working together is called A) fighting B) sleeping C) cooperation D) trading
2. A common clean-up day in a community is usually A) every morning B) on a fixed day C) at midnight D) never
3. A strong community gives its people A) dirt B) fear C) safety and unity D) quarrels
4. Which one is NOT a community activity? A) clean-up B) festival C) town meeting D) solitary quarrelling
5. Fill in: Many ____ make the work light.
6. Mention three ways communities help one another.
7. Write two sentences on one community work you have seen.

**My own work**

Pick one piece of litter in your street or compound today and put it in the bin; write what you saw afterwards.

Draw the place in your community where people meet and colour the roof.

---

### WEEK 5 — CIVIC RESPONSIBILITIES (PARTS 1 AND 2)

*Depth: NERDC Primary 1 · SCS · First Term W7 and W8 (pp 655–656) — both weeks in one.*

**You will learn to**

• say what a responsibility is
• list my duties at home and at school
• keep my belongings in order
• treat other people with respect

**Things to know**

• A **responsibility** is a thing we **must** do, not a thing we may choose to do.
• At home: cleaning my own room, washing my plate, putting away my clothes, helping with small chores.
• At school: coming on time, doing my homework, keeping the classroom clean, bringing my books, respecting the bell.
• Being responsible with my belongings: my bag, my books, my uniform and my shoes are kept in order.
• If I am not responsible, there is a consequence: no homework means a poor class work; a lost book must be replaced.
• Respecting **elders**: greet them, give them a seat, do not answer back.
• Respecting **people of my age**: no fighting, no bullying, no giving nicknames that pain.
• Respecting **younger ones**: do not push them, help them when they fall.
• Use the polite words: **"please", "thank you", "sorry", "excuse me"**.
• Take turns, share, and help those in need — a responsible child does not watch another suffer.

**Words for my notebook**

Responsibility is what I must do. I keep my things, do my work, respect elders and friends, and use polite words.

**Let us talk**

1. What is a responsibility?
2. Mention two duties of a pupil at school.
3. What is the consequence of not doing homework?
4. Say four polite words.

**Worksheet**

1. A responsibility is a thing we A) must do B) buy C) may ignore D) forget
2. Which of these is a duty at school? A) fighting B) doing homework C) coming late D) tearing books
3. Bullying other pupils is A) polite B) responsible C) wrong D) brave
4. What should we do with our books and bag? A) lose them B) keep them in order C) throw them D) hide them
5. Fill in: The four polite words are please, thank you, excuse me and ____.
6. Mention three responsibilities of a child at home.
7. Write two sentences on what happens when a child is not responsible.

**My own work**

Write your own morning list: three things you must do before coming to school, and tick them tomorrow.

Draw three things you do to keep your school clean and colour the one you do most.

---

### WEEK 6 — NATIONAL IDENTITY AND SYMBOLS (PART 1)

*Depth: NERDC Primary 1 · SCS · First Term W9 (pp 655–656).*

**You will learn to**

• say that Nigeria is my country
• state where Nigeria is
• describe our national flag
• say how the flag is treated

**Things to know**

• **Nigeria is my country**; I am a **Nigerian** and Nigeria is my identity.
• Nigeria is in the west of the continent of **Africa**; our neighbours are **Benin Republic, Niger, Chad and Cameroon**.
• The **capital** of Nigeria is **Abuja**; the biggest city is **Lagos**.
• Our national flag has **three vertical parts — green, white, green**.
• **Green** stands for agriculture and our forests; **white** stands for **peace and unity**.
• The flag was made by **Michael Akinkunmi** in 1959, and it was first raised on the 1st of October 1960.
• The flag is saluted at school in the morning; it is flown at half a mast when a great person dies.
• We treat the flag with respect: it is not used as a towel, a napkin, a bag or a table cloth, and it is not allowed to touch the ground.
• Other documents of my identity (seen later at school): birth certificate, national identity card, passport.

**Words for my notebook**

Nigeria is my country in Africa. Our flag is green, white, green: land, peace and unity. We respect the flag.

**Let us talk**

1. What is your country?
2. Which continent is Nigeria in?
3. What is the colour in the middle of our flag, and what does it mean?
4. Mention two ways we show respect to the flag.

**Worksheet**

1. Nigeria is in the continent of A) America B) Africa C) Europe D) Asia
2. The capital of Nigeria is A) Abuja B) Ibadan C) Kano D) Lagos
3. The colours of the Nigerian flag are A) red, white, blue B) green, red C) black, yellow D) green, white, green
4. The white colour on our flag stands for A) wealth B) war C) peace and unity D) rain
5. Fill in: Our flag was designed by Michael ____ in 1959.
6. Name the countries that border Nigeria.
7. Write two sentences on how the flag is respected at school.

**My own work**

Draw the Nigerian flag with a ruler, colour it correctly, and write "green — white — green" under it.

---

### WEEK 8 — NATIONAL IDENTITY AND SYMBOLS (PART 2)

*Depth: NERDC Primary 1 · SCS · First Term W10 (pp 655–656).*

**You will learn to**

• sing the first stanza of the national anthem
• describe the coat of arms
• name our money
• say when our independence is kept

**Things to know**

• The **national anthem** is sung standing at school and at national events. First stanza: *"Arise, O compatriots, Nigeria calls you all; To serve her throne, her country, with heart and might…"* (learned as the class is taught it).
• The **national pledge** is also said at school; we pledge to be faithful and to honour Nigeria.
• The **coat of arms** has: two **horses** on the sides, a **shield** in the middle with a **black band** in a **Y-shape**, a **red eagle** on top, and **grass and a flower** below.
• The **Y** stands for the rivers **Niger and Benue**; the **black band** is the fertile, rich soil of Nigeria.
• Our money is the **Naira (₦)**, and its small part is the **kobo**: **100 kobo make one Naira**; we use **notes and coins**.
• Our **independence** is on the **1st of October**; it is kept as a public holiday with parades and the President's broadcast.
• Other things that identify us: the **eagle**, our membership of the United Nations and the African Union, and the languages of our peoples.
• Being proud to be Nigerian means: I do not say my country is the worst; I work to make it better.

**Words for my notebook**

Anthem, pledge, coat of arms, Naira and kobo, October 1st — these are the symbols of Nigeria.

**Let us talk**

1. What is on top of the coat of arms?
2. What does the Y-shape on the shield stand for?
3. How many kobo make one Naira?
4. Which day is our independence day?

**Worksheet**

1. The national anthem is sung while we are A) eating B) sitting C) standing D) running
2. The two animals on the sides of the coat of arms are A) cows B) horses C) lions D) goats
3. Our money is the A) franc B) Naira C) cedi D) dollar
4. Nigeria became independent on A) 1st June B) 1st October 1960 C) 29th May 1999 D) 1st January
5. Fill in: The black band on the shield stands for the ____ soil.
6. Mention four national symbols of Nigeria.
7. Write two sentences on what makes you proud to be a Nigerian.

**My own work**

Practise the anthem and the pledge at home, and write how many kobo make five Naira.

Draw the Nigerian flag with green at each side and white between them, then colour it.

---

### WEEK 9 — PEOPLE, PLACES AND OBJECTS TO RESPECT (PARTS 1 AND 2)

*Depth: NERDC Primary 1 · SCS · Second Term W1 and W2 (pp 657–658) — both weeks in one.*

**You will learn to**

• name the people I must respect
• show respect in four ways
• behave well in a sacred place
• treat public places and objects with care

**Things to know**

• **Elders are respected**: father and mother, grandparents, teachers, community leaders, and every grown person older than me.
• Ways of showing respect: **greeting them well**, **listening**, **obeying**, and **using their respectful title** (ma, sir, papa, chief, alhaji, pastor, reverend).
• We also respect **people of all ages and backgrounds** — the small child, the person with a disability, a person of another tribe or religion.
• Kindness is respect: we speak softly, we help, we do not mock.
• Respect matters in society because without it people quarrel, and no work goes on.
• **Sacred places**: the church, the mosque, the shrine and the traditional site. In them we are **quiet**, we **dress properly**, and we **do not litter or run about**.
• **Public places**: school, hospital, market, bus park, library — we keep them clean, queue, and do not damage them.
• **Objects to respect**: the **flag**, **religious books** (Bible, Qur'an), the **elders' belongings**, and **national monuments**.
• Respecting national monuments means not writing on the wall, not breaking, and telling others to stop.
• A child who respects is respected; a child who honours is honoured.

**Words for my notebook**

I respect elders, all people, sacred places, public places, the flag, religious books and monuments.

**Let us talk**

1. Name four people you must respect.
2. Mention three ways of showing respect.
3. How should we behave in a church or a mosque?
4. Why must we respect the flag and religious books?

**Worksheet**

1. The first people we respect are our A) friends only B) dogs C) parents and elders D) strangers only
2. In a sacred place we should be A) eating B) running C) quiet and well dressed D) noisy
3. Writing on a national monument is A) allowed B) clever C) cheap D) wrong
4. Which one is an object to respect? A) the flag B) a broken cup C) a stone D) my old shoe
5. Fill in: A child who respects is ____.
6. Mention four public places and how we behave in them.
7. Write two sentences on why respect is important in a community.

**My own work**

Write five sentences beginning with "I respect…" naming five people or places.

Draw the thing in your house you must hold with care and colour it.


---

# Yoruba

Weeks 1 to 6, 8, 9 and 11. No note for Week 7 (Mid-Term) or Week 10 (Revision).
**Week 11 carries a note** because the school's own table prints it as a teaching row ("Exam
Preparation"), not as an examination week.
*Depth: this subject is school-generated — there is no NERDC Yoruba scheme to floor it against. The
extent of each week is the three strands the school itself prints (Ẹ̀dẹ̀ · Àṣà · Lítíréṣọ̀). The master
corpus prints Yoruba without tone marks; tone marks are added in the body of the note so that the
child reads the word correctly, and the topic line is left exactly as the school prints it.*


### WEEK 1 — Ede: Didaruko nnkan inu yara ikawe / Asa: Ikini ni ile Yoruba / Litireso: Orin keekeekee (imototo, ibawi)

*Depth: school-generated — three strands as printed in the master corpus for N2 F1 W1.*

**You will learn to**

• name things in the classroom in Yoruba
• greet an elder the Yoruba way
• say the words of a small song on cleanliness and respect

**Things to know — Ẹ̀dẹ̀ (naming things in the room)**

• yàrá ìkáwé — the classroom
• tébèèlì — table · ìgáòkó — chair · pátákó — the board
• ìwé — book · ìyàgà/penṣìlì — pencil · ẹlẹ́dọ̀tí — eraser
• bẹ́ẹ̀dì — bed (in the picture) · wọ̀nyí ni nǹkan tí mo rí nínú yàrá — "these are the things I see in the room"
• Pattern of speech: **"Èyí ni ______."** (This is a ______.)
• Naming game: I touch a thing and say its Yoruba name; my friend says it back.

**Things to know — Àṣà (greeting in Yorubaland)**

• In Yorubaland we **greet**; we do not walk past a person in silence.
• **Ẹ́ káàárọ̀** — good morning · **Ẹ́ káàṣánṣá** — good afternoon · **Ẹ́ káàlẹ̀** — good evening
• **Ọ́ dábọ̀** — welcome (to one who has returned) · **Ẹ ṣé** — thank you
• A boy greets an elder by **ìdòbì** (prostration); a girl by **ìkúnlẹ̀** (kneeling). To a king: **"Ẹ̀ kóò"** with the hands on the ground.
• When an elder greets me, I stop what I am doing and answer.
• Greeting is the first lesson of Yoruba manners: **àgànmọ́** (good character).

**Things to know — Lítíréṣọ̀ (the small song)**

• Orin kékeré — a small song. We sing it twice, then we do the actions.
• *"Ìmọ̀tọ̀́ tọ́, ìbàwọ̀n rẹ̀;* — cleanliness is right, and respect goes with it;
• *"ọmọ tí ó mọ́, ni a fiṣọ́ ọwọ́."* — the child that is clean is the one people welcome.
• Words of the song to say aloud: **ìmọ̀tọ̀́** (cleanliness), **ìbàwọ̀n** (respect), **ọmọ** (child), **ọwọ́** (hand).

**Words for my notebook**

Ẹ̀ káàárọ̀ · Ẹ̀ káàṣánṣá · Ẹ̀ káàlẹ̀ · Ẹ́ ṣé. Èyí ni ìwé. — This is a book.

**Let us talk**

1. Point to the chair and say its Yoruba name.
2. How do you greet your teacher at eight in the morning?
3. What does a girl do when she greets an elder?
4. Sing the small song with the actions.

**Worksheet**

1. "Good morning" in Yoruba is A) ẹ̀ káàlẹ̀ B) ẹ̀ káàárọ̀ C) ọ́ dábọ̀ D) ẹ́ ṣé
2. Ìwé means A) chair B) book C) board D) bag
3. A girl greets an elder by A) kneeling B) shouting C) sitting down D) running
4. "Ẹ́ ṣé" is said when somebody A) gives you something B) calls you C) hurts you D) leaves
5. Fill in: The Yoruba name for the classroom is yàrá ____.
6. Say four things in the room in Yoruba.
7. Sing or say the small song of the week to your class.

**My own work**

Write the Yoruba name of five things in this room, and greet three people at home this evening in Yoruba.

Draw five things in your classroom and colour them; say each Yoruba name as you colour it.

---

### WEEK 2 — Ede: Onka ede Yoruba 1-10 / Asa: Iwa rere / Litireso: Orin ikilo fun omode

*Depth: school-generated — three strands as printed for N2 F1 W2.*

**You will learn to**

• count from one to ten in Yoruba
• name the marks of good character
• say a small warning song for children

**Things to know — Ẹ̀dẹ̀ (counting 1–10)**

• **ọ̀kan** 1 · **èjì** 2 · **ẹ̀ta** 3 · **ẹ̀rin** 4 · **àárún** 5
• **ẹ̀fà** 6 · **ẹ̀je** 7 · **ẹ̀jọ** 8 · **ẹ̀sán** 9 · **ẹ̀wá** 10
• Clap once for ọ̀kan, twice for èjì… ten claps for ẹ̀wá.
• Counting backwards: ẹ̀wá, ẹ̀sán, ẹ̀jọ, ẹ̀je, ẹ̀fà, àárún, ẹ̀rin, ẹ̀ta, èjì, ọ̀kan.
• Ask and answer: **"Meloo ni o ní?"** (How many do you have?) — **"Mẹ́ta ni mo ní."** (I have three.)
• Numbers are used for days, for age and for the market.

**Things to know — Àṣà (good character — ìwà rere)**

• **Ìwà rere** means good character, and it is the greatest wealth of a child.
• A child of good character: tells the **truth** (òtítọ́), is **kind** (àànú), **shares**, **obeys**, **does not steal**, and **does not lie**.
• **"Ìwà lẹ́wàá"** — character is the true beauty; a fine face without good character is nothing.
• A good child greets, says "Ẹ́ ṣé", and returns what he borrowed.
• The Yoruba say: **"Ọmọ dé, a wo bí a ṣe ń hùwà"** — when a child arrives, people watch how he behaves.

**Things to know — Lítíréṣọ̀ (warning song — orin ìkìlọ̀)**

• An **orin ìkìlọ̀** is a song that warns children away from bad things.
• *"Má ṣe kòrò, kí owó má ṣè sọnù;* — do not be idle, so that your money will not sleep;
• *"má ṣe ja, kí ọ̀rẹ́ má ṣè bọ́."* — do not quarrel, so that your friend will not fall away.
• The song has three rules: do not be lazy, do not quarrel, do not tell lies.
• We say it as a rhyme in the morning and sing it once before we sit down.

**Words for my notebook**

ọ̀kan · èjì · ẹ̀ta · ẹ̀rin · àárún · ẹ̀fà · ẹ̀je · ẹ̀jọ · ẹ̀sán · ẹ̀wá. Ìwà lẹ́wàá.

**Let us talk**

1. Count from ọ̀kan to ẹ̀wá.
2. What is the Yoruba word for five?
3. Mention three marks of good character.
4. What does "Ìwà lẹ́wàá" mean?

**Worksheet**

1. The Yoruba word for four is A) ẹ̀ta B) àárún C) ẹ̀rin D) ẹ̀fà
2. ẹ̀wá means A) eight B) six C) ten D) nine
3. "Ìwà lẹ́wàá" teaches that A) food is good B) character is beauty C) clothes are beauty D) money is beauty
4. Which one is NOT good character? A) sharing B) lying C) truth D) kindness
5. Fill in: The Yoruba for five is ____.
6. Count from 1 to 10 in Yoruba, then backwards.
7. Say two rules of the warning song in your own words.

**My own work**

Write the Yoruba names of numbers one to ten in order, and count ten beans into a cup aloud.

Draw ten oranges and colour them while you count them out loud in Yoruba.

---

### WEEK 3 — Ede: Kika alifabeeti ede Yoruba / Asa: Awon ojo ninu ose / Litireso: Orin idaraya

*Depth: school-generated — three strands as printed for N2 F1 W3.*

**You will learn to**

• say the Yoruba alphabet in order
• name the seven days of the week in Yoruba
• join in a play song with the right actions

**Things to know — Ẹ̀dẹ̀ (the Yoruba alphabet — alifábéẹ̀dẹ̀)**

• The Yoruba alphabet has **twenty-five letters: 18 consonants and 7 vowels**.
• The vowels are: **a, e, ẹ, i, o, ọ, u** — the dots under **ẹ** and **ọ** change the sound.
• The consonants are: **b, d, f, g, gb, h, j, k, l, m, n, ń, p, r, s, ṣ, t, w, y**.
• Yoruba does not use **c, q, v, x, z**; those are only found in borrowed words.
• **gb** and **ṣ** are two letters each: gb as in "bag-bag", ṣ as in "she" but shorter.
• The **ń** (n with a dot) is a nasal sound: ìń ìń — as in "ọ̀nà".
• Three tones mark the voice: **à** (low), **á** (high), **ā/à** with the middle — and the combination marks, e.g. **ǒ, ǐ, ẹ́**.

**Things to know — Àṣà (the days of the week — àwọn ọjọ́ inú ọ̀ṣẹ̀)**

• **Ọjọ́ Àìkú** — Sunday · **Ọjọ́ Ajé** — Monday · **Ọjọ́ Ìṣẹ́gun** — Tuesday
• **Ọjọ́-rúkú-ọ̀jọ́** — Wednesday · **Ọjọ́ Bọ̀ṣẹ̀** — Thursday · **Ọjọ́ Ẹtì** — Friday · **Ọjọ́ Àbámẹ́ta** — Saturday
• Say them in order and clap seven times: the week is complete, then it begins again.
• Our markets have days: Ọjọ́ Àìkú is the day of church and rest; Ọjọ́ Ẹtì is the day of prayer for the Muslims.
• The names teach history: **Ìṣẹ́gun** (victory), **Bọ̀ṣẹ̀** (the market day of the Ibadan people), **Ẹtì** (Friday).

**Things to know — Lítíréṣọ̀ (play song — orin ìdáráya)**

• **Orin ìdáráya** is a song sung while we play, with actions and a ring of children.
• *"Yíyí po, a má yí po;* — going round, we turn round;
• *"ọmọ tí ó bá ṣàn, ni ó máa ṣojú."* — the child that runs fast will be the one chosen.
• How to play: children hold hands in a ring, sing twice, and on the last word one child is chosen to stand in the middle.
• The play song teaches turn-taking and the rhythm of the language.

**Words for my notebook**

a b d e ẹ f g gb h i j k l m n ń o ọ p r s ṣ t w y — twenty-five letters; 7 vowels.

**Let us talk**

1. How many letters are in the Yoruba alphabet?
2. Say the seven vowels.
3. Which day is Ọjọ́ Bọ̀ṣẹ̀?
4. Sing the play song and show the action.

**Worksheet**

1. The Yoruba alphabet has A) 21 letters B) 25 letters C) 26 letters D) 30 letters
2. Which of these is a Yoruba vowel? A) q B) v C) ẹ D) c
3. Ọjọ́ Àìkú is A) Friday B) Sunday C) Wednesday D) Monday
4. The two Yoruba letters with special sounds are gb and A) sh B) ch C) kh D) ṣ
5. Fill in: The seven ____ of the week are called àwọn ọjọ́.
6. Say the days of the week in Yoruba in order.
7. Name the letters of the Yoruba alphabet from a to gb.

**My own work**

Copy the twenty-five Yoruba letters into your notebook, and write your own day of the week under each.

---

### WEEK 4 — Ede: Isorongbesi laarin akekoo / Asa: Imototo ara eni / Litireso: Orin iwa rere

*Depth: school-generated — three strands as printed for N2 F1 W4.*

**You will learn to**

• speak politely to another pupil in Yoruba
• say the daily acts of keeping the body clean
• sing a song of good character

**Things to know — Ẹ̀dẹ̀ (polite talk among pupils — ìsọ̀rọ̀ńbẹ̀sọ̀rọ̀)**

• **"ọ̀rọ̀ rẹ̀ dáadáa ni"** — your word is good (a praise of what a friend said).
• Asking: **"Ẹ̀ jọ̀ọ́, fún mi ní penṣìlì."** — please give me a pencil.
• Answering: **"Ẹ́ ṣé o."** — thank you. · When you hurt somebody: **"Ì jọ̀ọ́ ẹ̀, ẹ̀ má bà mi nù."** — please, do not be angry with me.
• Greeting a friend: **"ọ́ rè káàbọ̀"** — welcome, my friend; and asking **"Ṣé àlàáfíà?"** — is it well? Answer: **"Àlàáfíà ni."**
• We do not use rude names; nicknames that pain are not "polite talk".
• Polite words end in the respectful **"ẹ̀"** when speaking to an elder — ẹ̀ jọ̀ọ́, ẹ́ ṣé, ẹ̀ máàdọ́rùn.

**Things to know — Àṣà (cleanliness of the person — ìmọ̀tọ̀́tọ̀ ara ẹni)**

• **Ìmọ̀tọ̀́tọ̀ jẹ́ ìlera** — cleanliness is health.
• Every day: **bìrìkì** (bath), **fífó ẹnu** (washing the mouth), **fífọ́ ọwọ́** (washing the hands).
• **Dídá nǹkan rẹ̀ mọ́** — keeping your own things clean: uniform, shoes, bag.
