# Mathematics & English

## LITERACY — LANGUAGE DOMAIN

### WEEK 1 — VOWELS AND CONSONANTS

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · Literacy (Language Domain) · First Term W1 (pp 465–469).*

**You will learn to**

• name the five vowels: a, e, i, o, u
• say that all the other letters are consonants
• sort letters into vowels and consonants
• find the vowels inside a word

**Things to know**

• Of the **26** letters, **five are vowels: a, e, i, o, u**. Sometimes **y** works as a vowel too (my, fly, happy).
• The other **twenty-one** letters are **consonants**: b, c, d, f, g, h, j, k, l, m, n, p, q, r, s, t, v, w, x, y, z.
• Why they are special: when I say a vowel my mouth stays open and the air goes out freely; a consonant is made by closing or narrowing the mouth with lips, teeth or tongue.
• **Every word in English has at least one vowel.** "I" and "a" are words made of one vowel only.
• A word without a vowel is not readable — that is why we write "skul" as **school**.
• Two vowels together often make one long sound: **see, boat, rain, read**.
• Sorting game: two hoops on the floor, vowel letters in one hoop, consonant letters in the other.
• Counting vowels in a word: **banana** has 3 a's and 2 a's — five vowels in all; **book** has 2.
• Clap the vowels: I clap once for each vowel sound I hear in a spoken word.
• This work is the foundation for reading and writing; every word I read will need a vowel.

**Words for my notebook**

The vowels are a, e, i, o, u. The other 21 letters are consonants. Every word has at least one vowel.

**Let us talk**

1. Say the five vowels.
2. How many consonants are there?
3. Circle the vowels in your name.
4. Is "m" a vowel or a consonant, and how do you know?

**Worksheet**

1. The five vowels are A) a, e, i, o, u B) a, b, c, d, e C) x, y, z, w, q D) b, c, d, f, g
2. Letters that are not vowels are called A) names B) sounds C) consonants D) words
3. The vowel(s) in "cat" is/are A) t B) c C) a D) none
4. Which word has TWO vowels? A) cat B) dog C) book D) pen
5. Fill in: The letter that is sometimes a vowel is ____.
6. Write the five vowels in small letters, then the first five consonants.
7. Say whether each of these is a vowel or a consonant: m, o, p, e, s.

**My own work**

Write your name and circle every vowel in it.

Draw five little houses for a, e, i, o and u, write one letter on each roof, then colour the roofs.

---

### WEEK 2 — SINGLE SOUNDS: d, a, i, m

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · Literacy (Language Domain) · First Term W2 (pp 465–469).*

**You will learn to**

• say the sounds /d/, /a/, /i/, /m/
• write the letters d, a, i, m
• blend these four sounds into real words
• hear where a sound sits in a word

**Things to know**

• Four sounds this week: **/d/** as in dog, doll, dad, dig; **/a/** as in apple, ant, cat, mat; **/i/** as in ink, insect, sit, pin; **/m/** as in mum, man, mat, milk.
• With only four sounds I can already make real words: **dad, mad, dim, aim, amid, man** (with /n/ from before).
• **Blending** is saying the sounds fast together: **d – a – d** = *dad*; **m – a – d** = *mad*; **d – i – m** = *dim*.
• **Segmenting** is the opposite: I hear "dim" and say /d/ /i/ /m/, three sounds, three letters.
• Sound positions: in "dog", /d/ is at the **beginning**; in "mad", /a/ is in the **middle**; in "bid", /d/ is at the **end**.
• Writing: **d** is a circle with a tall stem up; **a** is a circle with a short line; **i** is a short line with a dot; **m** is three little humps.
• Say the sound, not the letter name: /m/, not "em", when we are building words.
• Finger spelling: one finger per sound, then write the letters in order.
• Tricky pair: /d/ and /b/ feel the same in the mouth — the difference is that /d/ uses the tongue tip and stays short.
• Practice game: I raise a card when I hear /m/ in a word you say.

**Words for my notebook**

d a i m — /d/ dog, /a/ apple, /i/ ink, /m/ mum. d-a-d = dad. m-a-d = mad. d-i-m = dim.

**Let us talk**

1. Make the sound /m/ for me.
2. Blend: m – a – n.
3. What is the first sound in "dog"?
4. Which of our four letters needs a dot?
5. Say a word that ends with /d/.

**Worksheet**

1. The first sound in "mum" is A) /m/ B) /u/ C) /d/ D) /n/
2. d – i – m spells A) dam B) dim C) mad D) mid
3. The sound /a/ is heard at the beginning of A) dog B) mum C) apple D) ink
4. Which letter is written with a dot on top? A) a B) m C) d D) i
5. Fill in: The word "dad" is made of ____ sounds.
6. Write each of d, a, i, m four times in small letters.
7. Say three words that begin with /m/.

**My own work**

Write the words dad, mad, dim, man and use each in a spoken sentence in class.

Draw a dog, an ant, an insect and a map; write the first sound under each one and colour the ant.

---

### WEEK 3 — SIGHT WORDS: You, Me, Her, We

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · Literacy (Language Domain) · First Term W3 (pp 465–469).*

**You will learn to**

• read four sight words at once
• write them correctly
• say what each one means
• use them in sentences

**Things to know**

• A **sight word** is a word I know on sight — I do not sound it out, I read it in one look.
• This week's four: **you, me, her, we**.
• **me** points at myself; **you** points at the person I am talking to; **her** points at a girl or woman; **we** means me and other people together.
• "You" and "we" start with the same letter **w**? No — **y**ou and **w**e: look carefully, these two are often swapped.
• These words break the sound rules a little, so they must be memorised by the eye: "you" has a **y** but no /y/ sound in it.
• Flash-card drill: the card shows for two seconds; I say the word before it is hidden.
• Sentences I can now read: *"You and me." "We see her." "Her bag is red."*
• Writing: these words go on the line with no capitals unless they begin a sentence; "I" is the only small word that is always a capital.
• Memory games: word-to-picture matching, and "what is missing?" when one card is hidden under a book.
• My sight-word bank is growing: this week four, next week three more.

**Words for my notebook**

you · me · her · we — I read them without spelling. "We see her."

**Let us talk**

1. Which word points at yourself?
2. Read this card: "you".
3. Make a sentence with "we".
4. Which of these four words is about a girl?

**Worksheet**

1. The word that points at the person being spoken to is A) her B) me C) you D) we
2. "We" means A) one girl B) me and others C) only me D) many things
3. Choose the correct word: "____ see the fan." A) We B) Me C) Her D) You and me only
4. A sight word is a word that A) must be sounded out B) has six letters C) is always a name D) is read at once
5. Fill in: The four sight words this week are you, me, ____ and we.
6. Write one sentence using "her".
7. Read these cards aloud in class: you — me — her — we.

**My own work**

Write each of the four words five times and read them to your classmate tomorrow.

Draw the people in your family, point at yourself, write 'me' under that one and colour the picture.

---

### WEEK 4 — SIGHT WORDS: I, Go, Car

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · Literacy (Language Domain) · First Term W4 (pp 465–469).*

**You will learn to**

• read and write three sight words you have not used yet
• always write "I" as a capital
• join new words with the old ones
• read short sentences

**Things to know**

• Three new words: **I**, **go**, **car**.
• **I** is a single letter but it is always a **capital**, wherever it stands: *"I go." "She and I run."*
• **go** is a verb — it is a doing word; **car** is a noun — it is a thing.
• My sight words are now seven: **you, me, her, we, I, go, car**.
• Short sentences I can read and write: *"I go." "We go." "I see a car." "You and I go." "I go in her car."*
• Word order matters in English: *"I go car in"* is wrong; *"I go in the car"* is right.
• Read the whole sentence, then look back at the words you did not know.
• Building sentences: add one word at a time to a short sentence and read it again each time.
• Copying practice: read the word, cover it, write it, then check — never copy letter by letter.
• Dictation: I hear the sentence twice, then write it, then read my own writing back.

**Words for my notebook**

I · go · car. "I" is always big. Sentences: I go. We go. I see a car.

**Let us talk**

1. Why is "I" written big?
2. Read this sentence: "I go in her car."
3. Which of this week's words is a doing word?
4. Make a sentence that has "car" and "we".

**Worksheet**

1. The word "I" is written A) always a capital B) always small C) only at the start D) with a dot
2. "car" is a A) thing B) doing word C) letter D) sound
3. Which sentence is correct? A) I go in the car. B) I go car. C) Car I go. D) Go I car.
4. Which sight words do I now know? A) six B) seven C) four D) ten
5. Fill in: "We ____ in her car." (go / car / I)
6. Write two sentences, each using "I" and "car".
7. Read aloud: "You and I go. I see her car."

**My own work**

Write five sentences using any of the seven sight words, and underline every "I".

---

### WEEK 5 — DIGRAPH: th, ck, wh

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · Literacy (Language Domain) · First Term W7 (pp 465–469).*

**You will learn to**

• say what a digraph is
• read and write words with th, ck and wh
• hear /th/ at the beginning and at the end of words
• sort words by their digraph

**Things to know**

• A **digraph** is **two letters that make one sound**: *sh, ch, th, ph, wh, ck*.
• **th** makes two different sounds: a soft/buzzy one at the start of **the, this, that, with, they**, and a hissy one at the end of **bath, math, three, think, thumb**.
• For the buzzy /th/ my tongue tip touches the teeth and my voice works; for the hissy /th/ the voice is off. Feel your throat to tell them apart.
• **ck** says /k/ and is nearly always at the **end** of a small word: **back, clock, duck, pick, stick, black, kick**.
• **wh** says /w/ at the start of questions: **what, when, where, which, white, wheel**.
• Words where two letters do one job are not spelled the way they sound — that is why they need practice.
• Sorting game: three columns on the board — th, ck, wh — and I put each word card in its column.
• Reading practice: *"Who took the clock from the bench?"* — three digraphs in one sentence.
• Writing: underline the two letters that work together, so I do not forget them.
• Sound hunt: how many words with **th** can our class say in one minute?

**Words for my notebook**

A digraph is two letters, one sound. th: this, bath. ck: duck, clock. wh: what, when, where.

**Let us talk**

1. What is a digraph?
2. Say "this" and "bath" — where is your tongue?
3. Name two words ending in ck.
4. Which two letters are in "what"?
5. Is "sh" a digraph? Why?

**Worksheet**

1. A digraph is A) a capital letter B) a word with six letters C) two letters making one sound D) two sounds in one letter
2. The digraph at the end of "duck" is A) ck B) wh C) sh D) th
3. "think" begins with the sound A) /t/ B) /th/ (voice off) C) /f/ D) /s/
4. Which word has "wh"? A) rich B) chin C) back D) which
5. Fill in: The two letters that make the /k/ sound at the end of "clock" are ____.
6. Write two th-words, two ck-words and two wh-words.
7. Underline every digraph in this sentence: "They took the black clock home."

**My own work**

Find five things at home whose names have a digraph in them and write the digraph under each.

---

### WEEK 6 — SIGHT WORDS: The, This, They

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · Literacy (Language Domain) · First Term W8 (pp 465–469).*

**You will learn to**

• read and write three new sight words
• notice the "th" digraph inside them
• use them in sentences
• read longer sentences

**Things to know**

• Three more sight words: **the**, **this**, **they**.
• All three begin with the **th** digraph I learnt last week — so my eye already knows two letters of each word.
• **"the"** is the most common word in English; it points at the one thing you mean: *the car, the fan*.
• **this** is for one thing near me; **these** would be for many (that comes later).
• **they** means more than one person or thing already spoken about: *"Where are the boys? They are outside."*
• My sight words are now ten: **you, me, her, we, I, go, car, the, this, they**.
• Sentences I can read: *"This is the car." "They see me." "The cat sat on the mat." "I go with them."*
• Do not write "teh" — the letters in "the" are t-h-e and the h is not a mistake.
• Flash-card speed: ten cards, ten seconds each, no sounding out.
• Writing practice: one sentence with "this", one with "they", then read them back to check.

**Words for my notebook**

the · this · they — all start with the th digraph. "This is the car." "They see me."

**Let us talk**

1. Which of our new words is the commonest word in English?
2. Read: "They see this."
3. Point at one thing and say a sentence with "this".
4. What do "the", "this" and "they" have in common at the start?

**Worksheet**

1. "the", "this" and "they" all begin with A) wh B) th C) sh D) ch
2. The word that points at one thing near me is A) the B) me C) this D) they
3. "They" means A) more than one person or thing B) a place C) a doing word D) one thing
4. How many sight words do I know now? A) ten B) seven C) twelve D) six
5. Fill in: "____ is the fan." (This / They / Me)
6. Write two sentences, one with "the" and one with "they".
7. Read all ten of your sight words from a card in class.

**My own work**

Make a card for each of the ten sight words and keep them in your pencil case for reading practice.

Draw three things in this room you could say 'this' about, and colour two of them.

---

### WEEK 8 — DIGRAPH: ph, sh, ch

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · Literacy (Language Domain) · First Term W9 (pp 465–469).*

**You will learn to**

• read and write words with ph, sh and ch
• know that ph sounds like f
• tell the three sounds apart by ear
• sort words into digraph families

**Things to know**

• **ph** says **/f/**: **phone, photo, elephant, graph, dolphin**. Strange, but true in most English words.
• **sh** says **/ʃ/** — the quiet sound a mother makes: **ship, shop, fish, wash, she, shell, brush, shoe**.
• **ch** says **/tʃ/** — a quick soft hitting sound: **chair, church, chat, chip, much, bench, lunch, chin**.
• "sh" and "ch" are easy to confuse by ear: *ship / chip*, *wash / watch*. Say them slowly, then check.
• **sh** can sit at the start or the end of a word (shop, fish); **ch** is common at both ends too (chair, much); **ph** is usually at the start or end (phone, graph).
• Digraph families I now have: **th, ck, wh, ph, sh, ch**.
• Sorting game: put word cards under the right digraph on the wall chart.
• Sentence reading: *"The children wish for a new phone."* — ch, sh and ph all in one line.
• Writing: write the two letters together, never one alone; "fone" is a spelling mistake even though it sounds right.

**Words for my notebook**

ph = /f/ (phone, photo). sh (ship, fish). ch (chair, much). Six digraphs now: th, ck, wh, ph, sh, ch.

**Let us talk**

1. What sound does ph make?
2. Name three things with "sh" in their names.
3. Say "ship" then "chip" — how are they different?
4. In "elephant", which letters say /f/?
5. Which digraph is in "lunch"?

**Worksheet**

1. "ph" is pronounced like A) f B) v C) p D) ph
2. Which word has the "sh" digraph? A) step B) soap C) shop D) ship and shop — choose the odd one
3. The digraph in "much" is A) th B) ck C) sh D) ch
4. Which spelling is correct? A) phoe B) phone C) phon D) fone
5. Fill in: In "photo", the two letters that make one sound are ____.
6. Write two words each with ph, sh and ch.
7. Read this sentence twice: "The children wash the chess board on the bench."

**My own work**

Look for five digraph words in any of your story books and write them with their digraph underlined.

Draw a phone, a fish and a child; write the two-letter sound under each and colour the fish.

---

### WEEK 9 — SIGHT WORDS: Photo, She, Chart · CONSONANT BLENDS: Play, Fly, Greet

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · Literacy (Language Domain) · First Term W10 (pp 465–469).*

**You will learn to**

• read and write three sight words that carry digraphs
• say what a consonant blend is
• read words with pl, fl and gr
• tell a blend from a digraph
• read and write sentences using everything I know

**Things to know**

• Three sight words: **photo** (has **ph**), **she** (has **sh**), **chart** (has **ch**) — my digraph words are now sight words.
• A **consonant blend** is **two consonants together where I still hear both sounds**: **pl**ay, **fl**y, **gr**eet, **bl**ue, **cl**ap, **fr**og.
• That is the difference: in a **digraph** two letters make **one** sound (sh, ch, ph); in a **blend** two letters keep **both** sounds (pl = /p/ + /l/).
• **pl**: play, please, plant, plus, plum.
• **fl**: fly, flag, flip, flat, flower.
• **gr**: greet, green, grab, grass, grape.
• My sight words are now thirteen: you, me, her, we, I, go, car, the, this, they, photo, she, chart.
• Reading: *"She can play with the children." "I greet the girl in the green dress." "We go to the chart."*
• Blending practice with three sounds: **s-p-l-a-t** has five letters but the blend /spl/ plus at — that is how longer words begin to open for me.
• Last week of teaching: I read my thirteen words and my six digraphs from the wall chart without help.

**Words for my notebook**

photo · she · chart. Blends: pl (play), fl (fly), gr (greet). A blend keeps both sounds; a digraph makes one.

**Let us talk**

1. Which digraph is in "photo"?
2. What sounds are in the blend "fl"?
3. Say a word beginning with "gr".
4. Is "sh" a blend or a digraph, and how do you know?
5. Read this sentence: "They play in the green grass."

**Worksheet**

1. "photo", "she" and "chart" each contain A) six letters B) two vowels C) a blend D) a digraph
2. In the blend "pl", how many sounds do I hear? A) two B) one C) none D) three
3. Which word begins with a blend? A) shop B) thin C) fly D) chat
4. "greet" begins with the blend A) gr B) ee C) g D) r
5. Fill in: A digraph is two letters with ____ sound; a blend is two consonants with both sounds.
6. Write three words each with pl, fl and gr.
7. Read and copy this sentence: "She can play with the children in the green field."

**My own work**

Write five sentences using your sight words, and underline every digraph and blend you use.

## LITERACY — LETTER WORK

### WEEK 1 — REVIEW OF ALPHABET: Aa – Ff

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · Literacy (Letter Work) · First Term W1 (pp 454–457).*

**You will learn to**

• know the letters A to F in big and small form
• match a capital letter to its small letter
• say the sound of each letter
• trace and write Aa to Ff

**Things to know**

• The alphabet has **26 letters**; I am reviewing the first six: **A a, B b, C c, D d, E e, F f**.
• A letter has two shapes: **capital** (big) and **small** (lower case). Capitals begin names and sentences.
• **A a** says /a/ — **apple**, **ant**; **B b** says /b/ — **ball**, **bat**; **C c** says /k/ — **cat**, **cup**.
• **D d** says /d/ — **dog**, **doll**; **E e** says /e/ — **egg**, **hen**; **F f** says /f/ — **fan**, **fish**.
• The sound a letter makes is not the same as its **name**: the letter is "bee", the sound is /b/.
• Matching game: capital card to small card — A with a, B with b, and so on.
• **Initial sound**: the first sound I hear in a word. "Fan" starts with /f/, so it belongs to F.
• Writing: **a** sits on the line, **b** has a tall stem up, no tail; **f** goes up and has a cross-bar.
• Alphabet song slowly: A B C D E F, then stop and clap the six sounds.

**Words for my notebook**

Aa Bb Cc Dd Ee Ff — apple, ball, cat, dog, egg, fan.

**Let us talk**

1. Sing the first six letters.
2. What sound does C make?
3. Name a word that starts with /d/.
4. Which small letter goes with E?
5. Trace b and d: which one has its tall stem up?

**Worksheet**

1. The small letter of "B" is A) p B) b C) d D) q
2. Which word begins with /f/? A) fan B) cat C) man D) van
3. The sound of the letter A in "apple" is A) /a/ B) /e/ C) /o/ D) /i/
4. Which pair matches? A) Ee B) Cc C) Dd D) Ff — find the odd one out in the pairs Aa, Bb, Cc, De
5. Fill in: The letter that begins "egg" is ____.
6. Write Aa to Ff, one line of capitals and one line of small letters.
7. Draw one thing for B and one thing for F, and write the letter beside each.

**My own work**

Find six things at home that begin with A, B, C, D, E, F and write their letters.

---

### WEEK 2 — REVIEW OF ALPHABET: Gg – Ll

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · Literacy (Letter Work) · First Term W2 (pp 454–457).*

**You will learn to**

• know the letters G to L
• say the sound of each of them
• match capitals to small letters
• write Gg to Ll on the line

**Things to know**

• **G g, H h, I i, J j, K k, L l** — the next six letters.
• Sounds: **G** /g/ — goat, gun; **H** /h/ — hat, hen; **I** /i/ — ink, insect; **J** /dʒ/ — jug, jam; **K** /k/ — kite, key; **L** /l/ — lion, lamp.
• **G** and **K** make the same sound in most small words (goat, kite) — that is not a mistake, English is like that.
• The letter **I** is also the word for me: **"I"** is always a capital, even in the middle of a sentence.
• Matching: capital to small, letter to picture, letter to the first sound in my name.
• Writing: **g** goes down below the line; **h** is tall; **i** has a dot; **j** has a dot and a tail; **k** is tall with two arms; **l** is a tall straight line.
• Sound hunt: point to things in the room that begin with /h/, /l/, /g/.
• My letters so far: **Aa Bb Cc Dd Ee Ff Gg Hh Ii Jj Kk Ll** — twelve.

**Words for my notebook**

Gg Hh Ii Jj Kk Ll — goat, hat, ink, jug, kite, lion.

**Let us talk**

1. What sound does H make?
2. Which letter always looks like a capital, even in the middle of a sentence?
3. Name a word that starts with /k/.
4. Say your letters from A to L.

**Worksheet**

1. The letter that begins "lion" is A) i B) n C) r D) l
2. Which pair does NOT match? A) Jj B) Kk, kite — choose: G–goat, H–hat, J–kite C) Gg D) Hh
3. /g/ is the sound of A) l B) g C) j D) k
4. Which letters both say /k/? A) G and L B) H and J C) C and K D) I and K
5. Fill in: The small letter of "H" is ____.
6. Circle the words that start with I: ink — insect — igloo — apple.
7. Write Gg to Ll, capitals and small letters.

**My own work**

Write the first letter of your name and your friend's name in capital and small form.

---

### WEEK 3 — REVIEW OF ALPHABET: Mm – Rr

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · Literacy (Letter Work) · First Term W3 (pp 454–457).*

**You will learn to**

• know the letters M to R
• say their sounds
• hear the first sound in a spoken word
• write Mm to Rr neatly

**Things to know**

• **M m, N n, O o, P p, Q q, R r**.
• Sounds: **M** /m/ — mat, man, milk; **N** /n/ — net, nut; **O** /ɒ/ — orange, ox; **P** /p/ — pen, pan; **Q** /kw/ — queen; **R** /r/ — rat, run.
• **Q** is nearly always followed by **u**: queen, quick, quiet — that is why its sound is /kw/.
• **M** and **N** are nasal sounds: I feel them in my nose, and my mouth closes for /m/ but not for /n/.
• Initial sound practice: hear "pan" → /p/; "net" → /n/; "run" → /r/.
• Writing: **m** has three strokes, **n** two, **p** goes down below the line, **q** below too; **o** is a full circle.
• Sorting game: which letters sit on the line and which have tails below it (g, j, p, q).
• My alphabet so far: eighteen letters, from **Aa** to **Rr**.

**Words for my notebook**

Mm Nn Oo Pp Qq Rr — mat, net, orange, pen, queen, rat.

**Let us talk**

1. What sound does N make?
2. Which letter always wants a "u" after it?
3. Say the first sound in "orange".
4. Which of your letters has a tail below the line?

**Worksheet**

1. "Queen" begins with the sound A) /k/ B) /n/ C) /q/ D) /kw/
2. The letter that begins "net" is A) r B) n C) m D) o
3. Which letter goes below the line when we write it? A) p B) m C) n D) o
4. The sound /m/ is heard first in A) net B) rat C) mat D) pen
5. Fill in: Q is nearly always followed by the letter ____.
6. Write Mm to Rr, capitals and small letters.
7. Say three words that start with /p/.

**My own work**

Look round the class and write down the first letter of six things you can see.

Write the letters M to R in large shapes on one page and colour each letter a different colour.

---

### WEEK 4 — REVIEW OF ALPHABET: Ss – Zz

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · Literacy (Letter Work) · First Term W4 (pp 454–457).*

**You will learn to**

• know the last letters S to Z
• say the sound of each
• write the whole alphabet in order
• finish the alphabet review

**Things to know**

• **S s, T t, U u, V v, W w, X x, Y y, Z z** — the last eight letters.
• Sounds: **S** /s/ — sun, sock; **T** /t/ — tap, tin; **U** /ʌ/ — umbrella, up; **V** /v/ — van, vat. **W** /w/ — water, wet; **X** /ks/ — box, fox; **Y** /j/ — yam, yes; **Z** /z/ — zebra, zone.
• **X** usually comes at the **end** of small English words: fox, box, six. Its sound is /ks/.
• **V** and **W** are not the same: for /v/ my teeth touch my lip; for /w/ my lips make a round hole.
• "X-mas" is not a word to start with x — say the sound at the end of "fox".
• Now the whole alphabet: **Aa Bb Cc Dd Ee Ff Gg Hh Ii Jj Kk Ll Mm Nn Oo Pp Qq Rr Ss Tt Uu Vv Ww Xx Yy Zz** — 26 letters.
• Singing it all: the alphabet song from A to Z with the sounds after each letter.

**Words for my notebook**

Ss Tt Uu Vv Ww Xx Yy Zz — sun, tap, umbrella, van, water, box, yam, zebra. The alphabet has 26 letters.

**Let us talk**

1. Say the alphabet from A to Z without looking.
2. What sound does Z make?
3. Where does the letter X usually appear in a small word?
4. Show me the difference between /v/ and /w/.

**Worksheet**

1. The number of letters in the English alphabet is A) 24 B) 27 C) 26 D) 25
2. "Zebra" begins with the sound A) /s/ B) /dʒ/ C) /v/ D) /z/
3. Which letter is NOT in the last eight? A) Q B) V C) W D) Y
4. The sound of X in "fox" is A) /s/ B) /ex/ C) /ks/ D) /z/
5. Fill in: The letter between T and V is ____.
6. Write the whole alphabet in small letters in your book.
7. Circle the letters that begin your name, your mother's name and your father's name.

**My own work**

Write your full name and underline each letter you have learnt this term.

Write the letters S to Z in large shapes and colour only the ones made with straight lines.

---

### WEEK 5 — THREE-LETTER WORDS: CVC PATTERN (Part 1)

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · Literacy (Letter Work) · First Term W7 (pp 454–457).*

**You will learn to**

• say what a CVC word is
• blend three sounds into one word
• read the -at and -an word families
• write three-letter words

**Things to know**

• A **three-letter word** has three sounds: a **consonant**, a **vowel**, a **consonant** — we call it **CVC**.
• To **blend** means to run the sounds together: **c – a – t** … *cat*. Say it slowly, then fast.
• The middle letter of these words is the short vowel **a**, so the family rhymes.
• The **-at** family: **cat, bat, rat, mat, hat, sat, fat**.
• The **-an** family: **can, man, pan, ran, fan, van, tan**.
• Changing the first sound makes a new word: *cat → hat → bat → mat* — only the first sound moves.
• Reading a CVC sentence: *"The cat sat on the mat."* — six words, all easy once I blend.
• Writing: say each sound, write its letter, then read my word back from left to right.
• Never write the sounds in the wrong order — read what you wrote to be sure.

**Words for my notebook**

c-a-t = cat. The -at family: cat, bat, rat, mat, hat, sat. The -an family: can, man, pan, ran, fan, van.

**Let us talk**

1. Blend this for me: m – a – t.
2. What is the middle sound in "pan"?
3. Change one sound in "cat" to make a new word.
4. Read: "The man ran."

**Worksheet**

1. A CVC word has the pattern A) three consonants B) three vowels C) vowel-consonant-vowel D) consonant-vowel-consonant
2. The word made by b-a-t is A) bat B) but C) tab D) bet
3. Which word belongs to the -an family? A) pen B) pin C) mat D) pan
4. In "cat", the vowel is A) none B) t C) a D) c
5. Fill in: m – a – ____ = man.
6. Write four words from the -at family.
7. Read this sentence twice: "The rat sat on the mat."

**My own work**

Write five CVC words and draw a small picture for two of them.

---

### WEEK 6 — THREE-LETTER WORDS: CVC PATTERN (Part 2)

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · Literacy (Letter Work) · First Term W8 (pp 454–457).*

**You will learn to**

• read the -en, -in and -og families
• blend words with the short e and short i sounds
• hear the difference between e and i
• take dictation of three-letter words

**Things to know**

• The **-en** family: **pen, hen, ten, men, den, wren**.
• The **-in** family: **pin, tin, bin, win, fin, sin**.
• The **-og** family: **dog, log, fog, hog, jog**.
• Short **e** — my mouth is open a little and flat: *pen, ten, hen*. Short **i** — my mouth is smaller and higher: *pin, tin, hit*.
• The pair that tricks people: **pen / pin**, **ten / tin**, **beg / big**. Listen for the vowel.
• Blending practice: **p – e – n** = pen; **d – o – g** = dog.
• Dictation: hear the word, say its sounds one by one with a finger for each, then write the letters.
• Word-picture matching: the picture of ten fingers goes with "ten", not "tin".
• Make a new word from an old one by changing the middle sound: *pen → pin → pan*.

**Words for my notebook**

pen, hen, ten · pin, tin, win · dog, log, fog. Short e and short i are different sounds.

**Let us talk**

1. Blend: h – e – n.
2. Say "ten" and "tin" — how are they different?
3. What word is d-o-g?
4. Change the middle sound in "pin" to make "pan".

**Worksheet**

1. The word made by t-e-n is A) ten B) tin C) ton D) tan
2. Which word has the short i sound? A) hen B) pen C) ten D) bin
3. "dog" belongs to the family A) -in B) -en C) -at D) -og
4. The first sound in "hen" is A) /hen/ B) /h/ C) /n/ D) /e/
5. Fill in: p – i – n spells ____.
6. Write two words from -en and two from -in.
7. Take dictation of four words and read your answers back.

**My own work**

Write the -en, -in and -og words on three cards each and shuffle them into their families at home.

Draw a cat, a pen and a pot; write the word under each picture and colour the pot.

---

### WEEK 8 — THREE-LETTER WORDS: CVC PATTERN (Part 3)

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · Literacy (Letter Work) · First Term W9 (pp 454–457).*

**You will learn to**

• read the -un, -op and -ug families
• build words from letter cards
• read all six word families together
• write CVC words without help

**Things to know**

• The **-un** family: **sun, bun, run, fun, gun, spun**.
• The **-op** family: **top, hop, mop, pop, cop, drop**.
• The **-ug** family: **bug, hug, mug, rug, jug, tug**.
• Word building with cards: give me s, u, n — I put them in order and read "sun".
• Sentence practice: *"The big bug sat on the rug."* — five words, and I can read all of them.
• The families so far: **-at, -an, -en, -in, -og, -un, -op, -ug** — that is the whole of my CVC collection.
• Reading games: sound a word, write it, and match it to a picture; read the fastest list in the class.
• Independent writing: no looking, say the sounds slowly, and write one letter per sound.
• Words that look similar: *bun / fun / run*, *top / hop / mop* — the first sound changes the word.

**Words for my notebook**

sun, bun, run · top, hop, mop · bug, hug, mug. I can read eight word families now.

**Let us talk**

1. Read: "The pup ran home."
2. What is the family of "mop"?
3. Change one sound in "sun" to make "fun".
4. Name the middle sound in "rug".

**Worksheet**

1. The word s-u-n is A) sun B) son C) sin D) san
2. "mug" belongs to the family A) -en B) -op C) -un D) -ug
3. Which word is made from the sounds b-u-g? A) bog B) bag C) bug D) big
4. The first sound in "top" is A) /p/ B) /t/ C) /to/ D) /o/
5. Fill in: The word that rhymes with "hop" and means the highest place is ____.
6. Build and write four words from the -un family.
7. Read this sentence: "The fun bug ran on the rug."

**My own work**

Write one sentence that uses three words from the families you have learnt.

---

### WEEK 9 — STORY TELLING AND LETTER WORK INTEGRATION

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 3 · Literacy (Letter Work) · First Term W10 (pp 454–457).*

**You will learn to**

• listen to a simple story and answer questions on it
• pick out letters and sounds from story words
• retell a story in my own words
• find CVC words inside a story

**Things to know**

• Listening rules: eyes on the teller, mouth quiet, hands still, and think about what is happening.
• Our story for the week: **"The Rat and the Sun"** — a rat, a mat, a pot and a hot sun.
• Comprehension questions are asked in this order: **who**, **what**, **where**, **when**, **why**.
• The words in the story that I can already read: *rat, sat, mat, sun, bun, pin* — all CVC words.
• Sound hunt: in the story, how many words begin with /s/? with /r/?
• Retelling: say it in three parts — the **beginning**, the **middle**, the **end**.
• The **moral** is the lesson the story teaches: for our story, small and clever can beat big and hot.
• Drawing story elements: I draw the rat, the mat, the sun, and label each with its first letter.
• Vocabulary from the story: **scorching**, **clever**, **shade** — new words go in my notebook.
• Telling my own story: every child says one sentence in turn, and we build a story together.

**Words for my notebook**

Who? What? Where? When? Why? — the five questions for any story.

**Let us talk**

1. Who was in our story?
2. What did the rat sit on?
3. Where was the shade?
4. Tell the beginning of the story in one sentence.
5. Draw one part of the story and write the letter it begins with.

**Worksheet**

1. The question that asks about a person is A) what B) where C) why D) who
2. Which of these is a CVC word from our story? A) rat B) shade C) scorching D) clever
3. The lesson a story teaches is called the A) ending B) rhyme C) title D) moral
4. The first part of a story is the A) beginning B) cover C) middle D) end
5. Fill in: In the story, the rat sat on the ____.
6. Write the letters of the three characters you liked in our story.
7. Retell our story in three sentences: beginning, middle, end.

**My own work**

Tell a story of your own to your classmate and ask them two questions about it.

## NUMERACY

### WEEK 1 — WHOLE NUMBERS 1–200

*Depth: NERDC Primary 2 · Mathematics · First Term W1 and W2 (pp 675–676); Nursery 3 · Numeracy · Second Term W7.*

**You will learn to**

• count from 1 to 200 without losing my place
• read and write numbers 1–200 in figures and in words
• say how many hundreds, tens and units are in a number
• compare two numbers with >, < and =
• count in 2s, 3s, 5s and 10s

**Things to know**

• Counting 1–100 is what I already know; this week we go on to **101, 102 … 200**.
• **100** is one hundred. **200** is two hundred — it is **two hundreds**, or **twenty tens**.
• Every number has a place: **hundreds, tens, units (ones)**. In **147** the 1 is one hundred, the 4 is four tens, the 7 is seven units.
• **Expanded form**: 147 = 100 + 40 + 7. The word "expanded" means opened out.
• Reading a number written in words: "one hundred and forty-seven" → **147**; "one hundred and ninety-one" → **191**; "two hundred" → **200**.
• Comparing: **>** is greater than, **<** is less than, **=** is equal to. The open mouth faces the bigger number: 147 **>** 74.
• Ordering 147, 102, 200, 96 from smallest to largest: **96, 102, 147, 200**.
• Skip counting: by **2s** — 2, 4, 6 … 20; by **5s** — 5, 10, 15, 20; by **10s** — 10, 20, 30 … 200.
• **Even** numbers end in 0, 2, 4, 6, 8; **odd** numbers end in 1, 3, 5, 7, 9.
• Counting is easier when I touch one object for one number: twenty caps, twenty sticks, twenty beads.
• We celebrate it: **"I can count to two hundred!"**

**Words for my notebook**

147 = 100 + 40 + 7. The places are hundreds, tens and units. 147 > 74. Counting by 10s: 10, 20, 30 … 200.

**Let us talk**

1. Count from 95 to 105.
2. In the number 152, which digit is the tens?
3. Say 100 + 60 + 3 as one number.
4. Which is bigger, 189 or 198?
5. Count by fives from 5 to 50.

**Worksheet**

1. Two hundred is the same as A) twenty tens B) two units C) two hundred units only D) two tens
2. In 163, the 6 stands for A) six units B) sixteen C) six hundreds D) six tens
3. The expanded form of 128 is A) 12 + 8 B) 1 + 2 + 8 C) 100 + 20 + 8 D) 100 + 28
4. Which sign is correct: 96 ___ 102? A) < B) > C) none D) =
5. Fill in: Counting in 10s, after 170 comes ____.
6. Write "one hundred and ninety-one" in figures.
7. Arrange 105, 99, 200, 150 from smallest to greatest.

**My own work**

Count the spoons in your house up to one hundred, and write the number you reached in figures and in words.

Draw twenty dots in two rows of ten and colour one whole row.

---

### WEEK 2 — PLACE VALUE

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 1 · Numeracy · Second Term W2 and Third Term W2 (tens and ones), reinforced by Primary 2 · First Term W1–W2.*

**You will learn to**

• group objects into tens and ones
• say the tens and ones in a two-digit number
• use a tens-and-ones chart
• build a number from its tens and ones

**Things to know**

• **Ten ones make one ten.** That one sentence is the whole lesson.
• Tie sticks in bundles of ten: three bundles and four loose sticks = **34**.
• A place-value chart has two columns: **Tens | Ones**. Put 3 beads under Tens and 4 under Ones → **34**.
• In **47**: the 4 is in the **tens** place and means 40; the 7 is in the **ones** place and means 7.
• The same digit means different things in different places: in **77** the first 7 is 70, the second 7 is 7.
• Ten frames help me see it: two full frames and six more = 26.
• Build it backwards: 5 tens and 2 ones = **52**; 9 tens and 0 ones = **90**.
• Compare with place value first: 61 and 16 use the same digits, but 61 is bigger because it has more tens.
• Round numbers: 30, 40, 50 are 3 tens, 4 tens, 5 tens — the ones column is empty (zero).

**Words for my notebook**

Ten ones make one ten. In 47, the 4 is tens (40) and the 7 is ones. Same digits, different places, different numbers.

**Let us talk**

1. How many ones make one ten?
2. Show me 27 with bundles and loose sticks.
3. In 63, which digit is in the ones place?
4. Which is bigger, 52 or 25, and why?

**Worksheet**

1. Seven tens and five ones make A) 57 B) 12 C) 705 D) 75
2. In 84, the 8 stands for A) 4 B) 800 C) 80 D) 8
3. 3 tens and 0 ones is A) 33 B) 3 C) 30 D) 13
4. The number in which the 6 shows six tens is A) 26 B) 61 C) 6 D) 16
5. Fill in: Ten ones make one ____.
6. Draw a tens-and-ones chart for the number 58.
7. Explain why 41 is greater than 14.

**My own work**

Take 40 beans or caps and group them into tens; write how many tens and how many ones you got.

---

### WEEK 3 — WRITING NUMBERS IN WORDS

*Depth: NERDC Primary 2 · Mathematics · First Term W1 (pp 675–676); Nursery 3 · Numeracy · Second Term W1.*

**You will learn to**

• write the names of numbers from 1 to 100 in words
• spell the tricky number names
• read a number name and write the figure
• match figures to words

**Things to know**

• 1 **one**, 2 **two**, 3 **three**, 4 **four**, 5 **five**, 6 **six**, 7 **seven**, 8 **eight**, 9 **nine**, 10 **ten**.
• The teens are the hard ones: 11 **eleven**, 12 **twelve**, 13 **thirteen**, 15 **fifteen**, 18 **eighteen**, 20 **twenty**.
• Note the spelling: thirteen (not *threeteen*), fifteen (not *fiveteen*), eighty (not *eighty*).
• From 21 upward we write with a space or a line: **twenty-one**, **thirty-four**, **ninety-nine**.
• 40 is **forty** — there is no "u" in it. 100 is **one hundred**; 200 is **two hundred**.
• Big ones: 147 is **one hundred and forty-seven**.
• Reading test: "sixty-five" → I write **65**; "one hundred and three" → **103**.
• Write it small and keep it on the line; a figure written upside down (6 for 9) is a mistake in sums, not in handwriting.
• Even and odd still work in words: "forty" ends in an even figure, "thirty-seven" in an odd one.

**Words for my notebook**

twenty-one · thirty-four · forty · fifty · one hundred and forty-seven. Spell "forty" without a "u".

**Let us talk**

1. Say the number names from eleven to twenty.
2. How do you spell 40 in words?
3. What figure is "sixty-five"?
4. Say the difference between "thirteen" and "thirty".

**Worksheet**

1. The correct spelling of 13 in words is A) thirden B) thirteen C) three-teen D) threeteen
2. "Forty" is the word for A) 40 B) 14 C) 4 D) 400
3. "One hundred and three" is written A) 130 B) 300 C) 113 D) 103
4. The words "twenty-one" mean A) 2 B) 12 C) 20 D) 21
5. Fill in: 100 is written in words as ____.
6. Write in words: 58, 91 and 147.
7. Read these names and write the figures: forty-four, sixty, one hundred and ten.

**My own work**

Write the number names of your age, your class number and your house number, then check them with the chart.

---

### WEEK 4 — WRITING WORDS IN FIGURES

*Depth: NERDC Primary 2 · Mathematics · First Term W1 (pp 675–676).*

**You will learn to**

• turn a number name into figures quickly
• hear a number and write it
• not mix up -teen and -ty
• check what I wrote

**Things to know**

• "Writing words in figures" is the opposite of last week: **thirty-six → 36**.
• Say the word in parts: **sixty-three** = sixty (60) + three (3) → **63**.
• The "-teen / -ty" trap: **thirteen** is 13, **thirty** is 30; **fifteen** is 15, **fifty** is 50. Listen for the long "ee" sound in -teen.
• Zero keeps a place: "two hundred and five" → **205**, not 250 and not 25.
• Write in figures only — no words, no commas — and put one number per line so it can be checked.
• Dictation: I hear the number twice; the first time I listen, the second time I write.
• Checking: read my figure back aloud as a word; if it does not sound like what I heard, try again.
• Matching game in class: the word card and the figure card go together on the wall.

**Words for my notebook**

sixty-three → 63. Two hundred and five → 205. Read your answer back to check it.

**Let us talk**

1. Write "seventy-two" in figures.
2. What is the difference between 13 and 30 in words?
3. Write "one hundred and eight" in figures.
4. How do you check your answer after writing it?

**Worksheet**

1. "Eighty-four" in figures is A) 14 B) 84 C) 48 D) 80
2. "Two hundred and seven" is A) 270 B) 27 C) 702 D) 207
3. Which word matches 60? A) sixty-one B) sixty C) sixteen D) seventy
4. "One hundred and ten" is A) 1001 B) 101 C) 110 D) 11
5. Fill in: The figure for "forty-nine" is ____.
6. Write in figures: fifty-five, one hundred and twenty, two hundred.
7. Read back your five answers and tick each one that is right.

**My own work**

Write your telephone number and your house number in words, then turn them back into figures and compare.

Write one, two and three in words on three cards, cut the cards out and paste them in your book.

---

### WEEK 5 — ADDITION (INTRODUCTION)

*Depth: NERDC Primary 1 · Mathematics · First Term W7 (pp 618–619).*

**You will learn to**

• say that addition means putting together
• use the signs + and =
• add within 5 in two formats
• tell an addition story with objects

**Things to know**

• **Addition** means **putting together** or **adding more**; the answer is called the **sum** or **total**.
• The **plus sign (+)** means "and" or "more"; the **equals sign (=)** means "is the same as".
• With counters: 2 stones and 1 stone → count them all → **2 + 1 = 3**.
• Horizontal form: **3 + 2 = 5**. Vertical form: write 3, put +2 under it, draw a line, write 5.
• Number bonds of 5: 0 and 5, 1 and 4, 2 and 3 — pairs that make five.
• Adding **zero** changes nothing: 4 + 0 = 4.
• Counting on is faster than counting all: for 4 + 2, start at 4 and count on two — 5, 6.
• A story problem: *"Ada has 2 oranges, and her friend gives her 1 more. How many has she now?"* → 2 + 1 = **3**.
• Our fingers are the first calculator; the number line is the second.

**Words for my notebook**

Addition is putting together. The sign is +, and the answer after = is the total. 3 + 2 = 5.

**Let us talk**

1. What does the + sign mean?
2. Show me 2 + 3 with your fingers.
3. What is the sum of 4 and 1?
4. Tell a story that ends in 5 + 2.

**Worksheet**

1. Addition means A) comparing B) sharing C) putting together D) taking away
2. The sign used in addition is A) + B) × C) – D) =
3. 3 + 0 = A) 3 B) 0 C) 1 D) 30
4. In 2 + 3 = 5, the 5 is called the A) difference B) tens C) place D) sum
5. Fill in: 1 and ____ make five.
6. Write 4 + 3 in the vertical format and solve it.
7. Make up a story that can be solved by 2 + 2.

**My own work**

Write two addition sums within 5 from things in your house (cups, spoons, plates) and solve them.

---

### WEEK 6 — ADDITION

*Depth: NERDC Primary 1 · Mathematics · First Term W8 (pp 618–619); Primary 2 · First Term W7 and W8.*

**You will learn to**

• add within 10 with objects and pictures
• add two single digits in my head
• add 2-digit numbers without carrying
• use a number line to add
• check an addition with subtraction

**Things to know**

• **Addition facts to 10** are the things I must know without counting: 6 + 4 = 10, 7 + 3 = 10, 8 + 2 = 10, 9 + 1 = 10.
• Adding **zero**: 9 + 0 = 9; adding one: 9 + 1 = 10.
• On a **number line**: to do 6 + 3, put the finger on 6 and jump three steps right → 9.
• Two-digit without carrying: 23 + 14 → add the ones (3 + 4 = 7), add the tens (20 + 10 = 30) → **37**.
• With carrying (a challenge for later in the week): 28 + 7 → 8 + 7 = 15, so 20 + 15 = **35**.
• Adding **three or more** numbers: 2 + 3 + 4 — take two first (2 + 3 = 5), then 5 + 4 = 9.
• **Mental addition**: "What is 5 and 5? 10. Then 10 and 2? 12." No fingers needed.
• Real life: how many in all — 6 pencils and 5 crayons = **11 items**; ₦5 and ₦10 = **₦15**.
• Check with subtraction: 23 + 14 = 37, and 37 − 14 = 23. If the check does not come back, do the addition again.
• The order does not matter: 4 + 6 is the same as 6 + 4.

**Words for my notebook**

Know the facts to 10. For 23 + 14: ones first (3+4=7), then tens (20+10=30) → 37. Check with subtraction.

**Let us talk**

1. What is 7 + 3? 8 + 2? 6 + 4?
2. Show me 5 + 4 on the number line.
3. How do you add 20 + 30 quickly?
4. How do you check that 25 + 12 = 37?
5. Give me two pairs that make 10.

**Worksheet**

1. 6 + 4 = A) 12 B) 11 C) 10 D) 9
2. 32 + 15 = A) 47 B) 17 C) 46 D) 57
3. On a number line, adding means jumping to the A) up B) left C) right D) down
4. 5 + 0 = A) 10 B) 0 C) 50 D) 5
5. Fill in: 28 + 7 = ____ (carry the ten).
6. Add 3 + 5 + 2 and show your working.
7. Check 24 + 13 by subtraction and write both lines.

**My own work**

Write five addition sums within 10 and five two-digit sums without carrying; solve them all in your book.

Draw four stones in one circle and three in another, join the circles with a plus sign, then colour both circles.

---

### WEEK 8 — SUBTRACTION (INTRODUCTION)

*Depth: NERDC Nursery 2 · Numeracy · Third Term W1 (pp 344–347); Primary 1 · Mathematics · First Term W9 (pp 618–619).*

**You will learn to**

• say that subtraction means taking away
• use the minus sign (−)
• subtract within 5 with objects and pictures
• tell what zero does in subtraction

**Things to know**

• **Subtraction** means **taking away**, **removing**, or finding the **difference** between two numbers.
• The **minus sign (−)** means "take away"; the answer is called the **difference** or the **remainder**.
• With counters: 5 stones, take away 2, count what is left → **5 − 2 = 3**.
• Words for the same thing: "5 **minus** 2", "5 **less** 2", "take 2 **away from** 5".
• Horizontal form: **5 − 1 = 4**. Vertical form: 5 on top, −1 below, line, then 4.
• With pictures: cross out the ones that go; count the ones not crossed.
• **Zero in subtraction**: 5 − 0 = 5 (nothing taken, nothing lost); 5 − 5 = 0 (all taken, none left).
• Counting back is the trick: for 6 − 2, start at 6 and count back two — 5, 4.
• A subtraction story: *"I have 5 sweets and I eat 2. How many are left?"* → 5 − 2 = **3**.
• Subtraction is the opposite of addition: if 2 + 3 = 5, then 5 − 3 = 2.

**Words for my notebook**

Subtraction is taking away. The sign is −, and the answer is the difference. 5 − 2 = 3.

**Let us talk**

1. What does the − sign mean?
2. Show me 4 − 1 with your fingers.
3. What is 3 − 3? What is 3 − 0?
4. Tell a story that ends in 5 − 2.

**Worksheet**

1. Subtraction means A) sharing fairly B) taking away C) putting together D) multiplying
2. 4 − 1 = A) 3 B) 0 C) 5 D) 4
3. The answer in subtraction is called the A) difference B) place C) sum D) total
4. 6 − 6 = A) 6 B) 1 C) 12 D) 0
5. Fill in: If 2 + 3 = 5, then 5 − 3 = ____.
6. Write 5 − 2 in the vertical form and solve it.
7. Make up a story that can be solved by 4 − 1.

**My own work**

Line up six caps, take two away, and write the whole thing as one number sentence.

Draw seven oranges, cross out two, and colour the five that are left.

---

### WEEK 9 — SUBTRACTION

*Depth: NERDC Primary 1 · Mathematics · First Term W10 (pp 618–619); Primary 2 · First Term W9 and W10 (pp 675–676).*

**You will learn to**

• subtract within 10 fluently
• subtract 2-digit numbers without borrowing
• borrow when the ones are not enough
• check a subtraction with addition
• use subtraction with money and with objects

**Things to know**

• **Subtraction facts within 10**: 10 − 4 = 6, 10 − 7 = 3, 9 − 5 = 4 — these must be quick.
• Subtracting zero changes nothing: 8 − 0 = 8.
• "Difference between" means: how far apart two numbers are — the difference between 7 and 3 is **4**.
• Without borrowing: 48 − 25 → ones (8 − 5 = 3), tens (40 − 20 = 20) → **23**.
• With borrowing: 42 − 15 → the 2 cannot take the 5, so borrow one ten: 12 − 5 = 7, and 30 − 10 = 20 → **27**.
• On the number line, subtracting means jumping to the **left**.
• Checking with addition: 48 − 25 = 23, and 23 + 25 = 48. If it does not add back, work again.
• Real life: ₦10 − ₦4 = **₦6** change; 20 pupils, 3 absent → **17 present**; a 60 km journey, 25 km covered → **35 km left**.
• Never swap the numbers without thinking: 3 − 8 is not 8 − 3; in subtraction, order matters.

**Words for my notebook**

For 48 − 25: ones first, then tens → 23. Check by adding back. If the ones are too small, borrow a ten.

**Let us talk**

1. What is 10 − 6? 9 − 4?
2. Find 57 − 23 in your book.
3. When do you need to borrow?
4. Your mother has ₦50 and buys ₦30 of bread — what is left?
5. How would you check 41 − 16?

**Worksheet**

1. 10 − 3 = A) 8 B) 7 C) 6 D) 13
2. 56 − 23 = A) 32 B) 79 C) 43 D) 33
3. In 42 − 15 we must A) add one B) borrow a ten C) multiply D) swap the numbers
4. Subtraction is checked with A) addition B) subtraction C) division D) counting
5. Fill in: The difference between 9 and 4 is ____.
6. Work out 63 − 28 and show the borrowing.
7. Write a money story that is solved by ₦100 − ₦45.

**My own work**

Write three subtraction stories from your own home (sweets, caps, money) and solve each one.

Draw ten shells, take away four, and colour the ones that stay.


---

# General Knowledge

Weeks 1 to 6, 8 and 9. No note for Week 7 (Mid-Term), 10 (Revision), 11 (Examination).
*Term theme: Myself, My Family & My School. This subject is school-generated — there is no NERDC
scheme for it — so each week's extent is the school's own `Breakdown (Subtopics)` list, reproduced
line for line as bullets below.*


### WEEK 1 — MYSELF: MY NAME, MY AGE AND MY BODY

*Depth: school breakdown for N2 F1 W1.*

**You will learn to**

• say my full name and my nickname clearly
• say my age and whether I am a boy or a girl
• name the parts of my body
• say which sense I use for each thing

**Things to know**

• My **full name** has two parts: my first name and my family name — "Adaeze Okafor".
• My **nickname** is the short name people at home call me — "Ada".
• I say it clearly, standing up, and I do not shout it.
• My **age**: "I am four years old." I know whether I am a **boy** or a **girl**.
• **"I am special, I am me"** — there is no other child with my face and my name.
• Parts of my body I can name: **head, eyes, ears, nose, mouth, hands, legs**.
• Senses: I **see** with my eyes, **hear** with my ears, **smell** with my nose, **taste** with my tongue, **feel** with my skin.
• Song we sing with the actions: *"Head, shoulders, knees and toes"* — twice, then fast.
• Rhyme of the week: **"I am special, I am me; I have two hands, one, two, three…"** (clap on each number).

**Words for my notebook**

My name is ______. I am ____ years old. I am a boy / a girl. I am special, I am me.

**Let us talk**

1. Stand up and say your full name.
2. What do people at home call you?
3. How old are you, and are you a boy or a girl?
4. Touch something you can smell, and tell us what you use.

**Worksheet**

1. The two parts of my full name are my first name and my A) age B) nickname C) class D) family name
2. We see with our A) eyes B) nose C) hands D) ears
3. "I am special, I am ___." A) we B) me C) they D) you
4. The name people at home call you is your A) full name B) nickname C) tribe D) school name
5. Fill in: I am ____ years old.
6. Name seven parts of your body.
7. Say which sense you use for: a bell, a flower, a mango.

**My own work**

Write your first name five times and draw your face under it.

---

### WEEK 2 — MY FAMILY: MEMBERS AND THEIR ROLES

*Depth: school breakdown for N2 F1 W2.*

**You will learn to**

• name the members of my family and of the extended family
• say what each member does at home
• show love and respect to my family
• use the polite words at home

**Things to know**

• The members of my family at home: **father, mother, brothers, sisters** — and me.
• The extended family is bigger: **grandpa, grandma, uncles, aunts, cousins**.
• What each one does: father works and brings things home; mother cooks and buys food; brothers and sisters play, learn and help.
• Love and respect at home: we greet in the morning, we do not shout at a small child, we help grandpa to sit.
• At home we say **"please"** when we ask, **"thank you"** when we receive, and **"sorry"** when we do wrong.
• We share what we have: the biscuit, the crayons, the chair by the fan.
• Family song: *"This is my family — this is my family, we love one another."*
• Drawing of the week: my family, with five names written under the five people.

**Words for my notebook**

My family: father, mother, brothers, sisters. Big family: grandpa, grandma, uncles, aunts, cousins.

**Let us talk**

1. Name the people who live with you.
2. Who is your father's mother to you?
3. Say one thing your mother or father does every day.
4. What do you say when your sister gives you a cup?

**Worksheet**

1. Your mother's mother is your A) aunt B) cousin C) sister D) grandma
2. When somebody gives you something you say A) please B) sorry C) goodbye D) thank you
3. A girl who is your father's daughter is your A) aunt B) cousin C) grandma D) sister
4. Who cooks food at home? A) anybody who can help B) only the mother C) only the children D) nobody
5. Fill in: My uncle's children are my ____.
6. Name four people in your family and one thing each does.
7. Say the three polite words we use at home.

**My own work**

Draw your family and write one word under each person: father, mother, brother, sister, me.

---

### WEEK 3 — MY SCHOOL: PLACES, PEOPLE AND THINGS

*Depth: school breakdown for N2 F1 W3.*

**You will learn to**

• say the name of my school and my class
• name places in the school and what each is for
• name the people who work in the school
• pack and care for my school bag

**Things to know**

• The name of my school: **______**. My class: **Nursery 2**. I say both out loud.
• Places in the school: **classroom** (we learn), **playground** (we play), **library** (we read), **toilet** (we freshen up), **office** (the head teacher works).
• People in the school: **the head teacher** (head of the school), **teachers** (they teach), **the caretaker** (he keeps the compound clean), **the school nurse** (she treats anyone who is hurt or sick).
• What is in my school bag: **exercise books, reader, pencils, crayons, sharpener, handkerchief, water bottle**.
• Taking care of school things: no torn pages, no writing on the desk, the bag shut, the books in the bag — not in a heap.
• School tour: we walked to the five places and said each name at the door.
• Matching game: the picture to the word — library, office, toilet.

**Words for my notebook**

My school is ______. My class is Nursery 2. Places: classroom, playground, library, toilet, office.

**Let us talk**

1. What is the name of this school?
2. Where do we read story books?
3. Who treats a pupil that has fallen?
4. Name four things inside your bag today.

**Worksheet**

1. We play during break in the A) library B) office C) toilet D) playground
2. The head of the school is the A) nurse B) head teacher C) pupil D) caretaker
3. Where do we freshen up? A) the toilet B) the store C) the library D) the office
4. Which of these should NOT be in the school bag? A) book B) cooking pot C) crayon D) pencil
5. Fill in: My class is Nursery ____.
6. Name four people who work in your school.
7. Write two sentences on how you take care of your books.

**My own work**

Colour the school bag and ring the five things that belong inside it.

---

### WEEK 4 — MY CLASSROOM: RULES AND MATERIALS

*Depth: school breakdown for N2 F1 W4.*

**You will learn to**

• name objects in the classroom
• state the rules we keep in class
• keep the classroom clean
• name the colours of things in the room

**Things to know**

• Objects in the classroom: **table, chair, board, books, pencils, crayons**, plus the duster, the clock and the shelf.
• Classroom rule one: **raise your hand** before you speak or before you leave your seat.
• Rule two: **listen** when the teacher or another pupil is talking.
• Rule three: **share** the crayons, the books and the toys.
• Rule four: **keep quiet** while somebody else is answering.
• Keeping the room clean: paper goes in the **dustbin**, desks are **tidied**, the board is wiped after use.
• Colours of the things we see: **red, blue, yellow, green, black, white**.
• Game of the week: **"Find something that is red… that is round… that is soft"** — three runs, one for each clue.

**Words for my notebook**

Our rules: raise the hand, listen, share, keep quiet. The dustbin is for paper. Colours: red, blue, yellow, green, black, white.

**Let us talk**

1. Name four things in this classroom.
2. What must you do before you speak?
3. Where does a piece of wrapper go?
4. Find something yellow and bring it near the board.

**Worksheet**

1. We write on the A) bag B) chair C) board D) dustbin
2. Before speaking in class we A) raise our hand B) stand on the chair C) clap D) shout
3. Paper and wrappers go into the A) cupboard B) shoe C) dustbin D) bag
4. Which of these is a colour of a crayon? A) fast B) soft C) loud D) yellow
5. Fill in: When a friend is answering, we keep ____.
6. Mention four rules of the classroom.
7. Name three objects in the room and the colour of each.

**My own work**

Colour the classroom picture: the book red, the bag blue, the board green, the chair brown.

---

### WEEK 5 — MY HOME AND MY NEIGHBOURHOOD

*Depth: school breakdown for N2 F1 W5.*

**You will learn to**

• name the rooms of my house and what each is for
• say who lives with me and where I sleep and eat
• describe my street, village or town
• use near and far, up and down

**Things to know**

• Rooms in my house: **sitting room** (visitors sit), **kitchen** (food is cooked), **bedroom** (we sleep), **bathroom** (we bathe).
• Some houses have a **dining room**, a **veranda**, a **store** and a **compound**.
• The people who live with me are my family; I sleep in the bedroom and I eat in the sitting room or the dining area.
• My street / village / town is called **______**; my house number or landmark is **______**.
• On my way to school I see: the shop, the bus stop, the church, the mosque, the gutter, the mango tree, my neighbour's gate.
• **Near** means not far — the gate is near. **Far** means a long walk — the market is far.
• **Up** is the hill or the shelf; **down** is the gutter or the floor.
• **"My home is my first school"** — at home I learned to walk, to speak, to greet and to hold a spoon.

**Words for my notebook**

Rooms: sitting room, kitchen, bedroom, bathroom. My street is ______. Home is my first school.

**Let us talk**

1. Where is food cooked in your house?
2. Which room do you sleep in?
3. Name two things you see on your way to school.
4. Point to something near you and something far from you.

**Worksheet**

1. We bathe in the A) kitchen B) bedroom C) store D) bathroom
2. The room where visitors sit is the A) fence B) shed C) toilet D) sitting room
3. A place a long walk away is said to be A) far B) small C) up D) near
4. We call our home our first A) church B) market C) school D) shop
5. Fill in: Food is cooked in the ____.
6. Name the four rooms of your house and what each is for.
7. Write two sentences on what you see on your way to school.

**My own work**

Draw your house with four rooms and write the name of each room inside it.

---

### WEEK 6 — COLOURS, SHAPES AND SIZES AROUND ME

*Depth: school breakdown for N2 F1 W6.*

**You will learn to**

• name the colours I can see
• find circles, squares, triangles and stars in the room
• compare two things by size
• name the colours of the Nigerian flag

**Things to know**

• **Primary colours** (the colours that other colours are mixed from): **red, blue, yellow**.
• Other colours I know: **black, white, green, brown, orange, purple**.
• Shapes: **circle** (plate, ball, clock, wheel), **square** (tile, handkerchief, box face), **triangle** (roof, sandwich cut in two, ruler end), **star** (on the flag, in the sky at night).
• Sizes: **big and small** (a bag and a pencil), **long and short** (a chalk and a stick), **tall and short** (two pupils, back to back).
• Words for comparison: "This is **bigger than** that", "Mine is **shorter than** yours".
• The **Nigerian flag is green and white**: two green strips and one white strip in the middle.
• Games: **shape hunt** (find five round things), **colour sorting** (beads into six cups), **size stacking** (biggest box at the bottom).
• Mixing two colours makes a new one: red and yellow make orange; blue and white make light blue.

**Words for my notebook**

Red, blue, yellow are primary colours. Circle, square, triangle, star. Our flag is green and white.

**Let us talk**

1. Say the three primary colours.
2. Bring something round to the front.
3. Which of you is taller, you or your partner?
4. What are the colours of the Nigerian flag?

**Worksheet**

1. Which of these is a primary colour? A) pink B) purple C) brown D) yellow
2. The shape of a ball is a A) circle B) square C) star D) triangle
3. A roof is the shape of a A) triangle B) square C) line D) circle
4. The Nigerian flag is A) red and blue B) green and white C) black and yellow D) purple and pink
5. Fill in: Red and yellow mixed together make ____.
6. Name four shapes and one thing of each shape in this room.
7. Write two sentences comparing two things by size.

**My own work**

Colour three circles red, two squares blue and one triangle yellow.

---

### WEEK 8 — ANIMALS AND BIRDS AROUND ME

*Depth: school breakdown for N2 F1 W8.*

**You will learn to**

• name domestic animals and the sound each one makes
• name wild animals I have seen in pictures
• name birds and say where animals live
• tell a domestic animal from a wild animal

**Things to know**

• **Domestic animals** live with people and are looked after: **dog, cat, goat, chicken, cow**, and in some homes, sheep and rabbit.
• Their sounds: dog — **woof**; cat — **miaow**; goat — **meh**; hen — **koko**; cow — **moo**.
• **Wild animals** live in the bush and are not kept in the house: **lion, elephant, monkey, snake**, leopard, buffalo.
• **Birds** have feathers, two legs and a beak, and most of them fly: **parrot, pigeon, hen, duck, eagle**.
• The **eagle** appears on our national coat of arms — it is the first look at "our national bird".
• Where animals live: **home** (dog), **farm** (cow, goat, hen), **bush** (lion, monkey), **water** (fish, duck, frog), **sky** (bird, kite).
• What animals give us: the cow gives milk, the hen gives eggs, the dog guards the house.
• Song with actions: *"Old MacDonald had a farm, E-I-E-I-O"* — each child makes the sound of one animal.
• We do not go near a strange dog, and we never touch a snake.

**Words for my notebook**

Domestic: dog, cat, goat, chicken, cow. Wild: lion, elephant, monkey, snake. Birds have feathers and beaks.

**Let us talk**

1. Which animal says "meh"?
2. Name two wild animals.
3. What does a hen give us?
4. Say two things that show an animal is a bird.
5. Where does a fish live?

**Worksheet**

1. Which animal gives us milk? A) dog B) hen C) goat D) cow
2. Which one is a wild animal? A) goat B) chicken C) cat D) lion
3. A bird has A) fur B) feathers C) four legs D) scales only
4. The sound of the dog is A) miaow B) woof C) koko D) moo
5. Fill in: The ____ is on our national coat of arms.
6. Name four animals that live on a farm.
7. Write two sentences on one animal you like and what it gives us.

**My own work**

Draw four animals, two that live with people and two that live in the bush.

---

### WEEK 9 — GOOD HABITS: GREETINGS, POLITENESS AND CLEANLINESS

*Depth: school breakdown for N2 F1 W9.*

**You will learn to**

• greet correctly at morning, afternoon and evening
• greet an elder before myself
• use the four magic words
• keep my body and my uniform clean

**Things to know**

• Morning (before noon): **"Good morning."** Afternoon: **"Good afternoon."** Evening: **"Good evening."** Bedtime: **"Good night."**
• **We greet elders first** — the grown person is greeted before a child of my age; it is a duty, not a favour.
• In Yoruba: **Ẹ̀ káàárọ̀** (morning), **Ẹ̀ káàṣánṣá** (afternoon), **Ẹ̀ káàlẹ̀** (evening). In Hausa: **Ina wuni?**, **Ina rana?**, **Ina fada?** — the reply to all is **"Lafiya."**
• The four magic words: **"please"** (asking), **"thank you"** (receiving), **"sorry"** (when I do wrong), **"excuse me"** (passing, or after sneezing).
• Hands are washed **before eating and after using the toilet** — with soap, rubbing the fingers and the nails.
• Teeth are brushed **morning and night**; the body is bathed daily; the nails are cut short.
• The uniform is kept neat: shirt in, buttons done, shoes brushed, hair combed.
• Role-play of the week: a visitor comes to the class — we stand, greet, offer a seat, and speak when spoken to.
• The Good-Habit chart on our wall: each day a tick for the habit we kept; Friday is the day we count the ticks.

**Words for my notebook**

Good morning, good afternoon, good evening, good night. Please, thank you, sorry, excuse me. We greet elders first.

**Let us talk**

1. What do you say at seven in the morning?
2. Whom do you greet first — your headmaster or your friend?
3. When do we say "excuse me"?
4. Show me how you wash your hands.
5. How many times a day do we brush our teeth?

**Worksheet**

1. At half past six in the evening we say A) hello sir B) good night C) good evening D) good morning
2. The word we use when we ask for something is A) please B) welcome C) sorry D) goodbye
3. We wash our hands A) only on Friday B) once a week C) before eating and after the toilet D) never
4. Who is greeted first? A) a dog B) a baby C) an elder D) a friend
5. Fill in: We brush our teeth ____ and night.
6. Mention the four magic words and when each is used.
7. Write two sentences on the good habit you will keep next term.

**My own work**

Write the four magic words and use each one in a sentence of your own.

   B) Spinning jenny
   C) Steam engine
   D) Difference Engine
28. Babbage's plans could not be built fully because the ___
   A) the money he had for the metal parts
   B) the numbers he wrote in his log book
   C) workshop of his day could not make such parts
   D) the plans he drew on his paper only
29. A set of written instructions for a machine is called a ___
   A) programme
   B) pillow
   C) parcel
   D) poster
30. A computer language was named after ___
   A) the monitor
   B) Ada Lovelace
   C) Babbage's town
   D) the abacus

## Section B — Sub-objective (10 marks)
Answer briefly.

31. Mention five digital devices you know.
32. State three things a smartphone does.
33. Name the four basic parts of a computer.
34. Say what each of these is used for: monitor and CPU.
35. Mention three places where a computer is used.
36. Give four rules for caring for a device.
37. State three safety rules of the computer lab.
38. Name the three kinds of computer we learnt.
39. Mention three uses of the abacus or old counting things.
40. Say two things about Babbage and Ada Lovelace.

## Section C — Theory (10 marks)
Answer in full sentences.

41. Explain what technology means and give four devices at home that are digital (2 marks)
42. Describe how you care for a tablet or a laptop, in four points (2 marks)
43. Write on the rules of the computer lab (2 marks)
44. Tell what happens from the keyboard to the monitor when you type (2 marks)
45. Write on what you learnt about the change of computers from the abacus to today (2 marks)

# EXAMINATION PRACTICE — NIGERIAN HISTORY ( · )

**Time:** 1 hour · **Total: 50 marks**

## Section A — Objective (30 marks)
Choose the correct option and ring the letter.

1. History is the story of ___
   A) what will happen tomorrow
   B) what we eat today
   C) how to count money
   D) what happened in the past
2. Which of these is a thing of the past?
   A) what you will do at noon
   B) what you are doing now
   C) what you did yesterday
   D) the lunch in the pot
3. The best people to tell you about the past are ___
   A) the toys under my bed at home
   B) grandparents and old people of the town
   C) the new clock on the staff room wall
   D) the puppies playing in our street
4. A photograph of your baby days helps you know about ___
   A) tomorrow's weather
   B) the price of bread
   C) next session
   D) your past
5. At the top of a family tree we write ___
   A) the grandparents
   B) the cousins of a friend
   C) the class teacher
   D) the babies
6. Knowing your family name tells people your ___
   A) bus number
   B) weight
   C) identity
   D) shoe size
7. The story of what happened before can help us to ___
   A) count to one hundred
   B) make good decisions
   C) run faster
   D) sleep late always
8. A community is made up of ___
   A) the people and places around us
   B) only the children of our class
   C) nothing except the market stalls
   D) only the big houses on our road
9. The person who sells us tomatoes and fish is the ___
   A) the pupil in Nursery 1
   B) the nurse at the clinic
   C) the pilot in the aeroplane
   D) trader
10. The man who keeps our street safe is the ___
   A) the baker with hot bread
   B) the tailor with a sewing machine
   C) policeman
   D) the driver of the bus
11. A sick person in the community is taken to the ___
   A) the barber at the shop
   B) nurse or hospital
   C) the driver of the keke
   D) the musician with a drum
12. The three major ethnic groups of Nigeria are ___
   A) Red, Blue and Green
   B) Tuesday, Friday and Sunday
   C) Lagos, Kano and Ibadan
   D) Hausa, Igbo and Yoruba
13. The greeting 'Sannu' belongs to the ___
   A) Hausa people
   B) Igbo people
   C) Yoruba people
   D) Tiv people
14. Amala and ewédú are food of the ___
   A) Kanuri only
   B) Ijaw only
   C) Yoruba
   D) Hausa only
15. 'Ndewo' is the greeting of the ___
   A) Fulani people
   B) Hausa people
   C) Nupe people
   D) Igbo people
16. Besides the big three, another people in Nigeria are the ___
   A) Tiv
   B) Toyota
   C) Ankara
   D) Naira
17. We buy food and things for the house at the ___
   A) mosque
   B) market
   C) classroom
   D) playground
18. A house that has caught fire needs the ___
   A) the tailor near the school gate
   B) the library of the school
   C) fire station
   D) the barber shop at the junction
19. A thief in the street should be reported to the ___
   A) the school gate keeper
   B) the church choir on Sunday
   C) the market woman with her basket
   D) police station
20. People gather to pray in the ___
   A) the market stall of the trader
   B) church or the mosque
   C) the playground in the evening
   D) the bus park in the morning
21. In the playground, when only one ball is available, we should ___
   A) take turns
   B) fight for it
   C) hide it
   D) take it home
22. The home of a traditional ruler is called the ___
   A) clinic
   B) garage
   C) palace
   D) bakery
23. The meeting of the whole town is held in the ___
   A) the kitchen of my house
   B) community hall
   C) the animal shed behind the fence
   D) the water side at the stream
24. In the north of Nigeria the traditional ruler is the ___
   A) Oba
   B) Igwe
   C) Deji
   D) Emir
25. An old monument in our town tells us about ___
   A) the next election
   B) the new cinema
   C) the history of the place
   D) the price of new school shoes
26. Tolerance means we ___
   A) respect people who are different from us
   B) laugh at the names of other people
   C) refuse to sit with anybody at lunch
   D) fight over food in the market square
27. What we want for ourselves, we should also ___
   A) hide from our friends
   B) take by force
   C) laugh at
   D) want for other people
28. A bundle of sticks is hard to break because we are ___
   A) weak alone
   B) too many children
   C) stronger together
   D) not allowed to try
29. How many groups of people are there in Nigeria?
   A) more than two hundred and fifty
   B) only three groups in the whole country
   C) about ten groups
   D) exactly one group
30. The green colour on the Nigerian flag stands for ___
   A) the river Niger in our country
   B) the land of Nigeria
   C) the sky above us at noon
   D) our school uniform on Monday

Answer briefly.

31. Mention three things or people that tell us about the past.
32. Write the order of a family tree from the top.
33. Name four people who help us in the community.
34. Mention the three major ethnic groups and one thing each is known for.
35. Match the place to its use: market, hospital, school, police station.
36. Name three traditional places of a town.
37. State four rules of living together in peace.
38. Mention three festivals in Nigeria and where they are held.
39. Describe the Nigerian flag and what its colours mean.
40. Say two ways Nigerians are different and two things that make us one.

Answer in full sentences.

42. Mention four people who help us in our community and what each does. (2 marks)

44. What happens in the palace, and what happens in the community hall? (2 marks)
45. 'Different people, one Nigeria.' Explain this in two sentences. (2 marks)
